# Chapter 1: The Dip

*The Bonded · 2391*

The line was singing in E-flat when Lusine Arda called the ice.

Maren Tsolo heard it before the board showed it: a thickening in the hum that came up through the gallery floor and into the soles of her boots, the note the tether made when something on it had gone heavy. Forty kilometres of carbon braid ran down from the drum beneath her feet, out through the keel slot of the _Patience_ and down, always down, the tether deck had no other direction, into the ochre murk of Gall, where the Bucket rode the cloud-tops at seven kilometres a second and swallowed weather.

"Runner Two," Lusine said on the line channel. "I've got rime at eleven-four. Thick. It's growing while I watch it."

"How thick?"

"Thumb and a half. Feathering on the windward side."

Maren put two fingers on the tension trace. It was climbing, slowly, the way a fever climbs. Rime on the line meant weight, and weight in the wrong place meant the line would start to oscillate, and oscillation on a forty-kilometre tether was the kind of thing that turned up in the Combine's quarterly loss summaries as a single line of figures. Equipment, one Bucket. Personnel, as listed.

"Clear it," she said. "Go slow. Runner One, hold at six and watch your sister."

"Holding," said Odile, who was nineteen and had never in her life held at anything. Her crawler sat on the line six kilometres down, a red dot on the board, and Maren watched the dot and did not trust it.

The tether gallery was a long room with a long window, and the window looked at nothing useful. You saw the drum below you, the size of a cathedral, turning; you saw the slot it fed into; and past the slot, if you leaned, a curve of Gall so close and so large that it was not a planet at all but a weather, a floor of ochre and old-bruise purple that went on until it bent away into black. Nobody leaned. The board was where the line was. The board was where you saw it breathe.

Maren had been tether-chief for nine years and on the line for twenty-six before that, and she had stopped thinking of the board as a picture of anything. It was a body. The long green trace of the tension was its pulse. The fat amber band of the Bucket's intake was its belly filling. The little coloured dots that crawled along its spine were her runners, and the numbers beside them were their air.

"Starting on it," Lusine said. Her voice had the slight breathless flatness of someone working hard in a suit. "Pick's biting. It's coming off in plates."

"Don't let them hit the crawler."

"I'm not new, Chief."

"You're not old either."

A laugh on the channel. It was a good channel, the line channel. Everyone on shift could hear everyone else, and in a dip, which was six hours of nothing followed by four seconds of everything, the voices were company. Down on the drum floor, Maren could see her riggers at their stations under the floodlights, little figures in grey coveralls, each one bent over a brake housing or a sensor head. Her brother was one of them. She could pick him out without trying, the way you pick out your own handwriting: Abel at the number three brake, leaning on the housing with the elbow that wasn't meant for leaning, his helmet pushed back off his forehead against regulations, talking to somebody. Abel was always talking to somebody.

"Abel," she said on the floor channel. "Helmet."

He looked up at the gallery window, though he couldn't have seen her through the glare, and pulled the helmet down with an elaborate, injured slowness that got a laugh from the rigger beside him.

The tension trace climbed another hair.

"Eleven-four's clear," said Lusine. "Moving down to eleven-six. There's more. It's in bands, Chief, like the line's been dipped in it."

"Weather," said Maren. She looked at the met strip along the top of the board. There was a storm coming up out of the south under the Bucket's track, a bruise the riggers had been calling Old Purple since the morning, and it had been throwing moisture into the high cloud for an hour. She had been watching it the way you watch a man at the next table who might be drunk. "It's the Purple. We'll be out of its shadow in forty minutes."

"Forty minutes is a lot of ice."

"Then work faster."

That was what a tether-chief said. You said it with a little edge on it so they'd know you meant it and a little warmth under it so they'd know you were there. Maren had learned the voice from her own chief, Hesketh, who had gone into Gall the year Abel was born; and Hesketh had learned it from the woman before her, and so on back up the line to the first tether-chiefs of the first skimmers, who had probably said it to people who were also going to die.

The _Patience_ was sixty-one years old. She had been built for the Combine in a yard four lanes away and towed to Corva in pieces, and she had been dipping Gall ever since: two dips a day, ten months of the year, with the drum shut down only in the dead season, when Gall's southern storms made the cloud-tops too violent even for the Bucket. She carried three hundred bonded crew. On the ice of Kettle, Gall's white moon, the Combine kept two thousand more families in the warrens of Kettle Camp, refining what the _Patience_ brought up and breathing what the refinery left over. Every one of them was in debt to the Combine for the air in their lungs. Every one of them had been born in debt, or would die in it, or both. Maren had been born in the Camp. Her mother had been a runner. Her mother had gone into Gall from fourteen kilometres down when Maren was fifteen and Abel was three, and the Combine had added the cost of the lost crawler to the family account, and Maren had paid it off at nineteen, and then started on her own.

You did not think about that in a dip. You thought about the trace.

"Eleven-six clear," Lusine said. "Eleven-eight is—oh, that's ugly. That's a fist of it, Chief."

"Can you take it?"

"I can take it."

The trace jumped.

It was not much of a jump. A bump. The kind of thing the line did ten times a dip when the Bucket hit a pocket of thicker air and bit. But the note under Maren's boots changed with it, dropped half a tone and wavered, and she had her hand on the damper before she knew why.

"Shear," said the met tech from the end of the gallery, too late. "Shear under the Bucket, the Purple's throwing—"

"I see it." Maren rolled the damper on. Down in the slot the brakes bit and the drum shuddered and every rigger on the floor grabbed something. The trace kinked, jumped again, and began, very slowly, to make a wave. "Runners, lock down. Lock down now. Line's going to walk."

"Locked," said Odile at once.

"Locking," said Lusine. "I'm—Chief, the crawler's bucking, I can't get the—"

"Get your clamps on and get flat."

"Clamps on. Clamps are on."

The wave went down the line. You could not see it from the gallery. You could only watch it on the trace, a long green swell running out from the drum, and know that forty kilometres away the Bucket was yawing in the cloud like a fish on a hook, and that at every point between, the braid was flexing in a long, slow, enormous S that would come back up again, and then go down again, until the dampers ate it or it ate the dampers. Maren had seen a line walk once that killed seven people and the Bucket. She had been a runner then. She had been on the line. She had held on.

"Second pulse coming up," she said, as calm as the floor under a bed. "Lusine. Odile. It'll be worse at you than at me. Hold on and count. It goes by."

"Counting," said Odile, and began to count aloud, which was what Maren had taught her.

Lusine said nothing. On the board her dot was steady, her air steady, her clamps green.

The swell came back up the line and passed eleven-eight, and Maren felt it pass in the drum, a heave like a ship going over a wave. The trace spiked. Then there was a noise on the line channel that was not a word, a small hard sound, like a cough or a stone dropped on a deck.

"Lusine."

"Chief." Lusine's voice was quite clear. "Chief, I'm off."

Maren looked at the board. Lusine's dot was where it had been. The crawler's dot. The crawler was still on the line, clamped and green. Beside it, separating so slowly that for a moment it seemed to be a fault in the display, was a second, smaller mark, the personal beacon in Lusine's suit, and it was moving away.

"Clip," said Lusine. "My clip went. Harness clip. I'm off the crawler. I'm—I've got nothing to grab, Chief, the line's gone past me."

"Stay on this channel."

"I'm on it."

The thing about a tether is that it hangs. It is not in orbit, except at one point; every part of it below the _Patience_ moves more slowly than an orbit at that height would need, and is held up only by the line itself. A runner who comes off the line comes off moving too slowly to stay up. She does not fall the way a stone falls on a world. She falls the way a dream falls: gently, a few metres a second at first, and then more, and then more, away and down along a long curve that ends in Gall.

Maren knew all of this the way she knew her own teeth.

"Gull," she said on the command channel. "Status on the Gull."

"In the cradle, Chief." The dock officer sounded sick. "Port thruster's still stripped, we've got it open, it's—forty minutes to button it, an hour—"

"Then button it."

"Chief, by the time—"

"Button it."

"Chief," said Lusine. "Don't."

Maren closed her eyes, once, briefly, and opened them again on the trace. It was still walking. The second pulse was going back down toward the Bucket and there would be a third, smaller, and a fourth, and Odile was at six kilometres counting aloud in a voice going higher with every number.

"Odile," Maren said. "Keep counting."

"That's my sister. That's my—"

"I know. Keep counting. You stay clamped. If you come off that crawler I will put you on report and then I will kill you myself. Count."

"Twenty-two," said Odile, and choked, and said, "twenty-three."

On the floor below the gallery the riggers had stopped working. They were standing at their stations with their faces turned up toward the window as if Maren could do something, as if she were a god in a glass box with her hands on the weather; and she could do nothing, so she did the only thing there was to do, which was run the line. She fed the damper in, and fed it out again, and listened to the drum, and kept the line from killing anyone else.

"It's quite nice actually," said Lusine, a little while later. "Out here. You can see the whole of the Purple. It's got a hole in the middle like an eye. Did you know that? You never see it from the line."

"I didn't know that."

"It's looking at me."

"It's not looking at you. It's weather."

"Everything's weather to you, Chief." A pause. A breath. "My air's good. Six hours on the bottle."

"Good."

"Not that I'll need six hours."

Maren didn't answer that. The dock officer came back on the command channel to say that the port thruster was out of its housing and the replacement was being walked down from stores, and Maren said, "Good," to him as well, and the word went dead in her mouth.

Lusine Arda was twenty-four years old. She had come up from the Camp at sixteen with her sister on her hip, practically, and been a rigger for three years and a runner for five. She did the comms relay on the off-shifts for the extra air-credit. She had a laugh like a dropped tray. She had been Maren's best runner for two years, and Maren had been meaning to tell her so and had not, because you didn't, because a runner who thought she was the best was a runner who went down the line too fast.

"Chief," Lusine said, after a time. Her voice had gone quieter. The channel was beginning to fuzz at the edges, the way it did when somebody was getting far from the antenna. "Chief, I want to tell you something. Not on this channel."

"Switch to four."

A click. Maren switched. Channel four was the chief's private, unlogged, a habit from the old days when tether-chiefs had needed somewhere to swear.

"Go."

"I read something," said Lusine. "On the relay. I wasn't meant to. There's a dispatch coming in on the _Clement_, it came ahead of her on the beacon, and it's—Chief, it's about all of us. I told someone. I shouldn't have told. And now my clip—"

The fuzz rose up and swallowed a sentence.

"Say again. Lusine, say again."

"—don't think it was the clip, I think it was—"

"Lusine."

"It's getting warm, Chief." Very faint now. "That's funny. I thought it'd be cold."

The beacon mark on the board touched the upper edge of the cloud.

"I can see the Lamp," said Lusine Arda, from very far away. "Did you know? It's coming round. It's just above the Purple. Teska says it takes the dead—"

And the channel was empty, and stayed empty, and Maren sat with her hand on the damper and her eyes on the trace for another four hours and fifty minutes, until the Bucket was full and the dip was done.

* * *

They reeled Lusine's crawler up with the line.

It came in through the slot at the end of the dip, riding the braid into the drum like a beetle on a string, and the riggers stood back from it as if it were hot. It was not hot. It was rimed white from its long ride in the shadow, and its clamps were still locked, and its little green lights were still green, because nothing had ever been wrong with it. The harness strap that had held Lusine to it was still clipped to its saddle ring. The other end of the strap, where her harness should have been, hung loose.

Maren went down the gallery ladder and across the floor and stood in front of it.

Nobody said anything. The floor was full of people. The off-shift had come in, and the galley hands, and riggers from the Bucket crews, and half of the dock section, so that the drum hall, which was the only room on the _Patience_ big enough to hold the whole crew, was holding most of it. The drum itself had stopped turning. Without its noise the hall sounded wrong, too large, full of small sounds: boots, breathing, the tick of the frost coming off the crawler as the warm air reached it.

"Get me a light," Maren said.

Danek Sorell gave her his hand-lamp. Danek was the biggest rigger on the line, a man with hands like shovels who sang in the shower block and was not singing now. Maren crouched and put the light on the saddle ring.

The clip was a dog clip, the kind every runner wore: a hooked steel jaw with a spring-loaded gate, so that it could be snapped on to a ring one-handed in gloves. The gate was missing. The jaw was there, and the hinge pin, and the stub of the spring, but the gate itself had gone, and the spring stub ended in a face that was not torn. Steel that fails under shock tears. It goes ragged and grey and granular, like the inside of a broken biscuit. This was bright for two-thirds of its width, a clean flat shine like the face of a coin, and only the last third was grey.

Maren looked at it for a long moment. Then she unhooked the clip from the ring, closed her hand over it, and stood up.

"Gate failure," she said. "Spring went."

"It was the shear," somebody said behind her. "That shear was a bastard."

"It was the shear."

Odile Arda had come in off the line twenty minutes after the end of the dip, stripped her suit in the lock without waiting for the dresser and walked into the drum hall in her undersuit, sweat-black down the spine, and now she was standing at the edge of the crowd with her arms wrapped around herself, staring at the crawler as if it might at any moment open and let her sister out. Grandam Teska was beside her. Teska was eighty-three and had been on the _Patience_ since the year it was towed in; she had one good eye and one milk-white one and she held Odile's elbow in a claw of a hand that was steadier than it looked.

"She's gone into Gall," said Teska, to no one in particular. "Gall has her."

"Gall has her," the hall said back, the low ragged chorus, like a congregation that has forgotten what church it's in.

That was all the funeral a dipper got when the line took them. When you died on the ship there was a proper giving: the body was wrapped and walked down the line in a crawler and released at forty kilometres into the cloud, so that the Deep could have it. When the line took you, Gall had already done the taking. There was nothing to walk down. There was only the crawler coming home empty, and somebody chalking the name on the drum.

Teska did the chalking. She had to be helped up the drum ladder to do it. The drum's outer skin was covered in names, layer on layer of them, the old ones rubbed back to ghosts by sixty years of hands and shoulders and the new ones bright: dates, names, sometimes a joke. GALL TOOK MY HUSBAND AND HE WASN'T EVEN GOOD. Teska wrote LUSINE ARDA 2391 in her slow shaking capitals and underneath it, after a moment's thought, a small round shape with a line trailing off it, like a seed with a tail.

"What's that?" said one of the young riggers.

"That's the Lamp," said Teska. "It's coming round. You'll see it in a week, if you go up the blister. It came round when my grandmother's grandmother was a girl and it'll come round when your grandchildren are dead. It goes out past the cold and it comes back. It takes the dead out with it and it brings them back."

"That's a story," said the rigger.

"Everything's a story," said Teska, climbing down. "You're a story. You're a short one."

Somebody laughed, the way you laugh at funerals. The hall began to break up into its small groups and its quiet talk. Maren stood by the crawler with the clip in her fist and watched the room.

She watched the riggers, who were looking at the floor. She watched the dock crew, who were looking at each other. She watched Odile, who was looking at nothing. And she watched her brother Abel, who had come across from the number three brake and was standing a little apart with a bulb of tea he was not drinking, his face greyish under the floodlights, and who, when he felt her looking, lifted the tea in a small salute and gave her the grin he had been giving her since he was four, the one that said _don't worry, it's me_; and then looked away, a fraction too soon.

"Chief Tsolo."

The voice came from behind her, and the hall, in its small way, went quiet again.

Corwin Hale was the chief warden of the _Patience_. He was a bonded man like everyone else, born in the Camp, carrying a family account like everyone else; but he was a trusty, which meant that he had been given a shock-stick, a cabin to himself, a double air ration and the job of keeping the other bonded in order on the Combine's behalf, and in return the Combine took a little off his account every year and nobody liked him very much. He did not seem to need to be liked. He was a big, slow, patient man with a soft voice and a shaven head, and he carried a slate under his arm the way a priest carries a book.

"Warden."

"I'm sorry for your loss." He meant the crew. He said it to her because she was the chief. "Arda was a good runner."

"She was."

"I'll need the incident report from you. And the dip log. I've filled in what I can." He held out the slate. "Sign where it's marked."

Maren took it. The form was already completed in Hale's neat hand. _Personnel loss, tether operations. Runner L. Arda. Cause: harness detachment during line oscillation; runner did not secure secondary lanyard per Standing Order 14._ Below that, the line items. _Personal beacon, lost. Suit, runner's, lost. Air, six hours, lost. Crawler, recovered, inspection and recertification._ Each with its figure. The total was transferred to Account ARDA, L., and since Account ARDA, L. had no further earning capacity, it had been carried forward, as the Combine's standing terms provided, to the next of kin. ARDA, O.

Maren read it twice.

"She didn't have a secondary," she said. "Nobody has a secondary. We haven't been issued secondary lanyards since before you came aboard. You know that."

"Standing Order 14 says a runner will secure a secondary lanyard."

"Standing Order 14 says a runner will be issued one."

"Then the shortfall is the Combine's," said Hale reasonably, "and the factor can strike the line. He'll be aboard in an hour. You can raise it with him. In the meantime the form has to be filed with the dip, and the dip has to be filed before the _Clement_ docks, or the load goes unregistered and the whole crew gets docked a day." He paused. "That would be a pity. Today."

Maren looked at him. He looked back with his patient, mild, slightly sorrowful face, as if he too were a victim of the form.

She signed. Of course she signed. You signed, or the crew got docked a day, and a day's air-credit to a rigger with two children in the Camp was a week of thin meals. Hale took the slate back and tucked it under his arm.

"And the clip," he said. "For the inspection."

"What clip?"

"The harness clip. Arda's. It'll need to go to the factor's engineer with the crawler. For the report."

"Spring failed," Maren said. "Gate's gone. It's in Gall with her."

Hale's eyes rested on her closed fist for a moment, and on her face, and then he nodded. "I'll note that," he said, and went away across the hall with his slate, and Bram Kovač, the youngest and hardest of his trusties, peeled himself off the wall by the door and went after him.

Maren put her fist in her pocket and left it there.

* * *

The _Clement_ came in at the end of second shift, out of the black on the Kettle side, a long dull hull with the Halloran-Vey wheel painted on her flank in green, and mated to the _Patience_'s forward collar with a bang that went through every deck. She was the supply ship. Four times a year she came up from the Throat, the lane mouth that hung in the dark behind Gall, with spare parts and medicines and new bonded and the Combine's paper, and went back with deuterium. She was eleven days early. Everyone knew she was eleven days early. Nobody knew why.

The factor came aboard at the change of shift, when he could address both watches at once, and he addressed them in the drum hall, under Teska's chalk.

He was not what Maren had expected. The last factor had been a fat cheerful thief called Moberly who had sold the infirmary's painkillers to the Camp and been recalled for it. This one was tall and spare and grey, fifty or a little past it, in a plain dark coat with the green wheel on the lapel and nothing else to say he was anyone. He had a long face, lined in the wrong places for a man who smiled. He stood on the drum gallery with Hale on one side of him and a marine sergeant on the other, a stocky woman in Combine grey with a sidearm, and he looked down at three hundred tired bonded and waited for them to stop talking. It took a while. He waited without impatience.

"My name is Aurelian Quist," he said, when they had. "I'm the Combine's factor for Corva, from today. I've been told there was a death on the line this morning. I'm sorry for it."

He said it as if he were. That was the first thing Maren noticed about him: that he said things as if he meant them, and it made the hall uneasy, because no factor in living memory had meant anything.

"I won't keep you," Quist said. "You've had a long day and a bad one. I'll say only this. The Combine has been reviewing the Corva operation, as it reviews all its operations from time to time. That review is now complete. There will be changes. In the next few days I'll be speaking to the chiefs and the wardens about what those changes mean for each of you, and I'll speak to all of you again before the end of the month. Until then, the dips go on as before. Thank you."

That was all. He turned and went down the gallery steps with the sergeant behind him, and Hale stood a moment longer looking out at the hall, and then followed.

The hall was very quiet.

"Changes," said Danek Sorell, at last, beside Maren. "What's that mean, changes?"

"It means changes," Maren said.

"Good changes or bad changes?"

She looked at him. Danek had been on the line eleven years. He had a wife in the Camp and three children and a laugh you could hear from the slot, and he was asking her what kind of changes the Combine made, as if there had ever been more than one kind.

"Go and get some sleep, Danek," she said.

* * *

The infirmary was the one place on the _Patience_ that was ever warm. Ekene kept it that way on purpose, cooking the heat exchangers past their rating and fiddling the logs, because, he said, sick people healed better warm and well people talked more truthfully; and it was true that you heard things in the infirmary at night that you did not hear anywhere else on the ship. It was a narrow white room with eight bunks, a dispensary cage, a surgical bay behind a curtain, and a single round port the size of a dinner plate that looked out along the hull toward Gall's limb. Ekene had hung a dried flower in a bag over the port. Nobody knew where he had got a flower.

He was sitting at the dispensary counter when she came in, writing up the day by the light of a single lamp. He looked up, and took off his reading lenses, and looked at her for a long moment without saying anything. Then he got up and came round the counter and put his arms round her, and she stood in them with her face against the stiff cotton of his coat and her fist in her pocket and did not cry, because she had not cried in nine years and was not about to start now in front of a dispensary cage.

"I heard it," he said into her hair. "Half the ship was on the line channel. I heard you talking to her."

"I didn't do anything."

"You talked to her."

"I couldn't do anything, Ekene. The Gull's thruster was stripped. It's been stripped three weeks. I put in for the part three times." She let go of him and stepped back. "I ran the line. That's what I did. I sat there and ran the dip and I listened to her go."

"If you'd stopped the dip—"

"If I'd stopped the dip the line would have walked and we'd have lost Odile as well. And the Bucket. And the drum, maybe. And they'd have docked the whole crew for the load." She sat down on the end of the nearest bunk. "That's the arithmetic. I did the arithmetic."

Ekene went back behind the counter and filled two cups from the urn and brought them over. It was the infirmary's tea, which was not tea at all but something he grew in a tray under the ultraviolet lamp in the surgical bay and dried and smoked, and which tasted like a wet fire. She drank it. He sat beside her on the bunk. He was fifty-two, eleven years older than she was, a big soft-spoken man with grey in his beard and the long patient hands of somebody who had spent his life doing small things carefully to people in pain. He had come to Corva on a medical contract six years ago, not bonded, which made him a strange thing on the _Patience_, a free man who could have left on any _Clement_. He had not left. Maren had asked him why, once, and he had said that he was a coward and the Combine's other postings were worse; and then, later, in the dark, that it was her.

"The factor," he said.

"You saw him?"

"He came by. Before the hall. He wanted to see the infirmary." Ekene turned his cup round in his hands. "He asked about our stock of painkillers. How many beds. Whether we had a cold store and what was in it. He was polite. He asked very good questions."

"What kind of questions?"

"The kind an auditor asks. What things cost. What could be moved." He looked at her sideways. "What do you think _changes_ means, Maren?"

"I think the _Clement_'s eleven days early," Maren said. "I think nobody sends a new factor to a skimmer that's making its numbers just to say hello." She drank the rest of her cup in one swallow, burning her throat. "And I think somebody cut Lusine's line."

She took her fist out of her pocket and opened it.

Ekene looked at the clip for a long time. Then he took it from her palm, gently, the way he took everything, and turned it under the lamp, and found the stub of the gate spring and the bright clean face of the cut, two-thirds of the way through.

"A blade," he said.

"A fine one. Not a cutter. Cutters crush. That's a saw cut, or a knife that's been honed. Somebody took that spring two-thirds through, so it'd hold in calm and go in the first hard shock." She heard her own voice, very level, the tether-chief's voice. "Every runner checks her clip at the lock. You snap it, you load it, you look at the gate. You don't look at the spring. Nobody looks at the spring."

"Who could do this?"

"Anybody who could get to her harness. Anybody in the runners' lock, between her last check yesterday and the dip. Riggers. Runners. Dock crew. Wardens." She stopped. "Me."

"Not you."

"No. Not me." She took the clip back. "She said something on the way down. On four, so it's not on the log. She said she'd read a dispatch on the relay, something that came ahead of the _Clement_ on the beacon. She said it was about all of us. She said she'd told someone, and she shouldn't have."

"Told who?"

"She didn't say. She went into the static." Maren closed her hand on the clip again. "She said, _I don't think it was the clip._"

They sat for a while without talking. Through the port, over Ekene's shoulder, she could see the long curve of the _Patience_'s hull lit by Gall-shine, rust-coloured, and beyond it the edge of Gall itself, and beyond that the black. It took her a moment to see what she was looking at. There was a smudge in the black, just above the planet's limb: a faint pale patch no bigger than a thumbprint, like a breath on glass, with the barest suggestion of a tail.

"There it is," said Ekene, following her eyes. "Teska's comet."

"The Lamp."

"She says it takes the dead out."

"She says a lot of things." Maren stood up. She was very tired. She put the clip back in her pocket and felt its small weight there, against her hip, where her line knife hung on her coat. "I'm going to find out who she told."

"Maren."

"What."

"Be careful who you ask," said Ekene.


# Chapter 2: Lampfast

*The Siege · 2654*

The first air I cut that year belongs to a family named Ocampo, and I cut it on the morning the Lamp comes back.

Their door is the eleventh on Ninth Tier South, a hatch in a wall of grey ice-brick with the number painted on it by somebody who had good intentions and no brush. The meter is set into the frame at chest height, under a little frosted window, and I don't need to wipe the window to read it, because I have read it every month for three years and I know what it is going to say. I wipe it anyway. You wipe the window. You read the numbers aloud into the ledger. You give people the courtesy of being measured.

"Ocampo, Ninth South eleven," I say, and the ledger on my wrist takes it down. "Household of five. Draw this cycle, four hundred and twelve litres an hour at the mean. Tithe owing, nineteen days. Tithe paid, none." I look at the door. "Prior notice served, twice."

Nobody answers. Somebody is behind the door; you can hear a household breathing, if you know how, a low composite sound under the hum of the warren fans, like a sleeping animal. I knock with the end of my valve key.

"Air-warden."

The fans hum. Up here, on the Ninth, they never stop, and they never quite manage; the Ninth is the highest tier of the city, cut so close under Kettle's surface that the ceiling sweats frost and the walls ache with cold, and the air that reaches it has been breathed by eight tiers of people on its way up. It tastes of them. You can taste the Third Tier's cooking in it, the Fifth's tannery, the Seventh's old men. Nobody shared lives on the Ninth. Nobody shared has ever been up here, except to look.

"Air-warden," I say again. "Isaura Venn. I'm required to put your feed to the floor. I'll do it from the frame. You don't have to open."

The hatch opens.

It's the girl. I knew it would be the girl. They always send the youngest, as if I were a dog that could be talked down. She is nine years old, Pilar Ocampo, small for nine the way the Ninth's children are all small, in a jumper with the sleeves rolled four times and a pair of cracked grey gloves she has had on all winter. She has a face like a coin, round and flat and very still, and she looks at me with it.

"Mama's at the plant," she says. "Papa's at the plant. Abuela's asleep. Tomás and Juli are asleep."

"I know."

"If you put it to the floor they won't wake up."

"They'll wake up," I say. "The floor is three hundred an hour for five. It's enough to sleep on. It's enough to live on. It isn't enough to work, or cook, or run about. It's the floor. Nobody dies on the floor."

This is true. I have to say it because it is true, and because it is in the Ordinance, and because if I don't say it then I am just a woman with a key standing in a corridor turning off a child's grandmother. Nobody dies on the floor. People die under it, and nobody is ever put under it, by me or by anybody, not by law; the floor is the floor. If you are on the floor and you get the ice cough, or your heart is weak, or you are very old or very new, then you may die, and that is not the floor's doing. That is the cough, or the heart. I read the Ordinance when I was nineteen and swore my oath as a warden, and I have read it every year since, and I am very clear on the point.

"Nobody dies on the floor," says Pilar, as if she is trying it in her mouth.

"Step back from the frame, please."

She doesn't step back. She stands in the hatch with her cracked gloves at her sides and watches me fit the key. The valve housing is cold enough to burn through my own gloves. It turns hard, the way they all turn hard up here, the brass furred with frost, and I have to put my shoulder into it, and the needle on the gauge goes down, and down, and settles on the red line, and the sound of the fans behind the hatch changes, very slightly, from a hum to a sigh.

"Ocampo, Ninth South eleven," I say to the ledger. "Feed set to floor at eight-oh-four, twelfth of June, fifty-four." I turn to the girl. "When your mother pays, it goes straight back. The ledger tells the plant. She doesn't have to find me."

"It's Lampfast," Pilar says.

"I know."

"Everybody's going to the Spine to see the Lamp. Mama said it's the first time in two hundred and sixty-three years. She said nobody alive's seen it. She said it's lucky."

"It's very lucky," I say. "You should go and see it."

"We can't go now. Abuela can't walk on the floor."

I don't have anything to say to that. I have the key in my hand and the ledger on my wrist and a list of forty-one doors to go before the end of shift, and I can feel the cold of the Ninth going down into my boots. I put the key back on my belt.

"Close your hatch," I tell her. "Keep the heat in."

She closes it. I stand for a moment looking at the number painted on it, the brave crooked eleven, and then I go on to the twelfth.

I am good at this. I want to be clear about that from the start, because I will say other things later that might make it seem I wasn't. I was a good air-warden. I was the best in my section three years running, which is to say I collected the most tithe and filed the fewest complaints and was never once reported for leniency, which is the only thing wardens are ever reported for. I knew every meter on the Ninth South by its sound. I knew which households would pay on the first notice and which on the second and which would never pay until the needle touched red, and which had a cousin on the Seventh who could be leaned on, and which had nobody at all. I did not cut a single feed that wasn't owed. I did not leave a single door without saying the words. I believed that what I did was hard and necessary and that it kept the city breathing, and that if I didn't do it somebody else would, worse.

All of that is true. I still believe some of it.

* * *

The Spine is the oldest thing in Patience and the only thing in it that was not built here.

It stands upright in the ice at the city's heart, two hundred metres of rust-red hull rising out of the lower warrens and up through every tier like the trunk of a tree up through the floors of a house. The founders sank it here, in the year of the Purchase, when they brought the old skimmer down out of Gall's sky and set it in Kettle's shell, and the city grew around it the way a pearl grows round grit. You can touch it on every tier. On the Ninth it comes through the floor of the South Market as a great curved wall with frost-flowers on it, and children lick the frost for luck. Inside it are the Council's chambers and the Archive and the old docks, and at the top, under the surface, the nave.

The nave was the drum hall of the ship, once. The drum is still there: a vast scarred cylinder lying on its side at the far end of the hall, as long as a street, with its ancient bearings seized solid and its skin covered from end to end in writing. Names. The founders' names, and the names of the dead before the founding, chalked by hand and then sealed under a skin of clear resin so that nothing would ever rub them off, layer on layer of capitals going brown with age. Nobody can read most of them any more; they are too old, too overwritten, too far up. Children are told they are the names of the Three Hundred. I have always liked one particular patch near the bottom, where somebody long ago drew, beside a name I could never make out, a small round shape with a tail. A seed with a tail. I didn't know what it was when I was little. I know now.

Above the drum, in the roof of the nave, is the Oculus. It's the only window in the city that looks at the sky. The founders cut it through forty metres of surface ice and lined it with a single great lens of fused glass, and for most of the year it shows you nothing but black and, if you're lucky, a slice of Gall going over like a striped wall. Today it shows the Lamp.

I come in late, after shift, with my key still on my belt, and the nave is fuller than I have ever seen it. The shared families are on the floor, where the benches are, in their good coats; the unshared are packed into the side galleries and the stairwells and up on the old maintenance gantries, hanging on to the rails, standing on each other. The air is thick and warm with them. Everybody is looking up.

It is not what I expected. I suppose I expected a star. It's a smear: a smudge of pale light in the black, very slightly green, no bigger than a thumbnail at arm's length, with a long thin fan of fainter light stretching away from it toward the bottom of the lens, like breath going out of a mouth on a cold day. It doesn't twinkle. It doesn't move. It hangs there, very quiet, looking down at us through forty metres of ice, and the whole of the nave has gone still to look back at it.

Two hundred and sixty-three years. The last time it was in that sky, the founders were alive. They looked up at it from the deck of the old skimmer, in the year they bought their air. It was here when the city began, the catechism says. _The Lamp was in the sky in the year of the Purchase._ Every shared child learns that line, and most unshared ones, and none of us has ever seen the Lamp, until now.

I find my mother on the east gallery, at the rail, near the top. She has come down from the Ninth with her neighbours in the morning to get a place, and stood all day for it, and she is grey with tiredness and with the cough, and she is looking up at the Oculus with her mouth a little open like a child's.

"You came," she says, when I put my arm through hers.

"I said I would."

"You've been on the Ninth." She can smell it on me; she can always smell it on me. She doesn't say anything else about it. My mother is a filter-mender, which is the kind of job the unshared do on the Ninth, sitting all day at a bench with a lamp and a pick, picking the sooted mesh of the warren fans clean enough to be used again. She's done it for forty years. She has the cough the filter-menders get, which is a dry deep bark that never quite goes, and she has my father's absence, and she has me, and she has her religion, which is the Lamp.

"Look at it, Isa," she says. "Look. It's come round."

"I'm looking."

"Your grandmother's grandmother told her about it. And her grandmother told her. All the way back." Her eyes are wet. "All the way back to the beginning. And now it's here. And we're here."

My mother is a Lamper. Not a loud one. She doesn't go to the meetings in the tannery yards on the Fifth or stand on the stairs with a placard; she doesn't tattoo comet-tails on her wrists, like the riggers do. She just believes, quietly, in her chair by her lamp, the thing the Lampers believe, which is that the city's comfort was bought with something, and that the something went out on the Lamp, and that it will come back. _The Lamp comes round,_ she used to say to me when I was small and couldn't sleep, _the debt comes due._ It was a lullaby to her. It took me until I was fifteen to understand that it was meant to be a threat.

Down on the floor of the nave, the First Shareholder has climbed on to the old tether gallery to speak. Lucan Strand is a tall man with silver hair and a voice like a good instrument, the kind that doesn't need to be raised. He has his son with him. Corin Strand is the best pilot in the dipper fleet, everybody knows it; he flies the deep runs into Gall's cloud-tops that bring up the city's fuel, and he has the face for it, all cheekbones and confidence, and he stands at his father's shoulder in his flight jacket like the illustration in a schoolbook.

Below them, on its mount on the drum's end, is the Share Plate.

It's smaller than you'd think, the first time you see it. A rough rectangle of grey steel about the size of a door panel, cut from the old hull of the skimmer by somebody who did not have the right tools for it, its edges blackened and wavy. Three hundred names are cut into it, in fifteen columns, by three hundred different hands. Some are deep and neat; some are barely scratches. Some are just marks. The first line, at the top of the first column, is blank: not empty but scraped, a gouged and roughened patch of metal where something was once written and somebody took it off again. The Blank Share. Every child knows what it is. It is the Company's share, the one share the Halloran-Vey Combine kept in the ship when the Three Hundred bought her, and which was struck off the day the Purchase was complete. It's the most famous scratch in the world.

"Two hundred and sixty-three years ago," says Lucan Strand, in his good voice, "that light was in this sky."

He doesn't need to raise it. The nave is silent.

"Two hundred and sixty-three years ago, three hundred men and women stood where I stand now, on the deck of this ship, and looked up at it, and made a decision. They decided that they would not be owned. They had nothing. They had a ship that was not theirs, and air that was not theirs, and lives that were written on somebody else's ledger. And they bought them back. Not with blood. Not with theft. With labour, and with bargaining, and with the long patience that gave this ship its name and this city its name. They bought their air." He lifts a hand toward the Plate. "And here are their names."

"_We bought our air,_" the floor says back, the shared families, in their good coats; and some of the galleries say it too, a little behind, the way you sing a hymn you don't quite know.

My mother doesn't say it. She is looking at the Lamp.

"Today," says Strand, "the Lamp has come round again. It has come round to a city of a hundred and eighty thousand souls, where there was once a camp of two thousand. It has come round to deep docks and a dipper fleet and the finest refineries in eleven lanes. It has come round to a free people. If the founders could see us—"

Somebody near the top of the west gallery shouts something. I don't catch it. Heads turn. A second voice takes it up, and a third, ragged, from the stairwells on the north side where the riggers have crowded in: _The Lamp comes round, the debt comes due._

Strand lets it go by. He is good at that. He waits with his hand still raised, mild, patient, until the wardens on the north stairs have moved in and the voices have stopped, and then he lowers it and smiles.

"There are always those," he says, "who would rather the past were a debt than a gift. I don't blame them. It's a hard city to be poor in." A small murmur of approval from the benches. "But it is the only free one there is. Happy Lampfast."

"Happy Lampfast," says the nave.

Afterward, when the crowd is breaking up and the stairwells are jammed solid, I take my mother down the east gallery the long way, past the Archive doors, because it's quieter. Brother Malachy is in his doorway. He's always in his doorway on feast days, watching the crowds go by like an old crane watching a river. He taught me my letters on the Fourth Tier when I was six and my numbers when I was eight, and argued with me about everything from nine to fifteen, and then got me my warden's place on his own recommendation, which I've never decided whether to forgive. He's seventy-six. He looks a hundred, and has done for twenty years.

"Isaura," he says. "Mistress Venn. You saw it?"

"We saw it," says my mother.

"Remarkable thing." He turns his head and looks up the length of the nave at the Oculus, where the smudge of green light hangs. "Do you know, they kept records of it? The founders. There's an orbit for it in the Archive, in somebody's hand, on the back of a dip log. They knew it would come round. They worked out to the week when." He smiles his thin smile. "They knew more than we give them credit for. They were dippers. Dippers know about things that go out and come back."

"The Lamp takes the dead out," says my mother, softly, "and brings them back."

Malachy looks at her for a moment. "So they say, on the Ninth," he says. "So they say."

* * *

The Concord comes through the Throat during the evening bells.

I'm on the Fourth by then, taking my mother home the long way round by the lifts, because she can't do the stairs any more. We're in the lift queue at Fourth Central when every speaker in the city clicks at once. That's what I remember: the click. Every speaker on every tier, all together, a single dry sound like a knuckle on a table, and then the hiss of an open channel, and then a voice.

"This is a message to the people of Kettle from the Concord of Vey."

The queue goes quiet. Everyone looks at the nearest speaker, which is a grey grille high on the wall above the lift doors, as if they might see the man through it.

He has a dry, educated, slightly tired voice. It sounds as though it is reading.

"My name is Anselm Grieve. I am a commodore of the Concord's Lane Service. At eighteen hundred today, Kettle time, a fleet under my command entered the Corva system through the lane mouth your people call the Throat. It consists of thirty-eight vessels. Their positions are being transmitted to your traffic control as I speak, as a courtesy, so that there may be no misunderstanding about our strength or our intentions."

My mother's arm tightens on mine.

"The Concord of Vey is the lawful successor to the Halloran-Vey Combine. It holds, by acquisition from the Combine's receivers, all of the Combine's titles, contracts, claims and accounts, including the claim to the Corva system and everything in it. The Combine never relinquished that claim. No instrument of sale exists. The occupation of the moon Kettle, and of the installations on it, and of the Combine vessel _Patience_, is therefore unlawful, and has been unlawful for two hundred and sixty-three years."

Somebody in the queue laughs, a short shocked bark of a laugh. Nobody joins in.

"The Concord has no wish to harm the people of Kettle. You are, in law, the Concord's tenants. Your labour and your debts, like your city, are its property; it has a lawful interest in your welfare. Accordingly I am instructed to offer the following terms. The governing council of the occupation will stand down. The Concord's officers will be admitted to the city and its installations. The accounts of all residents will be assessed and regularised. In return, there will be no reprisals, and no resident will be removed from Kettle without due process." A pause. A rustle, very faint, like paper. "You have three days to consider these terms. On the fourth day, if they are not accepted, I am instructed to take such steps as are necessary to secure the Concord's property. I would ask you to believe that I take no pleasure in saying so. This message will repeat."

It repeats. We stand in the queue and listen to it again, word for word, the same dry tired voice, the same small rustle of paper in the same place. Halfway through the second time the lift comes, and nobody gets in.

"Thirty-eight ships," someone says.

"They can't. They can't just—"

"Tenants." An old man near the front, a dye-worker from the Fifth by his hands, spits on the floor. "Tenants. We'll see about tenants."

My mother says nothing. She is looking at the speaker grille with the same face she turned up to the Oculus: wet-eyed, open-mouthed, almost glad.

"The Lamp comes round," she says, very quietly, to nobody, "the debt comes due."

"Mama. Don't."

"I'm only saying."

"Don't say it here."

The First Shareholder answers the Concord forty minutes later, on the same speakers. I'm in my mother's room on the Ninth by then, sitting on the end of her cot with my boots still on, and we listen to it together by the light of her work-lamp, with her filters laid out on the bench under it in their neat sooty rows.

Lucan Strand doesn't sound tired at all. He sounds the way he sounded in the nave: warm, unhurried, certain.

He says that the people of Patience have heard the Concord's message. He says that the city of Patience is a free city, established by lawful purchase in the year twenty-three ninety-one, and that it does not recognise the claim of the Concord of Vey or of any other body to its people, its labour, or its air. He says that he has spoken with the Council of Shareholders, and that the Council is unanimous. He says that the city has weathered worse than a fleet, that it was built by people who knew how to be patient, and that it will be patient now. He says that the dipper fleet is recalled and the surface locks are sealed and the city's defences, which have not been tested in a hundred years but which have been maintained, every one of them, are manned. He says that the people of Patience are not tenants. He says it the way you would say a thing about the weather.

"In the meantime," he says, "the Council has adopted certain emergency measures, which will be posted on every tier tonight. I ask every citizen of Patience to observe them, for the common good. Our air is our life. We must husband it. Happy Lampfast, and good night."

The measures are posted at midnight. I read them on my wrist, sitting on my mother's cot, while she sleeps.

There are eleven of them. Most are what you'd expect: curfews, rationing of heat, a ban on unlicensed movement between tiers. The ninth says that the dipper fleet is suspended until further notice, which means that nobody will be going up into Gall's sky for fuel while there are thirty-eight Concord ships between us and it. The tenth says that the refinery reserves are to be husbanded accordingly.

The eleventh says that, for the duration of the emergency, the air-tithe payable by unshared households is doubled, and that the floor is lowered by a third, and that air-wardens are to apply the new rates from the first of the next cycle.

I read it three times. Then I turn off my wrist, and sit in the dark with the fans sighing, and listen to my mother breathe.

The first slug hits the surface just before dawn.

I don't know that's what it is. Nobody does. I'm asleep sitting up, and I wake because the whole city rings: a deep enormous note that comes up through the floor and the walls and the cot and my own bones, like being inside a struck bell. It goes on a long time. Ice dust sifts down from the ceiling and settles on my mother's filters. Somewhere above us, forty metres up, something has hit the shell of Kettle at a speed I can't imagine, and the whole moon has taken it and passed it down to us, tier by tier, all the way to the bottom of the sea.

My mother sits up in her cot with her hair full of frost.

"Isa," she says. "Is it the Lamp?"

"No, Mama," I tell her. "It's the Concord."

The bell is still ringing.


# Chapter 3: Collateral

*The Last Return · 2917*

Mother Szabo keeps her ledgers in stone.

Til Brask has always found this funny, in the way she finds most things on Cinderhaven funny, which is to say not at all but with her mouth. The counting-house sits on the Ledger Ring, a long curved gallery of debt-houses with their shutters down and their lamps lit, and every one of them has a vault behind it cut into the ring's ballast, and in every vault, on shelves of fused grey rock, the paper. Real paper. Bound. The Directorate's courts burn down every few years, and the Directorate's data-houses get wiped every time the Directorate changes hands, which is more often than the courts burn; and so the debt-houses of Cinderhaven went back, a century ago, to writing their debts on something that does not care who is in charge. Paper keeps. Stone keeps. Debt keeps best of all.

Szabo's vault is the deepest on the Ring. Her counting-room is in front of it, a long low room lined with clerks' desks, warm and dim and smelling of ink and old tea, with Mother Szabo herself at the far end of it behind a desk the size of a bunk, writing. She doesn't look up when Til comes in. She never looks up when anybody comes in. She lets them stand.

Til stands.

She stands with her hands in the pockets of her deck coat and her weight on her good leg and looks at the clerks, because it's better than looking at Szabo. There are eleven of them, bonded every one, mostly young, mostly women, in the grey half-sleeves of the house, each bent under a lamp over a ledger. The fourth from the end is her niece. Juno has her hair cropped short now, like a rigger's. She's taller than she was at Mid-year. She has a pen in her hand and an ink-stain on her jaw and she's adding a column of figures with her lips moving very slightly, the way her mother used to, and she has not looked up either.

"Captain Brask," says Mother Szabo, finally, still writing.

"Mother."

"You've had my notice."

"I've had it."

"Then you know where we are." Szabo puts down her pen and lifts her head. She's a small, soft, round woman of sixty or so with a face like a kindly baker and eyes like a pair of rivets. "Eleven thousand two hundred marks, principal and interest, against the hull and engines of the salvage tug _Dray_, registered Dock Nine. Six months in arrears. Three notices served. The hull is mine on Thursday, Captain. My men will be at Dock Nine on Thursday morning with the yard-master and the seals, and they will put the seals on your hatches, and you will step off, and that will be that."

"I'll have it by Thursday."

"You will not have it by Thursday." Szabo says it kindly. "You have three hundred marks in your account at the Chandlers' and a job lined up in the Tarn yards that pays six hundred if it pays at all. You have a crew of one, who is sixty-three years old and owes me nine hundred himself. You have a tug that is fifty years past her rating and a licence that expires in May. I know what you have, Captain Brask. It's my business to know." She folds her soft little hands on the ledger. "I am not an unkind woman. I've carried you longer than I would carry anybody else. I've carried you because I liked your sister, and because I have your sister's girl, and because I thought, for her sake, you might make something of yourself. You haven't."

Down the room, Juno's pen has stopped.

Til looks at her own boots. They are old boots. Everything she owns is old. "What do you want, Mother?"

"What makes you think I want anything?"

"Because you asked me here. You could've sent the yard-master on Thursday and saved yourself the conversation. You never have a conversation you don't make money on."

Szabo smiles. It's a real smile, warm and pleased, the smile of a teacher whose slowest pupil has finally said something right.

"I have a client," she says. "Who wants a tug with a deep-salvage rating. Armour, reach, a long winch, a crew that can do a hard landing on an irregular body, and a captain who will go where she is sent and not ask the client his business." She tilts her head. "And who is desperate enough to go through the Corva lane."

There's a silence in the counting-room. One of the clerks drops a pen.

"No," says Til.

"I haven't told you what it pays."

"It doesn't matter what it pays. The Corva lane's raw. It's been raw since it opened. Three ships have gone through it in two years and one's come back, and that one came back without its crew."

"It came back without most of its crew."

"I'm not going through the Corva lane."

"It pays," says Mother Szabo, as if Til had not spoken, "thirty thousand marks. Ten in hand when you sign, twenty on delivery. Of which I am to have forty in the hundred, for the introduction and the risk." She lets that sit. "Which leaves you eighteen thousand. Which clears the _Dray_. Which leaves you, by my arithmetic, six thousand eight hundred."

Til doesn't say anything.

"And," says Szabo, "the balance of a certain bond held by this house, against the debts of the late Margit Brask, salvager, deceased, and carried to her daughter. Which stands at"—she does not look it up—"seventeen thousand nine hundred and forty marks."

At the fourth desk from the end, Juno Brask lifts her head.

She doesn't look at Til. She looks at Szabo. Her face is very still. She has her mother's face, broad across the cheekbones, with her mother's straight black brows, and when it's still like that Til can't look at it for long.

"Six thousand eight hundred doesn't clear seventeen nine," Til says.

"No. It doesn't. But it would bring it within reach, wouldn't it, Captain? Two more years of honest hauling. Three, perhaps. And there might be"—Szabo spreads her hands—"bonuses. My client is generous. He's also old, and ill, and in a hurry. A captain who did him good service might find him very generous indeed."

"Who is he?"

"That," says Szabo, "is between you and him. His agent will be at the Roundsmen's chapel on Dock Nine at the second bell. You can tell him no to his face. I would rather you didn't." She picks up her pen again. "That's all, Captain Brask."

Til doesn't move.

"Mother."

"Hm?"

"Why me?"

Szabo writes a line. Then she looks up again with her rivet eyes, and her voice is still kind, and Til would rather she had been anything else.

"Because nobody else I own is stupid enough," she says. "And because I've heard the stories about you. On the Harrow. They say when it came to it, you cut the line." She looks down the room. "A client likes to know his captain can do what's needed."

Til walks out of the counting-house without saying anything else. As she passes the fourth desk she slows, without meaning to, and Juno's eyes come up and meet hers for the first time in eleven months, black and level and without any expression in them at all; and then Juno looks down again and goes back to her column of figures, and Til goes on out into the noise of the Ring.

* * *

The Roundsmen keep a chapel at the end of every dock on Cinderhaven, because Roundsmen are dock people, and dock people die.

The one on Dock Nine is a converted lock, a round steel room with a domed ceiling and the old outer hatch welded shut and painted over with stars. There are benches. There's a little brazier in the middle, kept going day and night by whoever's nearest, with a dish of salt beside it. Round the walls, in chalk, the Roundsmen write the names of the dead and the dates they went out, and under each name a little loop, a circle not quite closed, which is the sign for _on its way_. Some of the loops are fifty years old. Some are from last week. Til knows eleven of the names on these walls personally, and one of them very well, and she does not look at that one.

Oz is sitting on the front bench with his hands in the salt.

"I heard," he says, without turning round.

"Everybody on the Ring heard. Szabo's clerks have mouths."

"Corva." Oz rubs the salt between his fingers and lets it fall into the brazier, where it spits blue. He is a big man gone soft and grey, with a face like a bag of stones and the most delicate hands Til has ever seen, an engineer's hands, an instrument-maker's; he's been her engineer for nine years and her friend for thirty, and he has followed her into places no sane man would go and out of them again, and he has never once said _I told you so_, which is the only reason she still speaks to him. "The Lamp's going round. They're saying it's the last time. They're saying Gall's going to eat it."

"Who's saying?"

"Everybody who reads the Observatory sheets. Which is me and four old Roundsmen and a boy who sweeps the observatory. It's coming in too close this time. Inside the giant's reach. It'll come apart." He lets the last of the salt fall. "The Lamp takes the dead out and brings them back. That's the saying. Nobody ever said what happens when it doesn't come back."

"I don't need a sermon, Oz."

"You're getting one anyway. I'm sixty-three. I've earned it." He wipes his hands on his knees and turns round on the bench. "Are you going to do it?"

"I'm going to hear the man out."

"That's yes."

"That's hear the man out."

"That's yes," says Oz, comfortably, and turns back to his brazier.

The agent arrives on the second bell exactly. He comes in from the dock side, ducking under the lintel, and stops just inside the door to let his eyes adjust; and Til, who has spent her whole life around men who are dangerous, files him immediately and without effort in the drawer where she keeps the worst of them. He's not big. He's not anything much. Forty, perhaps, slender, in a good grey coat with the collar turned up, close-cropped hair going silver at the sides, and a long pale scar running down from his left ear into his collar like a seam in a glove. He has a quiet, courteous, attentive face. He stands very still. That's the thing. Most people fidget when they come into a chapel, out of reverence or embarrassment. He stands like a man who has been taught that the first person to move is the first person to be seen.

"Captain Brask," he says. "My name is Cato Marchetti. I represent Mr Hollis Vey. Thank you for coming."

"I haven't said yes."

"No. Of course." He comes and sits on the bench across the brazier from her, and nods to Oz, who nods back. "Mother Szabo will have told you the terms."

"She told me the money. She didn't tell me the job."

"The job is a retrieval." Marchetti puts his hands on his knees. "You know of the comet the Corva people call the Lamp."

"Everybody on Dock Nine knows about the Lamp. Half the drunks on this dock have a map to the Lamp Hoard. I've bought two."

"Then you know the legend." He doesn't smile. "The Lamp is a periodic comet in a resonant orbit with Corva's gas giant. It comes in every two hundred and sixty-odd years, passes close by the giant, and goes back out. Last year the Tarn Observatory sent a probe through the Corva lane, which had just reopened, and the probe came back with its pictures, which is more than the crews of the two ships that followed it did." He reaches into his coat and brings out a slim grey slate and lays it on the bench between them. On the screen is a smear of light: a bright head, a long dim tail. "The Lamp is inbound now. It reaches the giant on the second of May. This time it will pass inside the giant's limit. It will be torn apart, and what's left of it will fall into Gall's atmosphere, and it will be gone."

"So I hear."

"Mr Vey believes there is something on the Lamp," says Marchetti, "that belongs to his family. He would like it retrieved before the second of May."

"What is it?"

"I don't know exactly."

"You don't know."

"Mr Vey's family were—are—an old one. They had interests in Corva a very long time ago. They lost something there, in the Corva system, in a way that has never been fully explained, and Mr Vey has spent a large part of his life and most of his fortune trying to understand what. He believes it went out on the Lamp. He believes this is the last chance anyone will ever have to bring it back. He is"—Marchetti chooses the word—"devoted to the question."

"He's mad."

"He's dying," says Marchetti, without any change in his voice. "It amounts to much the same thing, in my experience. He has perhaps three months. He would like to be there."

Til looks at him. "He wants to come?"

"He insists on it. He'll travel in his own medical cradle, with his own supplies; he won't be a burden on your ship. I will accompany him." Marchetti turns the slate off and puts it away. "You'll take us through the lane, Captain. You'll take us to the Lamp, using whatever approach you judge best. You'll land, and assist Mr Vey—assist me—in locating and recovering whatever is there. And you'll bring us home. Thirty thousand marks. Ten in hand."

"And if there's nothing there?"

"Then you'll have been paid ten thousand marks to fly a dying man to look at a comet. Many people would consider that a fair day's work."

Oz makes a sound into the brazier that might be a laugh.

"Szabo wants her girl aboard," Til says. "That's the deal, she said. One of her people, to witness."

"So I'm told."

"Which one?"

Marchetti considers her for a moment with his quiet, attentive face.

"I believe," he says, "she means to send your niece."

Til had known that. She had known it from the moment Szabo said _your sister's girl_, the way you know you've stepped on a loose plate on a hull a half-second before it goes. It doesn't help. She sits with her hands on her knees and looks at the brazier and the salt and the loops on the walls, the circles not quite closed, and one name in particular that she is not looking at, three rows up on the left.

"Mother Szabo," says Marchetti, "has a sense of humour."

"Mother Szabo," says Til, "has a sense of collateral."

She signs at the fourth bell. The ten thousand marks are in the Chandlers' by the fifth, and by the sixth eleven thousand two hundred of them are gone again, out through the Chandlers' wall and into the stone vault on the Ledger Ring, and the yard-master's seals are cancelled. That's what money is on Cinderhaven. You never touch it. It goes through you like weather.

* * *

The _Dray_ is not beautiful.

Til will be the first to say it. She's a deep-salvage tug of the old Tarn pattern, built sixty years ago for hauling dead hulks off the Harrow and the other wreck-fields of the outer Reach, and she looks it: a squat, blunt, armoured wedge of a hull, scarred and patched and painted over so many times that nobody knows any more what colour she started, with a stubby drive cluster at the back, a long grapple-and-winch boom slung under her belly like a folded insect's leg, and a forward cabin so small that two people can only pass in it if one of them is asleep. She has a fusion core that runs hot and an air plant that runs cold and a winch that will haul a hundred kilometres of cable and has hauled most of it. She is slow. She is ugly. She cannot be killed. Til has tried.

Oz spends two days going over her from the drive cones to the forward lamps, while Til spends two days spending other people's money. They buy cable. They buy reaction mass. They buy air, eleven tanks of it, more air than the _Dray_ has carried at one time in her life; they buy ice-anchors and screw-pitons and a thermal lance and two new pressure suits, one of them small, which Til buys without saying anything and Oz watches her buy without saying anything either. They buy food. Cato Marchetti comes down to the dock twice to look things over and goes away again, courteous, attentive, saying almost nothing; on the second visit he brings four long crates of his own, sealed, and asks for them to go in the cargo bay, and Til opens one with Oz standing by, because it's her ship, and finds medical supplies, and closes it again.

"He didn't mind," says Oz, afterward. "You opening it."

"No."

"He wanted you to."

"Yes," says Til.

They're in the cargo bay, on the night before departure, stowing the last of the cable. The bay's lit by work-lamps clipped to the ribs and smells of old grease and cold metal and the dust that gets into everything on Cinderhaven, fine grey dust from the ring's ore-mills that you can taste for a week after you leave. Oz is coiling cable on to a drum with his delicate hands, talking to himself in the old Roundsman cant his mother taught him, half prayer and half swearing.

"My mother's people came out of Corva," he says, unexpectedly.

Til looks up from the anchors. "You never said."

"You never asked. It was a long way back. Two hundred years and more. Before the lane went dark." He finishes the coil, ties it off. "Dippers. They used to go down into the giant's air on ropes, she said, forty kilometres of rope, and scoop the weather up in a bucket. And when they died they gave them to the giant. Lowered them down on the rope and let them go. And they had the comet, and they had a saying about it. The Lamp takes the dead out and brings them back." He picks up the next coil. "That's where we got it. The Roundsmen. All of it. The salt and the loops and the going-out. It's dipper stuff. It's from Corva."

"Then the Hoard's dipper stuff too."

"The Hoard's drunk stuff," says Oz. "There's no Hoard. Dippers didn't have anything to hoard. They had debts." He tugs the cable. "But it's funny. That we're going there. Back round."

"It's not funny. It's thirty thousand marks."

"That too."

The hatch at the forward end of the bay grinds open and lets in a slab of yellow dock light, and a figure in it with a bag over its shoulder.

Juno has a grey house coat on over a rigger's undersuit, and a cheap pair of dock boots, and the bag, which is small and old and has belonged to someone else before her. She stops in the hatch. She looks at Oz, and at the cable, and at the work-lamps, and at the stained ribs of the hull, and finally at Til.

"Mother Szabo sent me," she says.

"I know."

"I'm to witness the recovery. And log the cargo. And report back to the house." She says it like something learned off a slate. "I'm not crew. I'm not under your orders. Mother Szabo says if you put me to work I'm to log it and she'll bill you."

"That sounds like her."

"Where do I sleep?"

"There's a bunk aft of the galley," Til says. "It's the warm one. There's a new suit in the locker by it that ought to fit you. You'll need to try it before we go through the lane. Oz will check your seals."

Juno looks at her for a long moment. Til waits for it, whatever it's going to be, and is ready for it, she thinks; she has had eleven years to get ready for it.

"Don't talk to me," says Juno Brask, quite politely. "Unless it's about the job."

She goes past them down the bay toward the galley hatch, and Oz moves out of her way with his coil of cable, and she doesn't look at either of them; and then the galley hatch closes behind her and it's just the two of them in the work-light with the cable and the cold.

Oz goes back to his coiling. After a while he says, without looking up, "She's got Mags's walk."

"I know."

"Tall, though. Taller than Mags. That's from the father, whoever he was."

"I know, Oz."

"She'll come round."

"She won't," says Til. "And she shouldn't."

The client comes aboard at the first bell of the morning, an hour before the departure slot. Til has been expecting a sick old man in a chair. What comes up the ramp out of the dock's grey half-light is a coffin.

It's a medical cradle, she knows that; she's seen them before, on the Directorate's hospital ships, though never one this fine. It's a long, low, gleaming shell of white composite on a powered sled, with a clear canopy over the upper half and racks of quiet instruments along its sides, humming. Two dock porters walk it up the ramp, and Cato Marchetti walks behind it with a hand resting lightly on its rim, the way a man walks beside a horse. Under the canopy, propped on pillows, is Hollis Vey.

He is very thin. That's the first thing. He's a long man, and the illness has eaten everything from him but the length, so that he lies in his white shell like a heron in a nest, all bones and joints. His skin has the papery yellow-grey of the very sick. His hair is white and wispy and was once, you can see, thick and fair. He's seventy, perhaps, or a hard sixty. His eyes are pale blue, and wet, and completely alert, and they move over the _Dray_'s cargo bay, its ribs and its lamps and its drums of cable, with an expression that Til, after a moment, recognises as delight.

"Captain Brask," he says. His voice comes through a speaker grille in the cradle's rim, thin and dry and slightly delayed. "Forgive me for not getting up."

"Mr Vey."

"This is a very ugly ship."

"Yes."

"Good." He smiles. His teeth are perfect and slightly too large for his face. "Ugly ships last. My family built beautiful ones. You'll notice we haven't got any left." He turns his head on the pillow, with some effort, to look at her properly. "Mother Szabo tells me you're the best deep-salvage captain on the Ring."

"Mother Szabo tells people what makes her money."

"Then we understand each other. She also tells me you've no wish to know my business, which I very much appreciate, and which I don't for a moment believe." The pale eyes are amused. "We have a long way to go together, Captain. I'll tell you my business, when I've decided how much of it I can bear to say aloud. In the meantime, I would be grateful for a place to plug this thing in, and a window, if your ship runs to windows. I'd like to see it, when we get there."

"The Lamp?"

"Corva," says Hollis Vey. "All of it. My family hasn't seen it in five hundred years."

They undock at the second bell. Til takes the _Dray_ out herself, by hand, the way she always does, backing her off the dock on her manoeuvring jets with the yard-master swearing at her on the traffic channel for doing it by hand, and swings her round in the long shadow of the ring, and lights her drive. Cinderhaven falls away behind them, a great grey wheel turning slowly in the light of Tarn, with its docks and its chapels and its stone vaults full of paper. Ahead, two days off, is the Throat of the Corva lane.

Oz comes up into the cockpit as the ring shrinks. He leans on the back of her chair and looks at the rear display for a while without saying anything.

"Something just came off the ring behind us," he says. "Dock Two. Big hull. Running quiet."

Til looks. She knows the hull. Everybody on Cinderhaven knows the hull: a long black repossession cutter with a ram bow and four turrets, the house mark of a ledger and a pen painted small on her flank, the _Carrion Hymn_.

"Well," says Til. "Mother's coming too."


# Chapter 4: Consolidation

*The Bonded · 2391*

The runners lived aft of the drum, in a long low compartment the crew called the Shelf, where the bunks were stacked four high along both walls and the air came in through a single overworked fan that had been rattling since before any of them were born. Each runner had a bunk and a locker. When a runner went into Gall, the locker was emptied by the warden's office, and anything of value was sold against the account, and the rest went into the waste cycler. It had been three days. The warden's office had not got round to Lusine's yet. Maren had made sure of that, by giving Bram Kovač two dips' worth of overtime on the drum.

Odile was sitting on her sister's bunk when Maren came in, with the locker open beside her and its contents laid out on the blanket in rows, as if for an inventory. A spare undersuit. A comb. A packet of the cheap dried fruit they sold in the Camp at Midwinter. A cloth bag of hair ties. A stack of letters from somebody in the Camp, tied with string. A small battered slate with a cracked screen. A carved wooden fish, no bigger than a thumb, very old.

"That was our mother's," Odile said, without looking up. "The fish. She carved it. She used to carve things. She said she'd teach me but she didn't."

Maren sat down on the bunk opposite. "I'm sorry, Odile."

"You keep saying that."

"I keep meaning it."

Odile picked up the fish and turned it over in her fingers. Her face was swollen and blotched and fierce, and she had not slept, by the look of her, since the dip; she had done her shifts anyway, both of them, on the line, at six kilometres, and run them clean, and Maren had watched her dot on the board and said nothing, because there was nothing to say to a runner who wanted to be on the line except _clamps on_.

"The warden came round," Odile said. "Hale. Yesterday. With the form." She put the fish down in its place in the row. "Said I'd got her account now. Said it was standard. Two hundred and eighty days, carried forward, at the standard rate. For her suit and her bottle and her beacon and the inspection on the crawler. He was very sorry."

"He's always very sorry."

"I said I'd pay it." Odile looked up at last. "I said I'd pay it, Chief, because what else do you say? And he said that was very good of me and wrote it down. And then he said, was there anything of hers I'd found, any papers, any messages. For the inquiry." She was watching Maren's face. "I said no. Because I hadn't looked yet."

"And now you have."

"And now I have."

Odile reached under the pillow and brought out a strip of grey foil, the kind the relay printers used for hardcopy, folded small and soft with handling. She held it out.

"It was in her boot," she said. "Her deck boot. The left one. Inside the liner, under the heel. She always kept her money there when we were kids, in case somebody went through her pockets. I knew where to look." Her mouth moved. "Nobody else would."

Maren unfolded the foil.

It was a relay transcript, printed in the cramped type the old relay printers used, and it had been printed by hand, one line at a time, as if somebody had been feeding the printer a little at a time so it wouldn't log a full job. It was headed with the Halloran-Vey wheel and a string of routing codes, and below the codes, a single block of text.

_FROM: Combine Operations, Halloran. TO: Factor-designate A. QUIST, by beacon ahead of CLEMENT. SUBJECT: Consolidation of Corva Operation._

_1. Corva Operation to close effective arrival of factor-designate. Operation no longer commercially viable following Sorrel price adjustment. Board resolution 2391/114 refers._

_2. Skimmer PATIENCE to complete current season dips to maximise final load. Vessel to be decommissioned and scuttled in Gall at season end. Salvage value does not justify transit._

_3. Skilled bonded (est. 300, PATIENCE complement) to be transferred with accounts aboard CLEMENT to Levy Office, Sorrel Station, for assignment under Combine-Levy labour agreement 2390/22. Transfer to be presented as relocation._

_4. Kettle Camp installations and bonded accounts (est. 2,100 households) to remain in place. Supply to cease on departure of CLEMENT. Accounts to be offered at discount to secondary holders._

_5. Factor to manage expectations. Contingency authority (Schedule H) confirmed. ADMONITION to follow as escort._

Maren read it once, slowly, and then again. The fan rattled. Somewhere forward of the Shelf the drum was beginning its spin-up for the second dip, a long rising groan that came through the deck like a tide.

"What's the Levy," said Odile.
