"The Levy Office is how the Combine rents bonded to the Sorrel lane forts," Maren said. Her own voice sounded strange to her. "For the war. Diggers. Loaders. People to stand on gun-decks and fill sandbags with ice. They pay the Combine by the head. The Combine pays the bonded the standard rate against their account. Most of them never come back up the lane."

"And the Camp."

"_Supply to cease._"

"What does that mean?"

Maren looked at her. Odile was nineteen. She had been born on Kettle, in the Camp, in the warrens of the third shaft, where her mother had carved fish and her father had died of the ice cough when she was two; she had never been anywhere else. She knew what supply meant. She was asking because she wanted somebody to say it so she would not have to.

"It means nobody's coming back," said Maren. "It means the _Clement_ leaves and doesn't come again, and the Camp has what's in its tanks and its stores, and when that's gone, it's gone. The refinery runs on what the _Patience_ brings it, and the _Patience_ will be at the bottom of Gall. They'll have three months, maybe. Four, if they're careful. Then the air plant stops."

"Two thousand households."

"Twenty-one hundred."

"And then somebody buys their accounts," said Odile, very quietly. "At a discount. Somebody buys the debts of dead people."

"Somebody always does."

Odile put her face in her hands. She didn't cry. She sat there with her hands over her face and her shoulders very still, breathing, and after a while she said through her fingers, "She told someone."

"She said so. On the line."

"She'd have told me. She told me everything. She didn't tell me this." Odile took her hands away. Her eyes were dry and terrible. "She'd only not tell me if she was scared for me. If she'd told somebody else first and it had gone wrong. If she thought they'd—" She stopped. "Who'd she tell, Chief?"

"I don't know."

"She'd have told someone she trusted. Someone she—" Odile's mouth twisted. "She was seeing somebody. The last couple of months. She wouldn't say who. She went all quiet and pleased about it, like when we were kids and she'd stolen something. I thought it was one of the dock boys."

"Was it?"

"I don't know. She said—she said he was funny. She said he made her laugh in the lock." Odile looked at the rows of her sister's things on the blanket: the comb, the fruit, the fish. "She said he was always in debt and he didn't care. She said it was restful."

Maren's hands had gone very cold. She folded the foil up again along its old creases, small and soft, and held it.

"Keep this between us," she said. "Not Hale. Not anyone. Not even the runners."

"Why?"

"Because the last person who read it told somebody," said Maren, "and she's in Gall."

* * *

Quist told them that evening, at the change of shift, in the drum hall, under the names.

He did it well. Maren had to give him that, afterward, when she had the stomach to give him anything. He stood on the gallery with Hale beside him and the marine sergeant at his back, in his plain dark coat with the wheel on the lapel, and he did not read from a slate. He told them that the Combine had reviewed its operations in the Corva system and had concluded, with regret, that the market for Corva deuterium could no longer sustain them. He told them that the _Patience_ was an old ship, older than most of them, and that she would finish her season and then be retired. He told them that the Combine valued its skilled bonded very highly, and that every one of the _Patience_'s complement would be relocated, with their accounts intact and in good standing, to new postings in the Sorrel lanes, where the work was steady and the air was cheap. He told them the _Clement_ would carry them. He told them it would sail at the end of the month.

He told them nothing about the Levy, or the war, or the Camp.

He did not lie about the Camp. That was the clever thing. When old Pirro from the Bucket crew shouted up from the floor, _What about our families? What about the Camp?_, Quist turned to look at him with his long grave lined face and said that the Kettle installations would be transferred to a successor operator under the usual terms, and that the Combine's obligations to the Camp's residents would pass with them, and that family members of relocated bonded who wished to follow on were welcome to apply for passage at their own account. Every word of it was true. Maren, standing at the back of the hall under the drum with the foil in her pocket, could hear exactly how true each word was, and exactly how much it weighed.

_At their own account._ A passage on the _Clement_ to Sorrel cost more than a rigger earned against his debt in eight years.

The hall took it the way bonded halls took things. There was a long silence, and then a noise, a sort of collective exhalation like a fan spinning down, and then the arguments began, small ones, everywhere at once, people turning to their neighbours. A woman was crying near the slot. Somebody was laughing. Danek Sorell, beside Maren, said "Sorrel," three or four times, as if he were trying to remember where he had heard the word.

"I've got kids in the Camp, Chief," he said. "Three. Tamar's in the third shaft. What's he mean, apply?"

"He means you can't."

"So we go to Sorrel and they stay."

"That's what he means."

"For how long?"

Maren didn't answer him.

Up on the gallery, Quist had stepped back, and Hale had stepped forward with his slate under his arm and his soft patient voice raised just enough. Order, the warden said. Questions to be raised with section chiefs, who would be meeting with the factor individually over the next days. Dips to continue as rostered. Air rations unchanged. Any bonded found organising, or refusing duty, or damaging Combine property, would have their accounts reviewed, with all that that implied, and their families' accounts likewise. He said it gently. Bram Kovač and two other trusties stood at the foot of the gallery stair with their shock-sticks loose in their hands, looking at nobody in particular.

"Organising," said Teska, loudly, from the bench near the drum where she always sat. "What's that, organising? Is that talking? Is it organising if I talk?"

"Grandam," said Hale, with great kindness, "nobody's going to stop you talking."

"No," said Teska. "You never have." She turned her head toward the hall, with her one good eye. "I've been on this ship sixty-one years. I came out on her in pieces. I've buried two husbands and a daughter on that line. Now they say they're going to sink her." She spat on the deck. "Gall can have her. Gall's had everything else."

Some of the hall laughed. Most of it didn't.

Maren found her brother at the edge of the crowd near the riggers' stair, where he always was, where the talk was. He was flushed and bright-eyed and talking fast to a knot of young riggers, his hands moving, and when he saw her coming he broke off and came to meet her with his arms open like a man at a party.

"Sorrel," he said. "Did you hear him? Sorrel, Maren."

"I heard him."

"Off this rock. Off this ship. Do you know what they've got on Sorrel? Real gravity, on the forts. They've got markets. They've got—God, I don't know what they've got, I've never been anywhere. Neither have you." He caught her by the shoulders. His breath smelled of the illegal still in the dock section, which meant he had been celebrating since before the hall. "We're getting out. Both of us. Thirty years they've had us hanging off this planet on a string and now they're letting us go."

"They're not letting us go."

"Same thing."

"It isn't, Abel."

He looked at her face, and some of the brightness went out of his. He let go of her shoulders.

"You've got that look," he said. "Your chief's look. Like something's about to walk."

"Something is."

"What?"

She looked at him. Her little brother, twenty-nine years old, the same height as her and twice as handsome, with their mother's eyes and their father's absence and a laugh that had been getting him out of trouble since he was four. Everyone on the _Patience_ liked Abel. Everyone on the _Patience_ was owed money by Abel, or owed him a favour, or both. He had never kept a credit in his account for longer than a week in his life. It was, Lusine had said to her sister, restful.

"Not here," Maren said.

* * *

The factor sent for her two hours after the hall.

He had taken Moberly's old cabin, forward of the bridge, and done nothing to it; it was still Moberly's, with the fat man's soft chairs and his hideous picture of a sailing ship on a green sea, and his stains on the carpet. Quist sat behind Moberly's desk with a single slate in front of him and a cup of real coffee, which Maren could smell from the door, and he got up when she came in, which no factor had ever done.

"Chief Tsolo. Thank you. Sit down, please."

She stood.

He looked at her for a moment, and then sat down again himself, without fuss. "As you like. Would you like coffee? It's real. I'm told there hasn't been any on this ship in ten years."

"No."

"No." He turned the cup a little on its saucer. "I'll come to the point, then. I need a tether-chief to manage the end of the season. You're the senior chief on this ship and the crew listen to you, which is not true of the others, or of the warden, though he'd like to think so. I need the last dips run clean, the last loads brought up, and the _Patience_ ready for decommission on the twenty-ninth. I need the crew calm while it happens. I can offer you something for it."

"What?"

"An assessment," said Quist. "When you reach Sorrel. As supervisory rather than general labour. It would mean a different rate against your account, a different kind of work. You'd be off the gun-decks." He paused. "You'd keep your air."

"And Abel?"

"Your brother? I could put him on the same assessment. I'd have to bend it."

"And Danek Sorell's three children in the third shaft?"

Quist did not answer at once. He looked down at the slate in front of him, and then up at her again, and his long lined face did not change, but something in it went still, the way a man goes still when he hears a sound in the dark.

"You've read the dispatch," he said.

Maren said nothing.

"The advance dispatch. On the beacon. The one that came ahead of me." He nodded slowly. "There was a runner on the relay. The one who died on the line. I'm told she worked the relay on her off-shifts."

"Lusine Arda."

"Lusine Arda. And now you've read it." He sat back. "I wondered how long it would take. I wondered who it would be. I'd have said you, if you'd asked me. I've been in this work a long time, Chief Tsolo, and there's always one."

"Then you know."

"I know what I was sent to do." He picked up the coffee and drank some of it and put it down. "The Corva operation is finished. It's been finished for two years; it just hasn't been told. The Sorrel yards went over to catalysed helium last spring, and the price of deuterium in the outer lanes fell by two-thirds in a quarter, and there is no longer any way to bring Corva gas up the lane and sell it for more than it costs to get it out of the sky. So the Board has done what boards do. It has cut its losses. The ship is worth nothing; it goes into Gall. The crew are worth something, to the Levy; they go to Sorrel. The Camp is worth less than nothing. The Camp is a liability. So the Camp is left in place, and its accounts are sold to a discount house in Halloran for a fraction of a mark on the hundred, and the discount house will never send anybody to collect, because there will be nobody to collect from."

"Two thousand households."

"Twenty-one hundred."

"You're going to leave them to die."

"I'm going to leave them," said Quist, "to be somebody else's problem. That's the form of words. I didn't write it." He looked at her steadily. "I want you to understand that I'm not a stupid man, Chief Tsolo. Nor a cruel one, I think. I've done this before. I closed Bellamy Deep in eighty-four and the Hollow Moons in eighty-seven. Bellamy went quietly; most of them got out on the last ore boats, as stowaways, and I looked the other way. The Hollow Moons didn't go quietly, and it didn't help them. It's always worse when it isn't quiet."

"And Tamsin's Rock?" said Maren.

It was a guess, a name she had heard in the infirmary once, late, from Ekene, and never asked about. She saw it land. He did not flinch; he was too old and too practised to flinch. But he put the cup down very carefully, so that it made no sound on the saucer, and he took his hand away from it.

"I wasn't the factor at Tamsin's Rock," he said.

"But you know what happened there."

"Everybody in the Combine knows what happened there. That's what Tamsin's Rock is for." He looked at her for a long moment. "If you know what happened at Tamsin's Rock, then you know why I'm telling you the truth. Because you're going to do something, Chief Tsolo. I can see it in you. You're going to talk to your crew, and the crew are going to talk to the Camp, and somebody is going to have an idea. And I'd rather you did it knowing exactly what you're up against than not." He spread his hands on the desk. "The _Admonition_ is two weeks behind me. She has sixty marines and a torpedo bay. And I have contingency authority. Whatever you do, you can't make it end any better than it's going to end now. You can only make it worse."

"For who?"

"For everyone," said Quist. "For you. For them. For me." He paused. "Most of all for them."

Maren looked at him across Moberly's desk, at his plain coat and his good coffee and his long sorrowful face. She thought of Lusine's voice going faint on channel four. _I don't think it was the clip._

"Was it you?" she said. "Lusine. Did you have her line cut?"

For the first time Quist looked genuinely surprised.

"Her line was cut?"

"Her clip. Somebody took the spring two-thirds through."

He was quiet for a moment. Then he said, "No. It wasn't me. I didn't know her name until this morning." He looked down at the slate. "But I'd look closer to home, if I were you. I'd look at who on this ship would have wanted the Combine to be grateful to them. There's always somebody. There's always somebody who'd like to be on the right list." He looked up. "The offer stands, Chief. Supervisory. You and your brother. Think about it."

"I've thought about it," said Maren, and walked out.

* * *

She found Abel at the third bell of the night, in the blister.

The blister was a dome of thick old glass on the _Patience_'s dorsal hull, a relic of the days when skimmers had carried passengers who wanted to look at things. It was cold, and badly lit, and the crew used it for courting and drinking and the kind of conversations that couldn't be had anywhere else, and nobody ever cleaned it. Abel was sitting on the floor with his back against the frame and a bottle of dock-still between his knees, looking up. He didn't turn round when she came in. He held the bottle up over his shoulder.

She took it, and drank, and gave it back, and sat down beside him.

Above them, through the scratched glass, Gall filled half the sky, ochre and purple, a storm the size of a world turning slowly on its southern limb. Above the storm, in the black, the Lamp hung like a thumbprint of light. It was brighter than it had been three days ago. You could see the tail properly now, a long faint fan of pale green going away from the sun, so delicate that if you looked straight at it it disappeared, and came back when you looked to the side.

"Teska says it takes the dead out," said Abel.

"Teska says a lot of things."

"She says it'll be back when our grandchildren are dead." He drank. "That's a long time to be a comet."

"It's not a long time for a comet."

"No." He laughed a little. "I suppose not." He held the bottle up to the glass and looked at the Lamp through it, through the cloudy brown spirit, so that it wobbled. "I keep thinking about her. Lusine. Out there. Going in."

"So do I."

"You talked to her. The whole way. I heard you." He lowered the bottle. "That was a good thing you did, Mar."

"I didn't do anything."

"You did, though." He turned his head and looked at her for the first time. His face was grey and his eyes were wet and red-rimmed, and she could not tell how much of it was the still. "You always think you didn't do anything. You do more than anybody."

She looked at him for a long moment, at his handsome tired face in the Gall-light, her brother, whom she had carried on her hip through the third shaft when their mother went into the cloud.

"Were you seeing her, Abel?"

He didn't move. He didn't look away. But she had known him all his life, and she saw the small thing happen behind his eyes, the thing that happened when he was eight and she asked him who had got into the dried fruit, the half-second of arithmetic.

"Seeing her?"

"Lusine. Odile says she was seeing somebody. Somebody funny. Somebody always in debt who didn't care."

Abel laughed. It was almost his real laugh. "That's half the riggers on the ship."

"Were you?"

"We were friendly." He shrugged. "A bit. Now and then. She was—she was good fun, Mar. She liked a joke. We'd have a drink in the lock after shift. It wasn't serious. She wasn't serious about anybody." He drank. "Why?"

"Did she tell you anything? Before she died? About the _Clement_? About a dispatch?"

"A dispatch?" He frowned. "What dispatch?"

"Did she?"

"No." He shook his head. "No. Why would she tell me about a dispatch? I'm a rigger. I can barely read the duty board." He smiled at her, the old smile, _don't worry, it's me_. "What's this about?"

Maren looked at the Lamp.

"Nothing," she said. "It's late. I'm tired."

"You're always tired."

"I'm always tired."

He put his arm around her shoulders, the way he used to when he was sixteen and had just grown taller than her and wanted her to know it, and she let him, and they sat together in the cold blister and looked at the comet. His arm was warm and heavy and familiar. His hand, where it hung over her shoulder, was shaking, very slightly, with a small fine tremor, the kind of tremor that a man gets when he has been drinking for three days because he cannot stop seeing something.

She did not say anything about it. She sat there in her brother's arm and looked at the Lamp and thought, very clearly, _he's lying to me_, and then, because she was the tether-chief and that was what tether-chiefs did, she began to do the arithmetic.


# Chapter 5: The Blank Share

*The Siege · 2654*

On the eighteenth day of the siege the Ocampos' grandmother dies on the floor.

I'm the one who finds her. That's the way it falls out; I'm doing my rounds on the Ninth South with the new rates loaded in my ledger, and when I get to the eleventh door I knock and say _air-warden_ and nobody answers, and I knock again, and then I put my ear to the hatch the way the old wardens taught us, to listen for the household breathing, and I hear only the fans. So I open it with my key. We're allowed to. The Ordinance says a warden may enter any unshared household to read its meter, at any hour, without notice, and I have never once done it before.

The room is dark and very cold. The Ninth's rooms are all one room: a sleeping platform at the back, a bench and a heater and a basin at the front, a curtain between. The heater's off, because heat costs air. The curtain's open. On the sleeping platform, under three blankets and a coat, lies an old woman with a grey face and her mouth a little open, and beside her, sitting on the edge of the platform with her knees drawn up and her cracked grey gloves on, is Pilar.

"Abuela's asleep," she says.

I go and look. I'm not a medic, but you learn what you need to learn on the Ninth. The old woman's been dead some hours. Her hands are folded on the blanket, which means somebody folded them.

"Where's your mother, Pilar?"

"At the plant. They're doing double shifts. Because of the Concord." She looks at me. "Papa's at the plant too. Tomás and Juli are at the crèche on the Seventh. Abuela was sleeping. I was minding her."

"How long has she been sleeping?"

Pilar thinks about it seriously. "Since before the morning bell," she says. "She said she was tired. She said her chest was tight. She said it was like breathing through a sock." She looks back at her grandmother. "Then she stopped saying things."

I stand in the cold dark room with my ledger on my wrist. The meter by the door, under its frosted window, reads two hundred litres an hour for five people. That is the new floor. Two hundred for five. It came in on the first of the cycle, along with the doubled tithe, by Council order, and I loaded the new figures myself and turned the valves myself, every door on my section that was in arrears, which on the Ninth South was very nearly every door. I put the Ocampos to the new floor on the second. I remember the valve. It was stiff.

Nobody dies on the floor.

"She had the ice cough," I say. "Your grandmother. She'd had it for years. I heard it every time I came. Didn't she?"

"She coughed," Pilar agrees.

"It's the cough. When people get old, the cough gets into their chest, and their heart gets tired. It isn't anybody's fault." I hear my own voice, very even, very kind. The warden's voice. "I'm going to call the medics on the Seventh. They'll come and look after her. And I'm going to send a message to the plant, to your mother. All right?"

"All right," says Pilar.

I call the medics. I send the message. I file the death in my ledger, as the Ordinance requires, under _cause_, in the space provided: _Ice cough, pre-existing. Age._ My finger hovers over the line for a moment before I submit it, and then I submit it, because it's true. She did have the cough. She was old.

While I wait for the medics I sit on the bench by the dead heater, and Pilar sits on the platform beside her grandmother, and neither of us says anything. Through the ceiling, faintly, very far up, I can hear the surface being hit. It's a sound we've all learned to stop hearing, these eighteen days: a long dull boom like a door slammed in another house, every hour or so, as one of Grieve's slugs comes down out of the black and strikes the shell of Kettle somewhere over the city. He is careful, the Commodore. Everyone says so. He doesn't hit the tiers. He hits the refinery vents and the dipper docks and the old surface sheds where the fuel was stored, and the city's lungs on the far ice, the great intakes where the water is cracked for air. He is not trying to break us. He is trying to make us breathe less.

On the wall of the corridor outside, every tier now has a board, posted by the Council at the start of each shift. It says RESERVE, and then a number of days. When the siege began it said ninety-four. This morning it says sixty-one.

"Warden," says Pilar.

"Yes."

"When Mama pays, does it go straight back?"

"Yes."

"Even now?"

"Even now. The ledger tells the plant. She doesn't have to find me."

Pilar considers this. Then she gets down off the platform and goes to the hatch and looks at the meter under its frosted window, the needle lying on the red, and wipes the frost off the glass with her cracked glove, the way I do.

"It's very low," she says.

"It's the floor."

"Nobody dies on the floor," says Pilar, to the meter, quite seriously, as if she were reminding it.

* * *

In the afternoon I'm sent to the Spine.

The order comes down from the Wardens' Office with no explanation, which is how orders always come down: _Venn, I., to report Spine nave, air survey, priority._ When I get there I find out why. A slug in the night came down closer than any before it, on the old dock heads over the Spine, and the shock went down through the hull like a hammer down an anvil, and somewhere in the nave's air system something cracked. The nave's air is the Council's air. It's shared air, untithed, drawn straight from the city's best intakes and scrubbed three times on its way; it's also, like all old systems, a mess of pipes and baffles and valves that nobody has drawn a proper plan of in a century. Somebody has to walk it with a gauge and find the leak. The Council's own engineers are busy on the refinery vents. So they send a warden from the Ninth.

I walk it for three hours. I go up into the roof spaces above the nave on the old maintenance ladders, which were built for riggers two centuries ago and have been painted a dozen times since and never once mended, and crawl along the trunking with my gauge in one hand and my lamp in my teeth, listening. The nave below me is empty. Since the siege began the Council has met in the old bridge, which is deeper and safer, and the shared families have stopped coming up to sit on the benches under the Oculus and look at the Lamp. You can see it through the lens even from up here, sideways: a smear of greenish light much brighter now than it was at Lampfast, with a tail so long it crosses half the lens. The Lamp doesn't care about the Concord. It comes on in.

I find the leak in the end over the drum, where a flange on the main trunk has cracked across its face and is whistling out the Council's good air into the roof void. I can feel it on my cheek. I mark it with chalk and log it, and I'm climbing back down the ladder at the drum's end when the next slug comes.

It's close. Much closer than the one in the night. I feel it before I hear it, a shove from the ladder against my hands and my knees, as if the whole Spine had been kicked; then the sound, which isn't a boom at all this close but a crack, enormous, a sound like the sky being split down the middle; and then the nave rings. The old hull takes the blow and passes it down its whole length like a struck bell, and the ladder hums under me so hard my teeth buzz, and dust comes down out of the roof in a grey sheet, and somewhere below me something lets go.

I hang on. That's all you can do. When the ringing has gone down enough for me to hear myself think, I look down.

The Share Plate has fallen off the drum.

It must have been hanging on the same old fixings since the founding: four steel pins set into the drum's end, with the Plate's top corners hooked over two of them. The hull has rung and the pins have sheared. It's lying on the deck at the drum's foot, face down, in the dust, with one corner bent.

I climb the rest of the way down and stand looking at it. I don't touch it. Nobody touches the Plate. There are shared children who have been taken up to it on their naming days and allowed to lay one finger on the edge of their ancestor's name, and that is all; there are unshared families who have lived in this city for a hundred years and never been within ten metres of it. And now here it is at my feet, face down on the deck like a dropped tray.

The back is grey.

That's what I notice. I've never seen the back of it, nobody has. It's covered, edge to edge, in a coat of thick old grey paint, the kind of hull sealant they used to put on the outside of ships, gone hard and crazed with age, like the skin on old milk. Somebody painted the back of the Share Plate with ship's sealant, a long time ago, carefully, all over. And under the paint, where the light from the Oculus falls across it at a low angle, there are lines.

I crouch down. I still don't touch it. The lines are very faint, just a ridging in the sealant, the way the shape of a coin shows through a sheet of paper laid over it. Rows of them. Short marks, grouped. Something cut into the metal underneath and then painted over, so that only the ghost of the cutting comes through.

"Don't touch it," says a voice behind me.

It's Malachy. He's come up from the Archive in his slippers with his old grey coat thrown over his nightshirt, though it's the middle of the afternoon, and his white hair is full of dust, and he's out of breath. He comes across the nave to me at his slow crane's walk and stands beside me looking down at the Plate.

"I wasn't going to," I say.

"Good. Your hands are filthy." He crouches down beside me, which takes him some time, and puts his face close to the grey back of the Plate, and tilts his head to catch the light the way I did. He looks for a long time. He doesn't say anything. Then he lets out a long breath through his nose.

"Well," he says. "Well, well."

"Is that writing?"

"It's something."

There are footsteps on the nave floor, a lot of them. The Council's stewards, in their grey coats, four of them, with the First Shareholder walking behind. Lucan Strand looks tired. He has dust on his shoulders, too, and a cut over one eye with a dressing on it, and he moves like a man who has been awake for most of eighteen days; but his face when he sees the Plate on the floor goes very still, and then very gentle, the way a man's face goes when he sees a child hurt.

"Is it damaged?"

"One corner bent, First Shareholder," says Malachy, not getting up. "The fixings sheared. I'd like to look at it before it goes back up."

"It must go back up." Strand comes and stands over it. "Today. The people need to see it there. With what's happening—"

"It'll go back up," says Malachy, "on new pins, in a proper frame, when I've made sure that bend hasn't cracked the steel. If you hang it today on the old pins it'll be on the floor again in a week, and next time it might break." He looks up at the First Shareholder with his mild old eyes. "You wouldn't want to be the Shareholder who broke the Plate, Lucan."

Strand looks at him. I think for a moment that he's going to say no. I can see him weighing it: the steward at his elbow, the empty nave, the Lamp over his head in the Oculus, the slugs.

"How long?" he says.

"A week. Two at most."

"In the Archive."

"In the Archive, under lock, where it's safe. You can come and look at it any time you like."

Strand lets out a breath. "Very well. A week." He turns to the stewards. "Help the Archivist carry it. Gently." He looks down at me as if noticing me for the first time. "You found it?"

"I was surveying the air, First Shareholder. There's a cracked flange on the main trunk, over the drum. I've marked it."

"Thank you." He's already turning away. "Thank you, warden. Good work."

The stewards pick the Plate up between them, carefully, face down still, the grey back uppermost, and carry it toward the Archive stairs like a stretcher, with Malachy hovering behind. Nobody but me and Malachy looks at the back. The stewards are looking at their feet on the stairs. Strand is looking at the pins.

As Malachy goes past me, he puts his hand on my arm for a moment, very lightly.

"Come and see me tonight," he says. "After curfew. Bring your lamp."

* * *

I don't get to the Archive that night. At the eighth bell, as I'm coming off shift, the city's guns open up for the first time in the siege.

I didn't know we had guns. I don't think most people did. There are batteries, it turns out, in old pits on the surface ice around the city, built a hundred years ago in some war scare that never came to anything and maintained ever since by a few dozen shared families who have the right to do it the way other shared families have the right to the dipper fleet. They've waited eighteen days. Now, all at once, they fire, and the whole city hears them: a sound like heavy doors being slammed, very fast, one after another, coming down through the ice.

Everybody stops in the corridors and looks up.

The word comes down the speakers a few minutes later, crackling and excited. A Concord lander has come down low over the south ice, probing, the first time any of Grieve's ships has come close enough to touch, and the batteries have hit it. It's down. It's on the ice south of the city, a kilometre from South Lock. All damage-control parties to South Lock. All air-wardens with surface rating to South Lock.

I have surface rating. All wardens do; we read the meters on the intakes. I go.

South Lock is a vast old chamber cut in the ice at the bottom of a sloping ramp, with the surface doors at its top end and a long low gallery down one side where the suits hang on their racks like a row of hanged men. When I get there it's already full of people. Damage-control crews in orange. Wardens in grey. Militia in their blue armbands with their old rifles. And riggers, a lot of riggers, the big tattooed men and women of the dipper fleet, who've been grounded eighteen days by the Council's order and have nothing to do and are in the mood for something to do it to.

The doors are open when I get there. The lock's cycled. A team in surface suits has gone out across the ice on a crawler and is coming back, and on the big screen over the gallery we can all see the feed from the crawler's lamps: white ice, black sky, Gall's banded edge on the horizon, and a long smear of wreckage going away across the snow, burning green and orange where its fuel has caught. In the middle of the wreckage, something moving.

"There's one alive," someone says.

"Not for long."

The crawler comes back down the ramp with the outer doors grinding shut behind it, ice streaming off its tracks. The lock cycles. When the inner doors open, the crowd surges forward, and I'm pushed forward with it, and I see the damage-control crew climbing down off the crawler's bed with a body between them in a scorched white Concord pressure suit.

They put it down on the floor of the lock. The suit's helmet is cracked across the visor. There's frost on the inside of the crack. The figure inside it moves its hands, weakly, at the clasps of the helmet, as if it wants to get it off.

"Leave it," says somebody. "It's on its own air."

"Its air's gone," says one of the damage-control crew, a woman with a burned face, crouching by the helmet. "Tank's holed. It's breathing what's left in the suit." She looks up. "Maybe ten minutes."

"Then leave it ten minutes," says a rigger at the front of the crowd, a huge man with comet-tails tattooed up both forearms, and there's a growl of agreement behind him. "Let it breathe what's left. That's what they're doing to us."

"Put it out on the ice."

"Let it have the floor."

That gets a laugh, an ugly one. It goes round the lock like a wave. _Let it have the floor._ The Concord pilot's hands have stopped moving at the clasps. They lie on its chest. Through the cracked visor I can see a face, very young, very pale, with its eyes open, looking up at all of us.

I'm not brave. I want to say that clearly, as I said the other thing clearly before. What I do next isn't brave. It's habit. I'm an air-warden. I have spent twelve years kneeling beside meters, and what I'm looking at is a meter reading red.

I push through to the front, and kneel down beside the burned-faced woman, and take the spare bottle off my own belt. Every warden carries a spare, a little twenty-minute flask with a universal coupling, for when we're out on the intakes. I find the suit's emergency port under the left armpit, where every suit in eleven lanes has had it since before the founding, and I knock the frost off it with my glove and screw the coupling in, and open the valve.

The suit hisses. The frost on the inside of the visor starts, very slowly, to clear.

"What are you doing?" says the tattooed rigger.

"Reading a meter," I say.

There is a silence in the lock. I can feel it on the back of my neck, a hundred people looking at me. I don't look round. I watch the frost clear on the visor, and the young pale face behind it blink, and its lips move.

"Warden," says the rigger, in a voice I don't like.

"It's my air," I say. "It's on my account. If you want to take it off me, you'll have to take it off me."

Nobody moves. I don't know what would have happened next. I don't know whether they would have; I think perhaps they would. But the militia have got to the front by then, three of them with rifles and blue armbands and an officer with a whistle, and the officer is shouting that the prisoner is the property of the Council and anybody who lays a hand on him will be on the floor for a year. The crowd backs off, growling. The burned-faced woman helps me get the cracked helmet off. Underneath it the pilot is a man, young, younger than me, with a soldier's cropped fair hair and a soldier's frightened eyes, and a smear of blood under his nose.

He looks at me. He breathes. It's the first thing I ever hear him do.

"Thank you," he says, in the clipped accent of the inner lanes.

"Don't," I say. "It's on your account."

The militia take him. They lift him on to a stretcher and carry him up the ramp toward the inner city, and the officer with the whistle tells me to report to the Wardens' Office in the morning with my bottle receipt, and goes after them. The crowd breaks up, slowly, sullenly, back to whatever it was doing. The tattooed rigger stands looking at me a while longer and then goes. I'm left kneeling on the wet ice floor of the lock with my empty bottle in my hand.

Somebody crouches down beside me. A woman, a rigger, forty-odd, small and wiry, with her sleeves rolled up over forearms tattooed from wrist to elbow with the long thin fans of comets, one inside another, like the rings in a tree.

"You gave him your air, warden," she says.

"I gave him a bottle."

"That's a first." She looks at me with a sort of amused curiosity, as if I were a dog that had started to talk. "Twelve years I've lived on the Ninth North and I never once saw a warden give anybody air."

"It's not a warden's job to give air."

"No," she agrees. "It's not." She stands up and dusts off her knees. "I'm Dasha. Dasha Imre. Rigger. Grounded." She holds out her hand to pull me up. "You want to be careful, warden. Giving people things. It gets to be a habit."

I let her pull me up. "Isaura," I say. "Venn."

"I know who you are," says Dasha Imre. "Everybody on the Ninth knows who you are." She grins, quite friendly. "That's why it's a first."

When I get back to my room on the Fourth it's past the tenth bell, and there's a message waiting on my wrist. It's from the Archive. It's very short, in Malachy's old-fashioned way, the way he used to write notes in the margins of my exercises when I was ten.

_Isaura. There's writing under the paint. Come tomorrow. Come alone. M._


# Chapter 6: The Throat

*The Last Return · 2917*

A lane is not a road. People who have never been through one think it is, and draw it on their charts as a line between two dots, and talk about _going down the Corva lane_ as if it were a corridor with a door at each end. Til has been through forty-one lanes in her life, some of them many times, and she has stopped trying to say what they are. The nearest she has come is this: a lane is a place where the dark has been folded, and a ship in it is a crease.

An anchored lane is a good crease. Somebody, a long time ago, built a station at each end of it that holds the fold flat, and as long as the anchors hold, a ship goes in at one end and comes out at the other with nothing worse than a headache and a strange taste in the mouth, like licking a battery. An unanchored lane is called raw. The fold moves. It ripples like a flag in a wind. Nobody knows what the wind is.

The Corva lane has been raw for longer than anybody alive has been alive, and for most of that time it was closed. Two years ago it opened again, the way a healed bone sometimes aches and opens, and nobody knows why that either.

"Shear coming," says Oz, from the engine space. "Big one. Twelve seconds."

"I see it."

"You don't see it, you feel it. Hold on to something."

Til holds on to the yoke. In the forward seat beside her Juno is strapped in tight, her new suit sealed and her helmet clipped to her shoulder, and she is holding on to both armrests with both gloved hands and staring straight ahead at the cockpit glass, where there is nothing to see. In a lane there is never anything to see. The glass shows a dim grey flicker, like the inside of an eyelid, and occasionally a long slow ripple of brighter grey crossing it from one side to the other, which is the fold moving, and which you learn very fast not to look at.

The shear hits.

It's like being kicked by the whole universe at once. The _Dray_ doesn't shake so much as change shape: for one long, sick, impossible moment Til can feel the cockpit getting longer, the yoke in her hands drawing out away from her like taffy, her own arms stretching to follow it; and then it snaps back, and every rivet in the old tug's hull screams at once, a high thin chorus like a choir of kettles, and something aft goes bang.

"Coolant," says Oz, quite calmly, on the intercom. "Number two loop. That was the coupling."

"How bad?"

"Bad enough that I'd like some help, please, Captain."

Til looks at the board. The core temperature is climbing, not fast but steadily, a line going up at the angle of a cheap staircase. She can't leave the yoke. In a raw lane you never leave the yoke; the autopilot doesn't know what the fold is going to do any more than you do, and it doesn't have the sense to be frightened.

"Juno," she says.

Juno doesn't move.

"Juno. I need you to go aft and help Oz."

"I'm not crew." Juno's voice is very small and very flat. She's still staring at the glass. "Mother Szabo said—"

"Mother Szabo isn't in a raw lane with a coolant leak. Mother Szabo is a day behind us in a much bigger ship with a much better engineer. If the core goes, it goes, and you go with it, and then she'll bill me." Til doesn't look at her. She keeps her eyes on the board and her hands on the yoke and her voice exactly the way it would be if she were asking for a cup of tea. "Unstrap. Clip your helmet on. Go aft along the rail, hand over hand, don't let go of the rail for anything. Oz will tell you what to do. He's very good at telling people what to do."

"I am," says Oz on the intercom.

Juno sits for another two seconds. Then she unstraps.

Til doesn't watch her go. She hears her: the click of the harness, the clunk of the helmet seal, the scrape of boots on the deck, the hand-over-hand rattle of the rail. She hears the aft hatch cycle. She keeps her eyes on the board and her hands on the yoke and flies the _Dray_ through the dark that isn't dark, feeling for the fold the way you feel for a step in a black stairwell, and she listens to the intercom.

"There," Oz is saying. "That clamp. No, the big one. Get both hands on it. Now push. Harder than that. Pretend it's me."

A grunt.

"Good. Hold it there. Don't let go of it, whatever happens."

"It's hot."

"It's very hot. Your gloves are rated. Hold it. I'm going to put a sleeve on the coupling and it's going to take me forty seconds, and if you let go of that clamp in the next forty seconds the loop'll blow out past my hands and the core'll scram and we'll be sitting dead in a raw lane, so don't."

"I won't."

"Shear coming," says Til. "Eight seconds."

"Wonderful," says Oz.

"Hold on to the clamp, Juno."

"I'm _holding on to the clamp_."

The second shear is worse than the first. The cockpit stretches and snaps and the kettles scream and the board flickers, and Til, flying, feels the fold go under the _Dray_ like a wave under a small boat, a long sickening rise and fall, and holds the yoke against it and does not breathe. Aft, on the intercom, she hears Juno shout something wordless and furious, and Oz swearing in the Roundsman cant, a long liquid stream of it, and then silence.

"Oz."

Nothing.

"Oz."

"Sleeve's on," says Oz. He sounds out of breath. "Loop's holding. She held the clamp."

"Juno?"

A pause. Then Juno's voice, shaking, very young. "I held the clamp."

"Good," says Til. "That was good."

She keeps her eyes on the board. She doesn't let her voice change. She flies the _Dray_ on through the shifting dark for another nine hours, through four more shears, none of them as bad, and she never once takes her hands off the yoke. And at some point in the fifth hour, without either of them saying anything about it, Juno comes forward again and straps into the second seat and stays there, and when Til asks her to read off the core temperature every ten minutes, she does.

* * *

They come out of the Corva lane on the twelfth of April at the fourth bell, ship's time, into a silence so complete that for a long moment Til thinks the board has failed.

The board hasn't failed. It's showing her exactly what's there. The glass has gone from grey to black, real black, star-black, with real stars in it, a great spilled field of them; and ahead and a little below, so close and so huge that her eye refuses it at first as a planet and reads it as a wall, there's Gall.

She has seen gas giants before. Every salvager has; the wreck-fields of the outer Reach are full of them, and the dead hulks always fall toward the big ones in the end. She has never seen one like this. Gall is close, the lane mouth hanging in the dark only a few hundred thousand kilometres off its trailing edge, and it fills a third of the sky: a vast striped curve of ochre and rust and old-bruise purple, banded like the grain in a slab of wood, with storms in the bands the size of worlds, turning. At its southern limb there's a white cap. Across its face, very small and very sharp, a moon throws a round black shadow. And above it, high on the left, laid across a quarter of the sky like a scarf thrown on a floor, is the Lamp.

It's enormous. That's what nobody tells you. In the Observatory's pictures it was a bright head and a long dim tail; out here it's a smear of pale green light as wide as Til's hand at arm's length, with a head that hurts to look at and a tail that goes on and on and on, splitting into two, three, four fainter streamers, fanning out across the stars until it's lost in them. It's so bright Gall's night side is faintly lit by it.

Nobody in the cockpit says anything for a while.

"Well," says Oz eventually, from the hatch. "There it is."

Til runs the board. She does it automatically, the way she would coming out of any lane: traffic, beacons, anchor station, comms. There's no traffic. There are no beacons. There's no anchor station where the anchor station should be, at the lane's mouth, only a faint scatter of radar returns drifting in a loose cloud, metal-sized, like the debris from something that once was there and isn't any more. Whatever held this throat open has been gone a long time. Comms are open on every band she's got, and every band is empty: hiss, and the low growling roar of Gall's radio belts, and under that nothing at all. No traffic control. No nav pings. No voices.

"Nobody home," says Juno quietly.

"Lanes go dark for a reason," says Til. "Usually it's because nobody's left to keep them lit."

"It was a city once," says Oz. "Kettle. My mother said. The ice moon. There was a city under the ice."

"Was or is?"

"She didn't know. Nobody's been through in two hundred years and more. Nobody's said a word on the radio."

"Somebody'd say something," says Juno. "If they were there. Wouldn't they? After two hundred years. If a ship came."

Nobody answers her.

The cradle is in the main cabin, aft of the cockpit, where there's room for it, clamped to the deck by its sled and plugged into the _Dray_'s power with a cable Oz rigged out of three adaptors and a prayer. When Til goes back, Hollis Vey is propped up on his pillows with the cradle's canopy slid back and his face turned to the cabin's only port. Cato Marchetti is sitting beside the cradle on a crate, very still, as he always is, with his hands on his knees.

Vey's face is wet.

He doesn't wipe it. He doesn't seem to know it's wet. He's looking at Gall, at the bruised bands and the white cap and the moon's small shadow crossing, and at the Lamp above it, and his face is a long thin yellowish wreck of a face with the tears running down it into the pillow, and he looks happier than Til has seen anybody look in years.

"Captain," he says. "Look at it."

"I've looked."

"Five hundred years," says Hollis Vey. "My family owned this. All of it. That planet. That moon. That sky." The thin voice through the speaker grille is shaking with something like laughter. "Do you know, we never came? Not one of us. Not one Vey ever came to Corva. We owned it for sixty years and sent factors and took the money and never once came to look. And then we lost it, and spent five hundred years wondering how." He turns his head on the pillow. "I'm the first Vey ever to see it. And the last. That's rather funny, isn't it?"

"I wouldn't know, Mr Vey."

"No. You wouldn't." He smiles at her with his perfect too-large teeth. "Sit down, Captain. I promised I'd tell you my business, when I could bear to. I can bear a little of it now."

Til sits on the edge of the bunk across from him. Marchetti doesn't move. Through the hatch she can hear Juno and Oz in the cockpit talking in low voices; she can hear the _Dray_'s air plant coughing, the way it always coughs after a lane.

"My family's company was called Halloran-Vey," says Hollis. "It was a combine of the old sort, in the old lanes, before the Concord swallowed it and before the Concord choked on it in its turn. It dug things out of places and sold them. Gas from giants. Ice from moons. Labour from people." He says it lightly. "It held this system for sixty years. It ran a skimmer that dipped Gall for deuterium, and a camp of bonded workers on Kettle to refine what the skimmer brought up. And then, in twenty-three ninety-one, it lost it."

"Lost it how?"

"That," says Hollis, "is the question I've spent my life on. The records are very bad. The Combine had a habit, when something went wrong, of writing as little about it as possible. There's a register—a loss register, the Combine kept them for every operation—that says Corva was lost in that year, and that certain assets were not recovered. And then nothing. Two hundred and sixty years later the Concord came here to take it back, and the records of that are worse."

"Took it back from who?"

"From whoever was here." He shrugs, a movement of one bony shoulder under the blanket. "The Concord's archives are a midden. Half of them burned when it fell. I have spent a great deal of money buying the other half off the people who looted them, and they tell me almost nothing. That a fleet was sent. Whether it took anything back, or came back at all, they don't say." He looks back at the port, at the Lamp. "And then, in the dock-houses of Tarn, this story. That the founders of Corva hid something on their comet. That it comes back."

"The Hoard."

"The Hoard." He smiles. "You don't believe in it."

"I've bought two maps to it."

"So have I," says Hollis Vey. "Eleven. All different. All forged." He closes his eyes for a moment, as if the talking has tired him. "But my family lost something here, Captain. Something the register calls an asset not recovered. And the Lamp was in the sky the year they lost it. And it has come back only once since, and the Concord's fleet went to Corva that same year. And now it is coming back for the last time, because Gall is going to eat it." He opens his eyes. "If you were me, and had three months to live, where would you look?"

Til doesn't answer. Beside her, very quietly, Cato Marchetti shifts his weight on the crate, the first movement she has seen him make.

"What's the asset?" Til says. "The one not recovered."

"The register doesn't say," says Hollis. "It gives it a number. That's all."

He's lying. Til knows it the way she knows when a hull plate is about to go: by a change in the sound of things. It's not in his face, which is as open and amused and wrecked as before. It's in Marchetti, who has gone still again, still in a different way, the stillness of a man who has just heard his employer say something he was told not to say and is waiting to see whether anybody noticed.

"All right," says Til, and gets up. "Get some rest, Mr Vey. We'll be at Gall in six days."

* * *

On the second night out from the Throat, Til has the watch from the eighth bell to the twelfth, and Juno comes up at the ninth and sits in the second seat without being asked.

She doesn't say anything. She's in her undersuit and a borrowed jumper of Oz's that comes down to her knees, with her knees drawn up inside it and her arms round them, and she sits in the dark cockpit looking out at the Lamp. It's on their left now, high and enormous, and Gall is ahead and below, filling the lower glass with its slow bands. The _Dray_'s drive is running at a quarter, a low steady rumble aft. On the board, nothing moves.

Til does the things you do on watch. She checks the core. She checks the air. She checks the course, which is a long falling curve toward Gall that will take them past the giant and use its gravity to swing them out to meet the Lamp's path; Oz's idea, an old dipper's trick he says his mother told him about. She checks the rear scope. Nothing behind them yet. She checks the comms. Hiss and the roar of the belts.

After a long time, Juno says, "Szabo says you cut her line."

Til doesn't look round.

"On the Harrow," Juno says. "My mother's line. She says when the hulk spun up and started dragging you both, you had a choice, and you cut her line to save yourself. She says that's why you went to the prison, after. For the job being illegal and for that." Her voice is very level. "She says everybody on the Ring knows it."

"Everybody on the Ring knows a lot of things."

"Is it true?"

The Lamp hangs in the glass. Til looks at it.

There are things she has said to herself about this moment, over eleven years. She has had a lot of time to say them: four years of it in the Directorate's yard at Cold Harbour, and seven since on the _Dray_, alone mostly, on long hauls. She has rehearsed what she would say if Juno ever asked. She had it worked out, at one point, very carefully. She can't remember any of it now.

"There was a hulk," she says. "A big one. A Directorate ore carrier, dead forty years, out on the edge of the Harrow, where nobody's allowed to go. Your mother found it. She said there was a fortune in its tanks, and there was, if we could get it. It was a bad job. We knew it was a bad job. We went anyway, because we owed money, and because your mother never in her life saw a bad job she didn't want to go and look at." She stops. "We got a line on to it. Both of us, on one line. And the hulk spun up. Something in its old stabilisers woke up, and it spun, and the line went round it, and it started winding us in."

Juno says nothing.

"There was a knife," Til says. "On the line. There's always a knife on the line. Whoever's nearest the hulk can cut. Your mother was nearest the hulk." She takes a breath. "The line got cut. I came off and she didn't. The hulk took her in. I was picked up by a Directorate patrol eleven hours later with my air on red and they charged me with unlicensed salvage and I did four years in Cold Harbour." She looks at the board. "And Szabo bought your mother's debts off the Harrow brokers and bought you with them, and you were six."

"Who cut the line?"

"The line got cut, Juno."

"Who cut it?"

Til is silent.

"You won't say," says Juno.

"I've said what I've got to say."

"You've said _the line got cut_." Juno's voice is shaking now, just slightly, just at the edge. "You've said it like it was weather. Like it just happened. Like nobody did it." She uncurls her legs and puts her feet on the deck. "I was six. I waited for her at the dock for two days. Mother Szabo came and got me and said she'd gone round and she wasn't coming back, and said my aunt had cut the line. And then you came out of the prison and you never once came to see me. Not once. In seven years. You'd walk past the counting-house window. I saw you. I used to watch for you."

"I know."

"Then why?"

Til looks at her at last. In the dark of the cockpit, lit only by the board and the Lamp, Juno's face is her mother's face exactly, broad and stubborn and furious, with the straight black brows drawn down.

"Because you'd have asked me that," Til says. "And I'd have said what I just said."

Juno stares at her. Then she gets up, and goes out of the cockpit, and down the passage to the warm bunk aft of the galley, and Til hears the bunk curtain rattle shut.

She sits in the dark with the board.

At the eleventh bell the rear scope pings. Til looks. Far behind them, back at the Throat, where the lane's mouth is a faint distortion in the stars, something has come out of the fold: a long black hull with a ram bow, running dark, decelerating hard. She doesn't need the transponder. She knows the shape.

"Hello, Mother," she says to the glass.

She logs it, and turns back to the forward board, and that's when she sees the other thing.

It's very small. It's almost nothing. A single pulse on the radar warning, a bright green flick in the corner of the board, there and gone in half a second: somebody, somewhere ahead, has painted the _Dray_ with a ranging radar. One sweep. Then nothing. The warning light goes back to dark.

Til sits very still.

She runs the bearing. It's ahead and a little below, toward Gall. Toward the small white moon whose shadow she has been watching cross the giant's face for two days: a cracked white ball, very bright against the bands, that Oz's mother called Kettle.

She watches the board for the rest of her watch. The pulse doesn't come again. Comms stay empty, hiss and the roar of the belts. Nobody calls. Nobody says a word.

It could be automatic. Old systems run for a long time. A radar left on in some dead station under the ice, sweeping the sky for two centuries out of habit, the way a dead man's heart sometimes twitches on a slab.

When Oz comes up at the twelfth bell to relieve her, she tells him about the _Carrion Hymn_. She doesn't tell him about the pulse. She isn't sure why. She lies in her bunk afterward with her eyes open in the dark and thinks about it: one sweep, from a moon where nobody has said a word in two hundred years. One look.

As if somebody down there had raised their head, and seen them, and put it down again.


# Chapter 7: Sanitation

*The Bonded · 2391*

Under the drum there was a space nobody had ever found a use for.

The drum sat in a cradle of girders that went down through three decks to the keel, and between the girders, under the drum's great curved belly, the shipwrights had left a hollow the size of a chapel: a ribbed, dark, echoing void full of cable runs and bearing housings and old grease, where the noise of the drum when it was turning was so loud you could not hear your own voice, and when it was not turning, so quiet you could hear the hull tick. The crew called it the Belly. You got into it by a maintenance hatch behind the number four brake, and a ladder, and a long crawl on your hands and knees along a cable tray. Riggers went there to sleep off shifts and to do things they did not want logged. Wardens did not go there, because wardens were big men and the cable tray was narrow.

Maren called the meeting for the fourth bell of the night, in the dead hour between dips, when the drum was still.

Eleven of them came. She had asked for twelve. They sat on the girders and the bearing housings in a rough ring, with one lamp turned low on the deck plates between them, and their faces came and went in its light: Teska, wrapped in two coats, with her white eye shining; Odile, very upright, very quiet; Danek Sorell, too big for the space, folded up on a girder with his knees under his chin; old Pirro from the Bucket crew; two dock hands, a married pair called Senna and Ivo, who between them knew every valve on the forward collar; three riggers Maren trusted and two she trusted less, because you needed the ones who talked as well as the ones who didn't. Ekene was not there. She had not asked him. A free man could be hanged for what the bonded only had their accounts reviewed for.

Abel was not there either. She had asked him. He had said yes, and smiled, and not come.

"Read it," said Teska. "Read the paper. I want to hear it in my own ears."

Maren read it. She read it in the tether-chief's voice, without any expression at all, every line, the routing codes and the board resolution and the numbers, _est. 300_, _est. 2,100 households_, _supply to cease_. When she had finished nobody said anything for a long time. Somewhere in the hull below them a pump came on and ran and went off again.

"Sorrel," said Danek at last, in the same voice he had used in the hall. "The forts."

"The forts."

"And the Camp."

"Three months. Four if they're careful."

"Tamar's got the cough already. She had it last winter." Danek looked at his own big hands. "Four months she won't make."

"Nobody'll make it," said Pirro. "Not the old ones. Not the little ones. The plant'll go first, then the heat. You'll have people burning their bunks." He spat into the dark. "I was in the Camp when the third shaft caved in sixty-eight. We were nine days without the plant. I saw what nine days did."

"So we don't go," said Senna, from the dock. "We refuse. Strike. They can't move three hundred people on to the _Clement_ if three hundred people won't walk."

"They can if they've got sticks."

"They've got six marines and a dozen trusties. We're three hundred."

"And the _Admonition_," said Maren. "Two weeks behind the factor. Sixty marines and a torpedo bay. Due on the twenty-second." She looked round the ring. "And then they don't need us to walk."

"Then we take the ship," said Teska.

They all looked at her. She was sitting hunched on her bearing housing in her two coats, very small, with her good eye glittering in the lamp.

"We take her," she said. "She's ours. We built her. Well, they built her, but we've had her sixty years; we've kept her flying when they wouldn't send the parts. Every bolt in her's been turned by one of us. We take her and we don't sink her. We keep dipping. We keep the Camp breathing." She shrugged inside the coats. "Let the Combine come and get her."

"The Combine will," said Maren.

"Then we'll be dead in a month instead of four. I'm eighty-three. I can spare a month."

"Danek's children can't."

"Danek's children," said Teska, with great gentleness, "are going to die on the ice anyway, Maren. That paper says so. All you're doing is choosing which way."

Nobody said anything to that.

Maren had known it was coming. She had known since the night in the Shelf, with the foil in her hand and Odile's face in front of her, that it would come to this: a ring of tired people in the dark under the drum, with nothing in front of them but a wall, and somebody saying the thing out loud. She had spent four days running the arithmetic, in the gallery, at the board, in Ekene's bunk with her eyes open. It came out the same every time. Strike, and they would be broken. Comply, and the Camp would die. Fight, and the _Admonition_ would come, and then the Camp would die and so would they.

"If we take her," she said, "we have to take the _Clement_ too. Same night. Same hour. Otherwise the factor gets on her and casts off and he's two weeks from the _Admonition_ and he tells them everything. And the _Clement_'s got the marines, and the marines have guns."

"How many?"

"Six. Sergeant called Oyelowo. They're good. They're not like the trusties. They've done this before."

"We'll need sticks," said a voice from the dark at the edge of the ring. "At least."

The lamp didn't reach where it came from. It didn't need to. Everybody knew that voice, soft and patient and reasonable, and the ring went still the way a rigger goes still on the line when the trace jumps.

Corwin Hale came forward into the light at his slow walk, ducking under a girder. Behind him, in the dark, Maren could see the shape of Bram Kovač crouched at the end of the cable tray, blocking it.

"I'm sorry to interrupt," said Hale. "I didn't mean to listen. Sound carries in here. It's a terrible place for a secret."

"How did you get in?" said Odile. "The tray's too narrow for you."

"There's a hatch from the keel side, if you know where it is." Hale smiled faintly. "I've been on this ship twenty years, Arda. I know where everything is." He looked round the ring. His eyes rested on each of them in turn, mild and unhurried, as if he were taking a register: Teska, Danek, Pirro, Senna, Ivo, the riggers. Last of all he looked at Maren. "May I sit down?"

Nobody answered. He sat anyway, on the end of Pirro's girder, so that the old man had to shift up.

"You've read the dispatch," he said. "I know you have. Arda's sister had it in her boot. I've been waiting for you to do something about it." He folded his big hands on his knee. "I'm on the list too, you know. I'm bonded. I've got an account. My mother's in the Camp, on the second shaft, and she's sixty-eight, and she doesn't have four months either." He looked at Danek. "Trusties go to Sorrel with everybody else, Sorell. They don't need wardens on the forts. They've got officers for that. I'll be filling sandbags beside you."

"You'll be filling sandbags," said Teska, "when Gall freezes."

"Grandam," said Hale, "I know what you think of me. I know what you all think of me. You think I'm the Combine's dog. You're right. I've been the Combine's dog for twenty years, because the Combine fed me." He opened his hands. "The Combine's stopped feeding me. That's all. I'd like to eat."

"What are you offering?" said Maren.

"Twelve trusties with sticks. The keys to the bridge and the armoury, such as it is. Two old flechette carbines that the Combine forgot it issued. And the warden's office, which means the logs, which means that whatever happens, the Combine won't hear about it from this ship until we want it to." He met her eyes. "And me. Who knows where everything is."

Maren looked at him. She thought of Lusine's voice on channel four. _I told someone._ She thought of Quist across Moberly's desk, turning his coffee cup: _I'd look at who on this ship would have wanted the Combine to be grateful to them._ She thought of the form on Hale's slate, already filled in, in his neat hand, before she ever came down the gallery ladder.

She thought of six marines with guns.

"What's Schedule H?" she said.

Hale blinked. "I'm sorry?"

"In the dispatch. Paragraph five. _Contingency authority, Schedule H, confirmed._ What's Schedule H?"

"I've no idea," said Hale. "Factors' business. I'm a warden." He shrugged. "Contingency means _if things go wrong_. I expect it means the _Admonition_."

He said it easily. Maren watched him say it. She could not tell. She had been watching Hale for twenty years, and she had never once been able to tell what was behind that mild patient face, and she could not tell now.

"We'll think about your offer," she said.

"Don't think too long," said Hale, getting up. "The twenty-second isn't far." He looked round the ring once more, taking his register. "And none of you need worry about me. I can keep a secret. I've kept a great many." He ducked back under the girder into the dark, and they heard him go, slow and heavy, toward his hatch in the keel; and after a moment Bram Kovač's shape at the end of the cable tray went too.

The ring sat in silence.

"Well," said Pirro. "Now we have to."

* * *

The _Clement_'s crew were not bonded. They were Combine contract hands, hired for the run at Halloran, and they kept to their own ship mostly, and looked at the _Patience_'s bonded the way people look at animals in a pen: without malice, without much interest. But the _Clement_'s medic had been left at Halloran with a fever, and so when one of her cargo hands got his left hand caught in the closing door of a cold-hold hatch, on the morning of the eleventh, they brought him across the collar to Ekene.

Maren heard about it from Ekene that evening. She came to the infirmary after the second dip and found him at the counter, not writing, sitting with his reading lenses in his hand and his face the colour of the counter.

"His name's Brandt," Ekene said. "Yusuf Brandt. He's twenty-three. He's from Halloran, from the yards. He's never been out of the inner lanes before. He thinks Corva is the most boring place he has ever been in his life." He turned the lenses over. "I had to take two fingers. The hatch was a cryo-hatch, the heavy kind, with the frost seals. It crushed them flat. So I gave him a lot of the good painkiller, which the Combine charges at nine marks a dose, and while I was working, he talked."

"What about?"

"About his ship. About the cold hold. About how he's on cold-hold duty this run, which means checking the cryostats every four hours, and how it's the worst duty on the ship because the hold's at sixty below and the gloves are useless. About how there's nothing in the cold hold this run except the usual medical stocks and one other thing." Ekene put the lenses down on the counter, very carefully. "About how the factor comes down to look at the other thing every day. Every single day. With the sergeant. And stands and looks at it. And how the hands call it the egg."

Maren said nothing.

"I asked him what kind of egg," said Ekene. "He laughed. He said a grey one, about this long"—he held his hands apart, the length of a forearm and a half—"in its own cryostat, with its own power and its own alarm, and a stencil on the casing that he'd read so many times on cold-hold duty he could say it in his sleep. And then he said it for me. In his sleep, nearly. The painkiller was very good." Ekene looked at her. "_Sanitation Agent. Pattern H. Factor's authority only._"

"Pattern H."

"Schedule H. Pattern H." His voice was steady and quite empty. "I've seen that stencil before, Maren. I didn't think I'd ever see it again. I think I came to the end of the lanes so that I wouldn't."

"Tamsin's Rock," said Maren.

He closed his eyes.

"Tell me," she said.

"Not here." He opened them again and looked toward the curtained bay where Yusuf Brandt was asleep, two fingers lighter, dreaming his painkiller dreams of Halloran. "Not tonight. I need to know you've seen it first. I need to know I'm not a frightened old man hearing things in a boy's babbling." He put out his hand and took hers on the counter, hard, the way he never did, his long gentle fingers cold as the cold hold. "If it's there, Maren, then everything you've been planning in the Belly is something else. Everything. Do you understand? You can't take a ship with that thing on the other end of the collar. You can't do anything."

"I can look at it," said Maren.

* * *

The _Clement_ was mated to the _Patience_ at the forward collar, a great ring of armoured coupling with its own lock and its own guard, and on the _Clement_'s side of the collar there was a marine on duty all day and all night. There was no going through the collar.

There was going round it.

"It's eleven metres," said Odile, in the suit lock on the forward hull, checking Maren's harness for the third time. "Outside. Along the collar fairing, then down the _Clement_'s spine to her number two maintenance hatch, which opens from outside, because they always do, in case somebody's stuck in a hold. Senna says the hatch is never alarmed. Senna says nobody on the _Clement_ thinks anybody's mad enough to go outside."

"Senna's never been outside."

"Senna's never been mad." Odile snapped the last clip and pulled on it. "We're runners, Chief. Eleven metres is nothing. Eleven metres is the length of the drum hall."

"Eleven metres with Gall underneath it."

"Gall's always underneath it." Odile looked at her through her visor, her young fierce face very pale in the lock lights. "You were a runner. You told me. You did four years at twenty kilometres. Before Hesketh made you chief."

"I was younger."

"You were braver," said Odile, and opened the outer door.

It was the second bell of the night shift, the dead time, and the hull was dark. They went out on to it on their hands and knees, clipped to a running line that Odile had rigged the night before, along the dorsal fairing of the collar, where the two ships' hulls met in a seam of scarred armour. Above them, to the left, the black was full of stars and the Lamp, which had grown again, a long pale streamer of light now with its tail split in two. Below them, to the right, was Gall.

Maren had not been outside the _Patience_ in nine years. She had forgotten. You forgot, on the gallery, with the board in front of you and the line a green trace on a screen. You forgot that the line was real, that it hung down out of the keel slot into that, into the endless ochre floor of the world, banded and boiling and so close it seemed you could reach down and put your hand in it. You forgot the size of it. Lusine had fallen into it from eleven kilometres. From here, Maren thought, holding the running line with both hands and not looking down, from here you would fall for a long time.

"Don't look at it, Chief," said Odile on the suit channel. "Look at your hands."

"I know."

"I know you know."

They went along the collar fairing and down the _Clement_'s long dull spine, hand over hand, past the green wheel of the Combine painted twenty metres high on her flank and lit from below by Gall-shine, so that it looked like a rusted sun. The number two maintenance hatch was where Senna had said it would be, a square of grey armour set flush in the hull with a recessed handle and a stencil that said NO ENTRY EXCEPT IN EMERGENCY. It was not alarmed. Odile opened it, and they went in feet first, and pulled it to behind them.

The cold hold was very dark and very cold, so cold that the moment the inner door of the maintenance lock opened, Maren's visor frosted at the edges and her suit heaters came on with a whine. They switched their lamps on low.

It was a long low hold, ribbed like the inside of a fish, with racks of sealed medical crates along both sides under a rime of frost, and at the far end, by itself, in a clear space on the deck, a cryostat.

It was smaller than she had imagined. A cylinder of brushed grey steel on a squat base, waist-high, with a ring of status lights round its upper rim glowing a steady green, and a frosted window in its lid. A thick power cable ran from its base to a socket in the hold wall. A second, thinner cable ran from its side to a box on the wall beside the socket, a grey box with a single red light on it and a keyhole.

Maren went and stood beside it. She did not touch it. She bent and looked through the frosted window in the lid.

Inside the cryostat, cradled in a nest of white foam that smoked faintly with cold, lay an egg.

It was grey. Matte grey, the grey of good ceramic, without any shine at all, so that the lamp's light seemed to sink into it rather than lie on it. It was about the length of her forearm and a half, smooth, slightly irregular, like a real egg, an egg from some enormous bird; and it had a seam running round its widest part, very fine, like the join of a cup and a saucer. On its upper surface, stencilled in black, in neat Combine capitals, were three lines.

SANITATION AGENT\
PATTERN H\
FACTOR'S AUTHORITY ONLY

Beneath the lines, small, the green wheel of Halloran-Vey.

Odile came and looked over her shoulder. Her breath frosted the inside of her visor.

"What is it?" she whispered.

"I don't know," said Maren.

That was true. It was also, she understood even as she said it, the last true thing she would be able to say about it. Because she was looking at it, at its smooth grey indifferent surface in its nest of smoking foam, and the cold of the hold was coming in through her suit, and something in the back of her skull, some old animal thing that had lived through twenty-six years on the line, was telling her very clearly and very quietly to back away.

There was a sound at the hold's forward end. A door.

Odile caught Maren's arm. They killed their lamps together and stood still in the dark, by the cryostat, with its ring of green lights glowing between them; and the forward door of the cold hold slid open and let in a wedge of yellow light, and in the wedge, two figures in heavy cold-weather jackets with the green wheel on the shoulder. A short broad woman with a sidearm. A tall thin man.

"Every day," said Sergeant Oyelowo's voice, flat and bored, echoing down the hold. "You know it's still there, sir. The board says it's still there. The light's green."

"I know," said Quist. "Humour me, Sergeant."

He did not come in. He stood in the doorway in the wedge of light with his hands in the pockets of his jacket, looking down the long dark hold toward the cryostat; looking, it seemed to Maren, straight at her, though he could not have seen her, with her lamp out, behind the glow of the status lights. He stood there for a long moment. His face was in shadow. Then he took one hand out of his pocket and touched something at his throat, a cord, a small shape on it that caught the light for an instant, the size of a key.

"Thank you, Sergeant," he said, and stepped back, and the door slid shut.

They stood in the dark for a long time afterward.

* * *

"They called it the Hush," Ekene said.

It was the fourth bell. The infirmary was dark except for the lamp on the counter, turned so low it was hardly more than a coal. Yusuf Brandt had been sent back to his own ship in the afternoon with his hand in a sling and a note to the factor. The bunks were empty. Ekene sat on the end of the nearest one with Maren beside him, both of them still in their clothes, and the cups of his smoked tea on the deck at their feet, cold.

"Not the Combine. The Combine calls it Sanitation Agent, Pattern H. The men who use it call it the Hush. Because that's what it does." He was looking at the port, where the Lamp hung over Gall's limb. "Tamsin's Rock was an ice-mining station in the Penner lanes. Four thousand bonded. Families, mostly, three generations of them, because the Rock was old. In seventy-nine the price of ice dropped and the Combine cut the air ration, and the Rock struck. They locked out the factor and the wardens and they sat in their warrens and said they would not dig another metre until the ration came back. They were very orderly about it. They kept the plant running. They kept the infirmary open. They sent a petition to Halloran." He paused. "The factor at the Rock had a Schedule H authority."

Maren said nothing.

"It's an aerosol," said Ekene. "That's all it is, in the end. An engineered thing, very small, that likes one sort of tissue and only that sort. Human nerve. Nothing else. Not rats, not plants, not machines. You let it into a habitat's air and the air system does the rest; it goes through every vent in an hour. It doesn't hurt. They told us that, after, the Combine's doctors, as if it were a kindness. You get tired. You get very quiet. People sat down where they were and went to sleep. In their bunks. At their tables. In the crèche." He swallowed. "In six hours there was nobody awake on Tamsin's Rock. In ten there was nobody alive. And the plant ran on. And the lights. And the infirmary's machines went on monitoring the patients in the beds, beeping, for two days, until the power ran down."

"You were there."

"I was a contract medic in the Penner lanes. They sent four of us in, afterward, with a Combine sanitation crew, to certify. That was the word. Certify." He laughed, a terrible small sound. "We had to go through every room. Every bunk. Every child. And write down the time we thought they'd died, so the Combine could close the accounts properly. It took us eleven days."

The Lamp hung in the port.

"The agent stays in the air for a few days and then it settles," Ekene said. "It goes dormant, like a spore. The sanitation crew sealed the Rock and pumped it hard vacuum and baked it, and then they let it sit for a year. And then they shipped in four thousand new bonded from the Kessel yards and opened it up again and started digging." He looked at his hands. "It was turning a profit inside eighteen months.
