They used it in the Combine's own reports. As an example of what could be achieved."

"Why didn't you stop?" Maren said. "Being a medic for them. After."

"I did. I came here." He turned and looked at her in the dark. "I came to the end of the lanes, Maren, to the most worthless operation the Combine had, so that nothing like that could ever happen around me again. Because who'd waste a Pattern H on Corva? Who'd bother?" His voice cracked. "And now there's one on the other side of the collar. With your factor checking it every day."

"Why bring it?" Maren said. "If they're just leaving. If the Camp's going to die anyway. Why bring that?"

"In case the leaving isn't quiet," said Ekene. "Schedule H. Contingency. You saw the dispatch. _Factor to manage expectations._" He took her hand. "If we take the ship, Maren, they won't send the _Admonition_ to fight us. Why would they? The _Admonition_ is expensive. Marines are expensive. They'll just tell the factor to open the box. One cryostat. One key. And then they'll wait a year and send somebody else to dip Gall, if they ever want to dip Gall again. Or not. It doesn't matter to them. That's the point. It doesn't matter."

Maren sat in the dark beside him with his cold hand in hers.

She thought of Quist in the doorway of the cold hold, with his hands in his pockets, looking. Every day. _Humour me, Sergeant._ She thought of the key at his throat.

She thought of the Belly, and the ring of tired faces in the lamplight, and Teska saying _We take her_. She thought of Hale's mild voice in the dark: _I'll be filling sandbags beside you_. She thought of eleven metres of hull with Gall underneath.

She did the arithmetic. It came out differently now. It came out, she realised, sitting there, with the Lamp brightening in the port and the egg asleep in its smoking foam across the collar, the only way it could have come out from the moment she bent over that frosted window and the old animal at the back of her skull told her to back away.

"We don't take the ship first," she said.

Ekene looked at her.

"We take the egg," said Maren Tsolo. "Before anything. Before they know we're coming. We take the egg and the key, and then we see who wants to talk about Sorrel."


# Chapter 8: Ephemeris

*The Siege · 2654*

The Archive is the Spine's old cargo deck, four levels down from the nave, and it smells the way I imagine the inside of a very old person's head smells: of dust, and paper, and cold, and something sweetish and faint like rot. There's no proper light. There never has been. Malachy works by lamps, a dozen of them on long arms clamped to his tables, so that the Archive is a great dark cave with little islands of yellow in it, and on every island, books, slates, rolled charts, folders of foil, ledgers bound in plastic gone brown, the whole two hundred and sixty years of Patience written down by people who mostly never expected anyone to read it.

He has the Plate on his biggest table, under three lamps, face down.

"Close the door," he says, when I come in. "And lock it. There's a bar."

I bar the door. When I turn round he's bending over the table with a magnifier strapped round his head on a leather band, like a jeweller, and a tiny brush in one hand and a dish of something in the other that smells sharp enough to make my eyes water.

"Solvent," he says, without looking up. "Old dock solvent. The kind they used to strip hull paint, back when we had hulls to strip. There's a drum of it in the bottom store that's been there since my grandfather's time. It does the job, slowly. Too fast and it would take the scratches with it." He dabs the brush, very lightly, and wipes. "Come and look. Don't breathe on it."

I come and look.

He's cleaned a patch in the top corner of the Plate's back, no bigger than my palm. Inside it, the grey sealant is gone, and the steel underneath is dark and dull and pitted with age, and across it, cut into the metal, there are marks. Not deep. Scratched, rather than cut: thin uncertain lines, as if made with a point dragged slowly and hard, by somebody pressing with all their weight. They're figures. Numbers. A column of them, and beside the column, letters, single letters, like labels.

"Somebody scratched these with an awl," says Malachy. "A rigger's scribe, probably. The kind they used for marking cable. You can see where the point skidded." He touches the air above a line with the end of his brush. "Look at the sevens. They cross their sevens. Nobody's crossed a seven in this city for two hundred years."

"What are they?"

"Numbers."

"Malachy."

"I don't know yet." He straightens up, with a little grunt, and pushes the magnifier up on to his forehead, where it sits like a third eye. "I've done this much in a night. There's the whole back of the Plate still under paint, and the paint's harder in the middle, where it was thicker. A week. Two." He looks at me. "I need you to keep this to yourself, Isaura."

"Who would I tell?"

"Your mother. Your section chief. That rigger woman from South Lock who's been asking about you on the Ninth." He sees my face and smiles thinly. "I hear things. I'm an archivist. Hearing things is most of the job."

"Why does it matter? It's numbers on the back of a plate."

"It matters," says Malachy, "because somebody went to a great deal of trouble to put them there, and then went to a great deal more trouble to make sure nobody could see them." He lays the brush down across the dish. "Think about it. This is the Share Plate. It's the first thing the founders made. It's been hanging in the nave since before there was a nave. Every child in Patience has stood in front of it. And somebody, two hundred and sixty years ago, took it down and cut something into the back of it with an awl, and then painted over it with hull sealant, carefully, edge to edge, so that it would look like nothing but the back of a piece of steel. And hung it up again." He spreads his old spotted hands. "You don't do that with a shopping list."

"Then why not throw it away? If they didn't want it read."

"Because they did want it read." Malachy pulls the magnifier back down over his eye and bends to the Plate again. "Eventually. Just not by whoever was standing in front of it at the time." He dabs with the brush. "Those aren't the same thing, Isaura. Keeping a thing and hiding it. You'll find most of this room is one or the other. The trick is telling which."

* * *

Nine days after South Lock, the Wardens' Office takes me off the Ninth.

I'm called in to see my section chief, a weary shared woman named Paske who has been running the South wardens since before I joined, and who looks at me across her desk with the expression of somebody who has been given a problem she didn't ask for. I'm not being disciplined, she says. Nobody's filed a complaint. The bottle's been paid for out of the Office budget; the militia were very grateful; there's even a commendation, which she pushes across the desk to me on a slip of foil. But there's been talk on the Ninth. About a warden giving away air to a Concord pilot while the Ninth's own children are on the floor. And there have been, Paske says delicately, incidents. A warden on the Ninth North had his ledger taken off him on the stairs and thrown down a shaft. Another had her valve key bent. It would be better for everybody, Paske says, if I were somewhere else for a while.

Somewhere else is the Cells.

The Cells are under the Spine, below the Archive, below the old docks, in what was once the skimmer's keel: a long dim corridor of steel boxes, each with a door and a grille and a meter. In peacetime they hold drunks and thieves and the occasional unshared rioter. Now, eight days into July and twenty-eight days into the siege, they hold a few dozen of each and one Concord lieutenant. Somebody has to read the meters. That's me.

It's quiet work. It's almost restful, after the Ninth. The prisoners are on a fixed allowance set by the Council, a little above the old floor, and nobody pays tithe, so nobody falls into arrears, so nobody has to be cut. I walk the corridor twice a shift with my gauge and read each meter aloud into the ledger and check the seals on the doors, and the prisoners mostly ignore me. Some swear. One old man on the end, a tannery thief from the Fifth, asks me every shift whether the Lamp's still in the sky, and I tell him yes, and he says _good_, and turns over.

The lieutenant's cell is the eleventh.

"Ebb, Mattias," I say to my ledger, on the first morning, outside his grille. "Concord Lane Service. Allowance three-twenty an hour. Draw this cycle, three-oh-five." I look through the grille. "You're breathing light, Lieutenant."

He's sitting on his bunk with his back against the wall and his knees up, in a grey prison smock with the sleeves too short, his cropped fair hair grown out a little into a pale fuzz. The bruising on his face has gone yellow. He looks up when I speak, and I see him recognise me, and something complicated happens in his face.

"I was told to," he says. "In training. _In captivity, conserve._ Every ration. Breathe slow, move slow, sleep a lot." He shrugs. "I'm very good at sleeping."

"You don't have to conserve here. It's your allowance. It doesn't go back in the tank if you don't use it."

"Doesn't it?"

"No. It's just wasted."

He thinks about this with a sort of grave interest. "That's very inefficient."

"Welcome to Patience," I say, and go on to the twelfth.

He's there on the second day, and the third, and every day after, and after the third day we talk. Not much. A few minutes, across the grille, while I take the reading and check the seal. It isn't permitted, exactly. It isn't forbidden either; nobody's thought to forbid it, because nobody's imagined a warden would want to. I'm not sure I want to. But he's the only person in the city who knows less about it than I do, and there's a strange relief in that.

His name is Mattias. He's twenty-four. He comes from a world called Corran, three lanes in, an old Concord holding where everybody works in the canneries. His father owed the Concord a great deal of money for something to do with a fishing boat that sank, and when his father died the debt came down to Mattias, and the Concord, which is efficient, offered him the choice of the canneries for forty years or the Lane Service for twelve. He chose the Lane Service. He's done four years of the twelve. He flies landers. He was flying a lander over Kettle's south ice on the thirtieth of June, on a probe, because his captain wanted to see whether the city had any guns, and the city did.

"That's what it's like everywhere," he says, on the fifth day, when I tell him about the floor. "The Concord. The tithe, the floor. They don't call it that. They call it _allocation_. But it's the same." He looks at me through the grille with his frightened soldier's eyes, which are, I've decided, not frightened at all, only very tired. "You'd think you'd be different here. You threw the Company out. Didn't you? You bought your own air. That's what they told us in the briefings. _The occupiers claim to have purchased the claim._ And then you went and did the same thing to each other."

"We didn't do anything. I wasn't born."

"No. Nor was I." He leans his head back against the wall. "Nobody's ever born when it happens. Have you noticed? It's always already happened."

On the sixth day he asks me why I gave him the bottle.

"It's my job," I say.

"It isn't, though. Is it? Your job's taking it away." He says it without any edge. "The militia officer told me. On the stretcher, when he thought I couldn't hear. He said, _that's the warden from the Ninth, she's cut more air than the plague_. He was laughing."

I look at the meter. Three-oh-eight.

"I don't know," I say, which is the truth.

"Well," says Mattias Ebb, after a while. "Thank you anyway. Whatever it was."

* * *

That afternoon Malachy sends for me again, and when I get to the Archive, the First Shareholder is there.

He's standing at the end of Malachy's big table with his hands behind his back, looking down at the Plate under its lamps, and he doesn't turn round when I come in. Two of his stewards wait by the door. Malachy, at the table, gives me a look over the top of his magnifier that I've known since I was eight years old, the look that means _say nothing, you'll only make it worse_.

Ten days of solvent have cleared a long strip down the left side of the Plate's back, about the width of my hand, from the top almost to the bottom. In the strip, the scratched figures go down in a column, with their letters beside them. Six lines. Below the six lines, a gap, and then more scratches, different, less regular, still half under the paint. The rest of the Plate is grey.

"Come in, warden," says Lucan Strand. "The Archivist tells me you were the first to see this. When it fell."

"Yes, First Shareholder."

"And you've said nothing to anyone."

"No, First Shareholder."

"Good." He turns his head and looks at me, and smiles a little, his tired silver smile. "Brother Malachy has been telling me what he thinks it is. I'd like you to hear it. Since you were there at the beginning." He nods to the old man. "Go on, Brother."

Malachy straightens up. He doesn't push the magnifier up this time; he takes it off altogether and lays it on the table, which with Malachy means that he's about to say something he's sure of.

"They're orbital elements," he says. "Six of them. Semi-major axis, eccentricity, inclination, the longitude of the node, the argument of perihelion, and a time." He touches the air above each line with his brush. "That's how dippers wrote down an orbit. They had to know about orbits to do their work, much better than we do; they lived in them. These are in the old dippers' notation, with the old dippers' units. Whoever scratched this knew exactly what they were doing."

"An orbit of what?" says Strand.

"Something long," says Malachy. "And very eccentric. Something that goes a very long way out and comes back." He purses his lips. "I'd need to run the figures to be sure, and I can't run them yet. There's a time on the last line, the time of perihelion, and it's under a flaw in the steel. Somebody's awl slipped."

Strand is quiet for a moment.

"And the rest?" He nods at the half-cleared scratches below the gap. "There's more. Writing?"

"Letters, yes. Words, I think. I haven't got to them." Malachy picks up his magnifier again and turns it over in his fingers. "Another week. Perhaps two. The paint's very hard in the middle."

"Faster, Brother."

"If I go faster I'll take the words off with the paint."

"Then don't go faster," says Strand mildly. "But don't go slower." He comes round the end of the table and looks down at the strip of scratched figures for a long moment, and I see his face in the lamplight and I see something in it I've never seen in it before: not the warmth he wears in the nave or the patience he wears on the speakers, but hunger, plain and sharp, like a man looking at a meal.

"The founders wrote this," he says softly. "On the Plate. And then they hid it."

"Somebody did," says Malachy.

"The founders," Strand says again, as if correcting him. He looks up. "Do you know what reserve stood at this morning, Brother? Fifty-two days. Grieve's slugs took out the third intake on the far ice last night. Fifty-two. A month ago it was ninety-four." He's not raising his voice; he never raises his voice. "The Council meets tomorrow. If the reserve falls below twenty days, we will have to consider measures. Measures nobody in this city wants to consider. Do you understand me?"

"I understand you," says Malachy.

"Then you understand why I want to know what the founders thought was worth hiding." Strand straightens his coat. "Nobody sees this but the three of us. Not the Council. Not my son. When you have the words, you send for me first. Before anyone." He turns to me. "And you, warden. Not a word. Not to your mother. Not to the Ninth. If this city ever needed its founders, it needs them now, and I won't have them turned into a Lamper rumour on the stairs." He smiles. "Do you understand?"

"Yes, First Shareholder."

"Good," he says, and goes out with his stewards.

When the door's barred behind him, Malachy sits down heavily on his stool and puts his face in his hands. He sits like that for a long time.

"_Measures_," he says at last, through his fingers.

"What measures?"

He takes his hands away and looks at me. "You've been a warden twelve years, Isaura," he says. "You know what a city does when it runs short of air. You've done it on a small scale every day of your working life. Think about it on a large one."

I think about it.

I think about the floor, and the doors on the Ninth, and the grandmother under her three blankets with her hands folded. I think about a hundred and seventy thousand unshared and twelve thousand shared, and a reserve going down like a needle on a meter. And I understand what he means, all at once, the way you understand the cold when you step out of a lock, and I sit down too, on the bench by the door, and for a while neither of us says anything at all.

* * *

On the last round of my shift, at the tenth bell, I stop at the eleventh cell longer than I should.

The lieutenant's asleep, or pretending. He's lying on his back on the bunk with his arm over his eyes. I read his meter—three-one-nine, he's stopped conserving—and log it, and check the seal, and then I stand at the grille without moving on.

"You never told me," I say. "Why you came."

He doesn't move his arm. "I told you. I was flying a probe."

"Not you. The Concord. Thirty-eight ships, all the way out here to the end of the lanes, for a moon nobody's wanted in two hundred years. For a claim nobody's tried to collect since before your great-grandfather was born." The corridor's dim and cold and quiet, the tannery thief snoring at the far end. "Grieve says the Concord holds the Combine's title. Fine. It's held it for a century. Why now?"

Mattias lies still for a while. Then he takes his arm off his eyes and turns his head on the thin pillow and looks at me through the grille.

"For what's ours," he says. "That's what they told us. _To recover the Concord's property._"

"The city."

"The city. The claim. The accounts." He hesitates. "And something else."

"What?"

"I don't know." He sits up slowly, wincing at his ribs, and swings his feet down to the floor and sits on the edge of the bunk with his hands between his knees. "I'm a lieutenant. They don't tell lieutenants things. But before we came through the Throat they briefed every lander crew in the fleet, all of us together, in the big bay on the _Steadfast_, and there was an officer from the Commodore's staff there, a commander I'd never seen before, and at the end of the briefing, after the guns and the pickets and the rules about civilians, he said there was one more thing." He looks at his hands. "He said the Combine had lost something here. A long time ago. An item of Company property, never recovered. He said the Concord had reason to believe it was still in the system. He said if any of us, on any probe or landing, came across anything that looked like old Company equipment, anything at all, with the Combine's mark on it, we were to report it and not touch it." He looks up at me. "And then he said, _and if you find it, you are not to open it. Under any circumstances. Not to look inside. Not to break the seal._ And then he said, _you'll know it if you see it._ And then he left."

"What does that mean?"

"I've thought about it a lot," says Mattias Ebb. "In here. I've had time." He's quiet for a moment. "I think it means they're not afraid of you, warden. Not of your guns or your city or your Council. I think they're afraid of something your founders had." He lies back down on the bunk and puts his arm over his eyes again. "Goodnight."

I stand at the grille a while longer. Then I go on down the corridor to the end, where the tannery thief is snoring, and read his meter, and log it, and he wakes up and asks me if the Lamp's still in the sky.

"Yes," I say.

"Good," he says, and turns over.


# Chapter 9: Old Iron

*The Last Return · 2917*

They find the platform on the eighteenth, on the way in, because Oz is looking for it.

"My mother said there were platforms," he says, in the cockpit, with his big soft face close to the scope. "Fuel depots. Where the skimmers topped up between dips. The Combine left them in high orbit when it went. She said her grandmother used to say you could see them from the ice at night, like little moons." He adjusts the gain. "Two hundred years and more. Most of them'll have come down. But high orbit's high orbit."

"We don't need fuel."

"We need reaction mass for the sling, and we need it cheap. And it's old iron, Til." He grins without turning round. "When did you ever pass old iron?"

There it is, on the third sweep: a small hard return in a long slow orbit far above Gall's equator, tumbling very slowly end over end. Til brings the _Dray_ up to it in four hours, carefully, the way you come up on any dead thing in the black, and holds her two hundred metres off while Oz puts the lamps on it.

It's ugly. It's uglier than the _Dray_, which takes some doing. A long girder spine with tanks clustered along it like grapes on a stalk, most of them burst open now and peeled back in grey petals; a docking ring at one end, crusted with something white; a squat block of habitat modules at the other, windowless, with a faded number painted on its flank in characters two metres high. A 4. And beside the 4, just visible under five centuries of micrometeorite pitting, a wheel. A green wheel with eight spokes.

"That's us," says Hollis Vey's voice over the intercom, very quietly. He's watching from the cradle through the cabin camera. "That's the family mark."

"It's a fuel depot, Mr Vey."

"Everything we owned had that on it. Everything and everyone." A pause. "Go and look, Captain. Please. Take a camera. I'll pay for the time."

They go across in suits, the three of them, on a line: Til first, Juno in the middle, Oz at the back with the lamps. Til didn't ask Juno to come. Juno asked. She came up to the suit lock while Til and Oz were checking seals and stood in the hatch with her new suit already on, helmet in her hands, and said "I'm witnessing," as if daring anybody to argue, and nobody did.

The habitat block's hatch is jammed. Oz cuts it. Inside, it's black and airless and so cold their suit heaters whine, and their lamps go down a narrow corridor lined with lockers and cable runs and frost, white frost thick as fur on every surface, so that the whole place looks like the inside of a mouth that hasn't been opened for a long time.

It's a small place. A dozen people lived here once, Oz thinks, maybe fifteen, on rotation from the skimmers: depot hands, the people who ran the hoses and kept the tanks from boiling. There's a bunk room with fifteen bunks, the bedding still on them, frozen into stiff grey shapes. There's a galley with a table bolted to the deck and eight chairs, and on the table a cup, standing upright, with a skin of ice in the bottom of it gone hard as stone. There's a washroom. There's a duty office, very small, with a desk and a slate screen gone black and a shelf of foil folders.

On the wall of the galley, under the frost, there's writing.

Juno finds it. She puts her glove to the frost and rubs, and it comes away in a soft white shower, and under it the grey paint of the bulkhead is covered in scratched and pencilled marks: names, mostly, scratched with something sharp, dozens of them, one under another. Some have dates beside them. The dates are old, very old, numbers that don't look like dates at all to Til until she reads them twice: 2361, 2374, 2388. Beside some of the names there are little drawings. A tank. A heart. A bottle. Near the bottom, beside a name nobody can read any more, a small round shape with a tail.

And under that, across the whole width of the panel, in capitals somebody took a long time over:

THE LAMP TAKES THE DEAD OUT AND BRINGS THEM BACK

Oz stands in front of it for a long time with his lamp up.

"That's ours," he says at last. "That's the saying. That's the Roundsmen's." His voice is thick in Til's helmet. "My mother's people wrote that. Here. Right here."

"Somebody did," says Til.

"Somebody did." He puts his big glove on the wall beside the words, flat, the way the Roundsmen touch the chalk loops on the chapel walls on Dock Nine. "Five hundred years, Til."

In the duty office, Juno has opened the shelf of folders.

She's standing at the little desk with one of them open under her lamp, and she's not moving. When Til comes in she doesn't look up. The folder's full of foil slips, thin grey strips printed in a cramped old type, frozen together at the edges. She's peeled the top few apart with her glove.

"Accounts," she says.

Til looks over her shoulder. Each slip has a name and a number at the top, and below them, columns. Dates. Codes. Figures. Some are in black, most in red. She can't read the codes. She doesn't need to.

"_Air, standard ration, thirty days,_" Juno reads, in her clerk's voice, very flat. "_Suit maintenance. Medical, two items. Food, standard. Carried forward from previous: four thousand one hundred and six marks._" She turns the slip over. "_Balance carried to next of kin._" She peels off another. "This one's a boy. Look. Sixteen. _Account opened, age of majority, carried from parent._ He'd been working one year and he owed six thousand." Another. "This one's dead. It says. _Deceased, Gall. Balance carried._" Her glove's shaking. "They're all like that. All of them. Every one."

"Juno."

"I do this," says Juno. "This is my job. This is what I do at the counting-house all day. I carry balances." She turns round in the little frozen office with the folder held against her chest, and through her visor her face is wet. "Five hundred years ago, and it's the same. It's exactly the same. The same words. _Balance carried._" She looks at Til. "Who are they going to carry it to? They're all dead. They've been dead five hundred years. Who's going to pay it?"

Til doesn't have an answer. She stands in the doorway of the duty office with her lamp, and Juno stands there with the folder, and after a moment Juno puts the folder down very carefully on the desk where she found it, open, with its frozen slips, and closes it.

"Leave it," she says. "Leave it here. They can keep it."

Over the intercom, from the cradle, Hollis Vey says nothing at all.

They pump four hundred tonnes of five-century-old reaction mass out of the depot's last unburst tank before they leave. It's dirty and full of ice and Oz swears at it for two hours while he filters it, but it burns. Til takes the _Dray_ away from Platform Four slowly, the way she came, and the old girder spine goes on tumbling end over end behind them in its long slow orbit, with its green wheel and its frozen cup and its wall of names.

* * *

"It's already breaking," says Oz.

He has the Lamp up on the main screen in the cabin, where the cradle is, so that everyone can see: the comet as it is now, from twenty million kilometres, magnified as far as the _Dray_'s old scope will go. It's not a smear any more. It's a body. A long dark lumpy thing like a potato, or a footprint, or two stones stuck together, wrapped in its glowing coma; and from its surface, here and there, thin bright jets of gas spurt out into the sunlight and curl away into the tail. Along one side of it, near the narrow waist where the two lobes meet, there's a line. A dark line. A crack.

"That's new," says Oz. "That wasn't in the Observatory pictures. That's opened up in the last month." He taps the screen. "And there. And there. Little ones. And it's shedding. See that? That's a chunk the size of the Ledger Ring coming off it."

"How long's it got?"

"Depends what you mean by got." Oz brings up another picture, a diagram, curves on a grid. "This is its path. This is Gall. This dashed line round Gall is the limit, the Roche limit; inside it, the giant's pull on the near side of anything is so much stronger than on the far side that the thing just comes apart. Rubble flies apart. Ice flies apart. Moons fly apart." He traces the comet's curve with his finger. "The Lamp crosses inside the line on the thirtieth of April. By the first of May it'll be in pieces. Twenty, thirty, maybe more. They'll go round Gall in a string, like beads, and they'll come in on the second and they'll hit."

"All of them?"

"Most. The big ones." He shrugs. "Some of the small stuff'll be flung out. Some might go round and come back. Some might go anywhere. Gall's moons are in the way. Kettle's in the way. It's a mess, Til. It'll be the most spectacular mess anyone's seen in five hundred years and there'll be nobody watching it but us." He folds his arms. "If we want to land on it, we land on it before it's pieces. The thirtieth. The first at the latest. After that there's nothing to land on, just a gravel cloud falling into a gas giant at sixty kilometres a second."

"And we can get there."

"We can get there." Oz brings up a third picture, a fat curve swinging round Gall. "This is how. We go in close to the giant, really close, cloud-tops close, and let it swing us round, and we light the drive at the bottom of the swing and we come out of it on the Lamp's path with its speed. It's the old way. The dippers' way. My mother called it the sling." He grins. "Nobody's done it in five hundred years and the _Dray_'s not built for it and if I've got the numbers wrong by a hair we'll go into Gall like one of their dead. But it'll work."

"It'll work," says Til.

"I said it'll work."

"You said it like it might not."

"Everything might not," says Oz comfortably. "That's what makes it interesting."

On his pillows, Hollis Vey has been watching the screen without saying anything. His pale eyes are fixed on the crack along the Lamp's waist, the thin dark line.

"So it's true," he says. "The last time."

"The last time," says Oz.

"Then we'd better be quick." Vey closes his eyes. "Thank you, Mr Mbeki. That was very clear."

Juno, sitting on the deck by the cradle with her knees up, hasn't taken her eyes off the diagram: the comet's path, the dashed circle, the little marks that are Gall's moons, sitting right in the way of whatever doesn't fall.

"What happens," she says, "if something's on one of the bits that gets flung out?"

Everybody looks at her.

"Nothing happens," says Til. "It goes off into the dark and nobody sees it again."

"Or it hits a moon," says Juno.

"Or it hits a moon."

"Kettle's in the way," says Juno, looking at the diagram. "He said."

"Kettle's been in the way of a lot of things," says Oz gently. "It's a big ball of ice, love. It's been hit before."

Juno doesn't say anything else. But Til, looking at her, sees her look across the cabin at Hollis Vey, lying with his eyes shut on his pillows, and sees Cato Marchetti, on his crate by the cradle, look at Juno looking.

* * *

Cato Marchetti comes to find her in the cargo bay on the twentieth, while she's checking the anchors.

She hears him coming. That's unusual in itself; he moves so quietly that Oz has taken to ringing a little bell on the galley hatch when he goes through it, as a joke, and Marchetti, who seems to have no sense of humour at all, has taken to ringing it back. He's let her hear him on purpose. He comes down the bay between the cable drums and stands at a courteous distance with his hands in his coat pockets and watches her work.

"Mr Vey's sleeping," he says. "He has bad nights."

"I know. I hear him."

"He's in a good deal of pain. He doesn't let it show. He's had a long time to practise." Marchetti watches her screw a piton into its rack. "He's very taken with your niece."

"Everybody's taken with my niece. She's very takeable."

"She reads to him. Did you know? In the evenings, while you're on watch. He asked her to. She reads him the ship's manuals, because there's nothing else aboard to read, and he listens with his eyes shut as if it were poetry." Marchetti's voice doesn't change. "He asked her yesterday what she'd do if her bond were paid. She said she didn't know. She said nobody had ever asked her."

Til racks the last piton and straightens up. "Is there something you want, Mr Marchetti?"

"I was curious," he says, "about you."

"I'm not that interesting."

"Everyone's interesting, Captain. That's been my experience. Everyone has a price, and the interesting thing about them is what it is." He takes one hand out of his pocket and runs a finger, idly, along the cold rim of a cable drum. "Mother Szabo said yours was the _Dray_. And then, when I met you, I thought it was your niece. And now I'm not sure."

"What's yours?"

He smiles, for the first time since she's known him. It doesn't change his face much; it's a small courteous movement at the corners of the mouth, like a man acknowledging a good move in a game. "Mr Vey pays me very well."

"That's not a price. That's a wage."

"Yes," says Marchetti. "It is." He takes his hand off the drum and puts it back in his pocket. "You went to prison for four years, Captain, for a salvage your sister planned. You came out owing more than you went in. You've hauled scrap for seven years to pay a debt that isn't yours, for a girl who won't speak to you. You came through a raw lane for a dying man's fairy tale. I've met a great many people who'd do anything for money, and I don't think you're one of them, and it's been puzzling me." He tilts his head. "What would you do, Captain, for nothing? That's what I'd like to know."

"Ask me on the first of May," says Til.

"I will," says Marchetti, and goes, and this time she doesn't hear him at all.

Later, in the galley, Oz says, "He's a bad one."

"I know."

"Not bad like Szabo. Szabo's bad the way weather's bad. You know where you are." Oz is peeling a ration bar with his delicate fingers, very slowly, as if it were a fruit. "He's the other kind. The kind that's polite to you right up to the moment."

"I know, Oz."

"And the old man's lying."

"I know that too."

"And the girl's reading to him in the evenings." Oz eats a piece of the bar. "She asked me about the Harrow."

Til's hands go still on her cup.

"Last night," says Oz. "While you were on watch. She came and sat in the engine space, while I was doing the coolant sleeve, and she asked me if I was there. On the Harrow. When it happened." He chews. "I said no. I was on the boat. I was on the boat on the other side of the hulk, minding the winch, and I didn't see. Which is true."

"What else did she ask?"

"She asked me if it was the way Szabo tells it." Oz looks at her across the little galley table, with his bag-of-stones face and his wet old eyes. "I said it wasn't. I said that was all I'd say, and if she wanted the rest she'd have to ask you. And she said she had, and you wouldn't." He finishes the bar. "That's twice she's asked, Til. She won't ask a third time. She's a Brask."

"I know."

"Then tell her."

"It doesn't help her," Til says. "It doesn't help anybody. It just moves it."

"Moves what?"

"The weight," says Til, and gets up and goes forward to the cockpit.

She has the watch from the eighth bell. Gall fills the lower half of the glass now, enormous, close enough to see the storms moving in the bands, slow as clock hands. Kettle hangs to the left of it, white and cracked, with its night side toward them, a curved black bite out of the giant's glow.

At the tenth bell, on Kettle's night side, a light comes on.

She sees it because she's been watching for it, ever since the radar pulse at the Throat. It's tiny. A pinprick. A single steady point of white light on the black curve of the moon's dark half, where there should be nothing but ice; not a reflection, not a star behind, not a flare of Gall-light off a ridge. A lamp. It burns for nine seconds by the board's clock. Then it goes out.

Til sits for a long time with her hands on her knees, looking at the place where it was.

She doesn't log it. She doesn't wake Oz. She doesn't know why, any more than she knew at the Throat; only that it felt like something private. As if somebody on that dark moon had lit a lamp in a window to see who was out there in the dark, and had seen her looking, and had put it out.


# Chapter 10: Seizure

*The Bonded · 2391*

They went at the fourth bell of the night, under the noise of the dip.

That was Teska's idea. _They'll be listening for quiet,_ she said, in the Belly, the last time they met. _Every guard listens for quiet. So give them noise._ So Maren had put a night dip on the roster, the first in a month, a short shallow one into the high cloud that would bring up almost nothing; and the factor had approved it without comment, because the last weeks of a season were for squeezing, and a tether-chief who wanted to squeeze was a tether-chief earning her supervisory assessment. At the third bell the drum began to turn. By the fourth it was running at full spin, and the whole forward half of the _Patience_ was shaking with it, a great steady thunder that came up through every deck and every bulkhead and every bone, so loud in the collar section that the marine on the _Clement_'s side of the lock had his ear-guards in.

Maren was outside by then, on the hull, with Odile and Danek, going hand over hand along Odile's running line.

Three of them, because the egg was heavy and a cryostat was heavier, and because Danek had asked. _I'm big,_ he said. _If there's a marine, I'm big._ She had said yes. She would think about that later, a great deal; she had said yes because he was big and because she wanted someone big beside her in that cold dark hold, and because he had three children in the third shaft and a wife called Tamar with the cough, and he wanted to be doing something for them with his hands instead of sitting in the Belly listening.

Gall turned below them. The Lamp hung above, so bright now it threw faint shadows on the hull: three small black figures crawling on a long rust-coloured whale, each with a shadow beside it from a comet.

The maintenance hatch opened as easily as before. They went in feet first. The inner door. The cold.

There was a marine in the cold hold.

There had not been one before. There had been the cryostat, and the racks, and the frost, and nobody; the hold was checked every four hours by a cargo hand and every day by the factor, and otherwise left alone. Tonight there was a marine sitting on a crate by the forward door in a cold-weather jacket with his rifle across his knees and his hood up, reading a slate, with a heater cube glowing orange at his feet. He looked up when the inner door of the maintenance lock opened. He was very young. He had the slate in one hand and the other on the rifle, and for a long moment he simply stared at them, three figures in white runner's suits coming out of a wall, as if they were ghosts.

Danek went for him.

He did it before Maren could say anything. He went across the hold in four long strides, enormous in his suit, and the boy got the rifle half up and Danek knocked it aside with one arm and took him round the chest with the other and bore him down on to the deck, and they rolled, and the heater cube went skittering away across the frost, and the boy was shouting something inside his hood and Danek was saying _quiet, quiet, quiet, I won't hurt you, quiet_, over and over, like a man gentling a dog.

"Tape," said Maren. "Odile."

Odile had the tape. They taped the boy's wrists and ankles and taped his mouth, carefully, so he could breathe, and sat him against a rack. He stared at them over the tape with huge wet eyes. Danek knelt in front of him and patted his shoulder.

"Sorry, son," he said. "Nothing personal."

Maren went to the cryostat.

The status lights round its rim were still green. The egg lay in its smoking foam under the frosted lid, grey and indifferent. The thick power cable ran to its socket in the wall. The thin cable ran to the grey box beside it, the box with the single red light and the keyhole.

"How do we move it?" said Odile.

"We unplug the power. It's got its own cells; Ekene says they last days. We unplug the box." Maren looked at the thin cable. "The box is how the factor opens it from here. Without the box, he'd have to open it by hand."

"Then pull it."

"Wait."

She didn't know why she said it. She stood with her hand on the cryostat's cold flank and looked at the box on the wall, at its red light, and something at the back of her skull, the animal that lived through twenty-six years on the line, said _wait_.

The forward door opened.

It didn't slide open, the way it had the last time. It slammed back on its track, hard, and Sergeant Oyelowo came through it fast and low with her sidearm up in both hands, and behind her, in a dark coat thrown over his sleeping clothes, came Aurelian Quist.

"Hands," said Oyelowo. "Hands where I can see them. All of you. Now."

There was a tamper sensor on the box. Of course there was. Maren understood it in the half-second it took Oyelowo to come through the door: the red light was not a light, it was a sensor, and when the maintenance hatch opened the cold hold's pressure had dipped, by a breath, and the box had felt it, and woken somebody. She'd had the right of it. She'd been told to wait, and she'd waited, and it hadn't mattered at all.

Odile put her hands up. Maren put hers up.

Danek didn't.

He was between the sergeant and the cryostat, on his knees by the taped boy, and he stood up, slowly, all of him, unfolding the way he always did out of the cramped spaces of the drum, and he put himself in front of the cryostat with his arms spread a little, as if it were a child.

"Sit down, rigger," said Oyelowo.

"Can't do that," said Danek. "Sorry."

"Last time."

"I've got three kids in the Camp," said Danek Sorell, conversationally. "Tamar's got the cough. You've got a key to a box that's going to let them go to sleep. I can't sit down, Sergeant. You see that."

Oyelowo shot him.

She shot him twice, in the chest, very fast, the sidearm making two flat cracks in the cold hold like boards breaking, and Danek sat down after all. He sat down against the cryostat with a look of mild surprise and put his hand on his chest and looked at it, and then he looked up at Maren, and opened his mouth to say something, and didn't.

Odile hit Oyelowo with a pry bar.

Maren never saw where she got it from. The racks, perhaps; there were pry bars in every hold, for the crates. She came in from the side while the sergeant was still looking at Danek, low and fast, the way runners move on the line when something's going wrong, and swung it with both hands into the back of Oyelowo's knee, and then, as the sergeant went down, into her forearm, and the sidearm went skating away across the frost under the racks. Oyelowo lay on the deck holding her arm and swearing in a dull steady monotone. Odile stood over her with the bar raised, shaking.

"Don't," said Maren.

Odile didn't. She didn't put the bar down either.

Quist hadn't moved.

He was standing just inside the forward door, where he'd stopped when the shooting began. He was not looking at Danek, or Odile, or the sergeant. He was looking at the grey box on the wall beside the cryostat; and he had his hand at his throat, and in his hand, on its cord, was the key.

Maren knew, as clearly as she had ever known anything, that she could not reach him in time. He was three paces from the box. She was eight from him.

"Factor," she said.

He looked at her. His face was white and very calm.

"If you put that key in that box," Maren said, "it goes into the hold. And through the hold into the _Clement_. And through the collar into the _Patience_. Three hundred people. Your own crew. Your sergeant. That boy. You."

"Yes," said Quist.

"Is that what your orders say?"

"My orders say," said Quist, "that if the bonded of the Corva operation take up arms against the Combine, or attempt to seize Combine property, the factor is authorised to exercise Schedule H at his discretion." He said it the way he had said everything since the day he came aboard, as if he meant it, and he did. "That's what I've been sitting up for, every night for two weeks. In case this. I've known for a week you'd come, Chief. I've known since the boy Brandt came back with his hand in a sling and a note from your medic." He took a step toward the box. "I've thought about it a great deal."

"Then do it," said Maren.

Odile made a sound.

"Do it, if you're going to," said Maren. She didn't put her hands down. She stood beside the cryostat with Danek dead against its flank and the egg asleep under the frosted lid, and she looked at Quist across eight paces of frozen deck. "Put the key in the box. I can't stop you. Nobody in this hold can stop you. Do it, and go to sleep with the rest of us, and in a year they'll send four thousand new bonded to dip Gall and you'll be a line in somebody's report. An example of what can be achieved."

Quist stood with the key in his hand.

The drum thundered through the collar, far away. On the deck, Oyelowo had stopped swearing. The taped boy against the rack was crying without any sound.

"I was at Bellamy Deep," said Quist, quite quietly. "And the Hollow Moons. I wasn't at Tamsin's Rock. A man I trained was. He used to send me letters, after. Long letters. About his garden. He'd taken up gardening. He wrote very well about roses." He looked down at the key. "He hanged himself four years ago, in his greenhouse. They found him among the roses." He was silent for a moment. "I have been the factor of the Corva operation for fifteen days, Chief Tsolo, and every night of the fifteen I have sat up in that cabin with that hideous painting and this key, and asked myself whether I'm the kind of man who takes up gardening."

"And?"

"And I don't know," said Quist. "I still don't know. I've been standing here trying to find out."

He took the cord from round his neck. He held it out, at arm's length, the key swinging a little on the end of it, catching the light from the cryostat's ring of green.

"Take it," he said. "Quickly. Before I find out."

Maren crossed the eight paces and took it.

* * *

The rest of the night came apart into pieces, and afterward Maren could never put them back in the right order.

She remembered the cryostat on its sled, the power unplugged, its own cells humming, being walked by Odile and the taped boy, untaped now and too frightened to do anything but push, back down the _Clement_'s long spine corridor toward the collar. She remembered the collar standing open, which meant Senna and Ivo had done their part, and the marine on the _Clement_'s side of it sitting against the bulkhead with his hands on his head and his ear-guards still in. She remembered Danek. She had left him in the cold hold, against the place where the cryostat had been, with his hand still on his chest and Odile's coat over his face, because there was no time; and she remembered thinking, quite clearly, _I'll come back for him_, and knowing as she thought it that she wouldn't be the one who did.

She remembered coming through the collar into the _Patience_ and the noise. The drum was still running; nobody had thought to stop the dip. Over its thunder came other sounds, shouting, boots, a high thin screaming somewhere aft that went on and on and then stopped. Riggers were running in the corridors with spanners and pry bars and lengths of chain. Somebody had broken open the stores and there was a smell of spilled fuel. On the wall by the forward stair, somebody had already chalked, in big ragged capitals, OURS.

She remembered the bridge.

Hale had taken it. He had said he would and he had. His trusties stood at its two doors with their shock-sticks and the two old flechette carbines the Combine had forgotten it issued, and inside, at the consoles, there were riggers in grey coveralls who did not know how to use the consoles and were afraid to touch them, and in the middle of the deck, very calm, with his slate under his arm, stood Corwin Hale.

At his feet were two bodies.

One was a Combine marine, a big man, face down, with the back of his head wrong. The other was a clerk. Maren knew him: a thin, prim, middle-aged man called Ferrand who kept the _Clement_'s cargo books and had come across to the _Patience_ most afternoons to argue with the dock section about manifests. He was lying on his back with his eyes open and his hands still half raised in front of his chest, palms out, the way you hold your hands when you are saying _please_.

Bram Kovač was standing over him. There was blood on the end of Bram's shock-stick.

"What happened?" Maren said.

"The marine came at us from the lift," said Hale. "Bram dealt with him." He nodded at the clerk. "This one came after. He was trying to get to the comms."

"He had his hands up."

"He was trying to get to the comms," Hale said again, patiently. "If he'd got to the comms he'd have raised the _Admonition_. On the long beacon. They'd have known by the morning." He looked at her. "We couldn't have that, Chief."

"He had his hands up, Hale."

"I know," said Bram Kovač. He was twenty-two and his face was white and blank and his eyes were very bright. "I saw. I did it anyway." He looked down at the clerk. "He was going to tell them."

Abel was there.

Maren hadn't seen him at first, because he was standing in the corner by the navigation console with his back to the wall, the way he stood at the edge of every crowd, and because he was so still. He was in his rigger's coveralls with a spanner in one hand, hanging down by his leg. He was looking at the clerk's body. He didn't look at her.

"Where were you?" she said.

He looked up. His face was grey. "Here," he said. "With the warden. He asked me." He swallowed. "I was on the lift door. With Bram. When the marine came."

"Did you hit him?"

"No." He looked down at the spanner as if he had just noticed it. "No. Bram did. I just—I was there."

"The rest of them," said Hale, who had been waiting for this to finish, "are in the _Clement_'s crew deck. Four marines, eleven hands, the cargo master. They put up a fight at the stair and then they didn't. We've lost two of ours. Jory from the Bucket crew and one of the dock girls, I don't know her name." He made a note on his slate. "And you've lost Sorell, I think."

"Danek's dead."

"I'm sorry." Hale said it the way he had said it to her in the drum hall the day Lusine went into Gall, with exactly the same soft regret. "He was a good rigger." He tucked the slate under his arm. "The question now is what we do with them. The crew. The marines. The factor." He nodded past her, to where Quist stood in the bridge doorway between two riggers with a cut over his eye, in his dark coat and his sleeping clothes, quite calm. "We can't keep them. We haven't the air. We haven't the people to guard them. And every one of them will talk, the moment the _Admonition_ gets here." He looked at her. "I'd put them in the _Clement_'s cargo bay and open the outer doors. It's quick. It's quiet. Nobody has to see."

The bridge went very still.

"No," said Maren.

"Chief, think about it—"

"I have thought about it. No." She looked round the bridge, at Hale and Bram and the riggers at the consoles and the trusties at the doors and her brother in his corner. She was holding the key in her fist. She could feel its small hard teeth biting into her palm. "Lock them in the cargo bay. Doors shut. Give them water and the floor ration. Nobody opens anything."

"They'll talk."

"Then they'll talk," said Maren. "The _Admonition_'s coming whatever they say. Let it come and find them alive."

Hale looked at her for a long moment. Then he nodded, very slightly, as if she had made an interesting move in a game, and turned to Bram and gave the orders.

Teska came at dawn, helped up the bridge ladder by two young runners. She stood in the doorway in her two coats and looked at the bodies and the riggers and the trusties and the strange new faces at the consoles, and at Maren, sitting on the deck against the navigation console with her head on her knees.

"Well," said Teska. "Now she's ours."

Then she sat down beside Maren on the deck and put her old claw of a hand on Maren's knee, and they sat there together while the drum ran down at last, and the line came in, and the Bucket came home with its little useless load of night cloud; and somewhere in the comms, faintly, on the open band from the ice, they could hear the Camp. Somebody on the Kettle side had heard. Somebody had told somebody. They were cheering. Two thousand households, in the warrens of the third shaft and the second and the first, cheering on an open channel at the break of an ochre day, because the _Patience_ was theirs.

* * *

They put the cryostat in the infirmary, because it was the warmest room on the ship, and Ekene said that was the stupidest reason he had ever heard and then, when Maren explained, went white and said it was also the most sensible.

"It wakes when it's warm," Maren said. "Quist says. So it needs its own power. Its cells have three days. After that it needs a socket. The infirmary has the best sockets on the ship and the best backup power and you." She looked at him. "And you know what it is."

"I know what it is," said Ekene.

The cryostat stood in the surgical bay behind the curtain, plugged into the bay's dedicated power line, its ring of status lights glowing green, humming. Ekene had put a sheet over it. He said he couldn't work with it looking at him. Then, an hour later, he had taken the sheet off again, because, he said, it was worse not seeing.

Quist sat on the end of the nearest bunk with a dressing over his eye and his wrists tied in front of him with a length of medical tubing, which was Ekene's idea of restraint. Two riggers waited outside the door. Maren had sent them out. She sat on the bunk across from the factor with the key on its cord round her own neck now, under her coverall, where she could feel it against her breastbone, cold.

"Tell me what it does," she said.

"You know what it does. Your medic knows."

"Tell me how. Tell me how to kill it."

Quist looked at her with his one uncovered eye. Then he looked at the curtain to the surgical bay, where the cryostat hummed.

"You can't," he said.

"Tell me anyway."

He sighed. "It's an aerosol agent in a suspension, in a ceramic shell, in a cryostat. The agent is dormant below about forty kelvin. Above that, it begins to wake, slowly, and once it's awake it replicates, in a medium; it doesn't need a host to do that, only moisture and warmth, which is why it's kept so cold. The shell is designed to vent the agent at pressure, through a ring of ports along the seam. It vents on the factor's key. It also vents," he said, "if anybody does anything to it the Combine doesn't like."

"Such as."

"Such as breaching it. Drilling it. Crushing it. Cutting it. There's a pressure layer under the outer ceramic; breach that and it vents. Such as heating it. If the internal temperature rises past forty kelvin, it vents, whatever else is happening. Such as unplugging it and leaving it to warm up. Such as throwing it on a fire. Such as putting it in your fusion plant, or under your drive, or in front of a cutting torch. The heat reaches the shell before it reaches the agent, and the shell vents, and the agent goes out ahead of the heat at two kilometres a second, and you've just sprayed it all over the inside of your own engine room."

"Space," said Maren. "Throw it into space."

"It survives vacuum. It goes dormant. It drifts. A cloud of it, spores, so small you'd never see them, drifting round Gall for ten thousand years, in the same orbits as your ships, waiting for one of your intakes to swallow a breath of it." He shook his head. "The Combine designs very well, Chief. They designed this so that the people they use it on can't take it away from them and turn it round. And so that nobody who captures it can get rid of it. They call it _spite engineering_. I've heard them laugh about it in the Halloran mess."

"There's always something."

"There is one thing," said Quist slowly. "In theory."

"What?"

"Pressure and heat. Together. Sustained. Enough pressure that it can't vent, enough heat that it can't survive, for long enough to cook it through." He nodded toward the deck, toward the keel, toward what was below the keel. "The deep of a gas giant would do it. Gall would do it. A thousand kilometres down. Two. Where the air's as thick as water and as hot as a forge." He paused. "But you'd have to get it there cold. You'd have to get it down through the upper atmosphere, through the cloud-tops, through your own skim layer where your Bucket feeds, faster than the heat of entry can wake it. If it woke in the upper cloud it would vent there. Into the layer you dip. And your next load would bring it home to you." He looked at her. "Your Bucket can't go deep enough. Your line can't. Nothing you have can. Nothing anyone has can, that I know of, except a falling mountain."

Maren sat with that.

"Why didn't you?" she said, after a while. "In the hold. Turn the key."

"I told you. I didn't know."

"You know now."

"No," said Aurelian Quist. "I know I didn't. That's not the same thing." He looked at his tied hands. "There's something else you should know, Chief. The _Admonition_'s captain is a man called Lachance. I've met him. He's very courteous. He carries his own Schedule H authority, as every escort captain on an H-carrying run does, and his own orders, which I've read. If the Sanitation Agent is lost to the bonded, he is to recover it. If he cannot recover it, he is to _sanitise the operation by other means_." He met her eyes. "He has a torpedo bay. Twelve tubes. The _Patience_ is a very large target, and the Camp is a very large hole in the ice."

"So holding it kills us."

"Holding it is the only reason he won't kill you on sight," said Quist. "He won't fire on a ship that might have the agent aboard. If he holed you and the cryostat lost power, it would warm, and vent, and he'd be breathing it with the rest of you once he docked to see. So he'll talk. He'll want it back. He'll offer you a great deal. And the moment he knows where it is and that it's out of your hands, he'll do what he's told." He spread his tied hands as far as the tubing let him. "You've taken a sword by the blade, Chief Tsolo. You can't keep holding it. And you can't put it down."

* * *

She went up to the blister alone, at the end of that long second day, and Ekene came after her, because he always did.

He didn't say anything. He sat down beside her on the cold floor under the scratched dome, against the frame, where she and Abel had sat two weeks before, and took her hand. Her other hand was at her throat, on the key, through the coverall. She could feel her own pulse in it.

Gall filled half the dome. The great southern storm was on the limb again, the one the riggers called Old Purple, turning slowly with its single dark eye. And above it, filling a quarter of the sky, the Lamp.

It was enormous now. Twelve days from its closest pass, coming in fast; Teska, who knew, said it would go by inside a day's flight of Gall this time, close as a dipper's spit. Its head was a hard white point too bright to look at, and its tail had split into two, then four, long pale streamers of green and gold fanning out across half the black, so delicate and so vast that it made the stars behind it look like dust. It lit the blister. It lit Ekene's face, beside her, and her own hands. You could have read by it.

"Teska says it goes out past the cold," Maren said.

Ekene said nothing.

"She says it goes out so far the sun's just a star. She says the ice out there is so cold it rings. Thirty degrees off nothing. Twenty. She says it stays out there two hundred and sixty years, and then it comes back." Maren's voice was very level. She was doing the arithmetic out loud, the way she did on the gallery when the trace was walking and the riggers were looking up at the glass. "Forty kelvin, Quist says. Below forty it sleeps. And it comes in close past Gall. Close enough to reach. If you had a fast boat, and a good line, and a tether-chief who knew how to throw."

Ekene's hand had gone still in hers.

"And it goes out," said Maren, "and nobody can follow it. Not the _Admonition_. Not anybody. Nobody can match a thing like that once it's going out. It's too fast and too far and it's going the wrong way. It'd be out past Lachance's reach in a day and past anybody's in a month. And then it's two hundred and sixty years in the cold." She was looking at the Lamp. "Two hundred and sixty years, Ekene. Long enough for the Combine to forget it ever had a thing called Corva. Long enough for everybody who wants to use it to be dead. Long enough for somebody to be wiser than us."

"And if nobody is?"

"Then it comes round," said Maren, "and they'll have to decide. Like we're deciding." She was silent for a moment. "At least it'll be them. At least it won't be Hale with the key."

Ekene was quiet for a long time.

"Teska says it takes the dead out," he said at last. "And brings them back."

"Then let it take this out," said Maren. "And let's hope to God it's dead when it comes back."

She took his hand in both of hers and held it, and they sat together under the cold dome in the light of the comet, the tether-chief and the medic, saying nothing more. Below them, behind the curtain in the infirmary, the cryostat hummed in its ring of green. Above them the Lamp burned on, coming round, as it had come round when Teska's grandmother's grandmother was a girl and before that, for a million years, for longer than there had been anyone to name it, falling in out of the dark toward the giant on its long patient orbit and going out again; and Maren Tsolo looked up at it with her brother's lie and Danek's blood and Lusine's voice and two thousand households in her chest, and she thought, very clearly, the way she thought on the line when everything else had gone quiet:

_There. We put it there._


# Chapter 11: Asset Seven

*The Siege · 2654*

The order comes down on the twentieth of July, on a slip of foil with the Council's seal, and my section chief brings it to me herself, which she has never done before.

Paske stands in the Cells corridor with the slip in her hand and doesn't give it to me at once. She's a tall stooped shared woman of sixty with a long grey face, and she's been running the South wardens for nineteen years, and I've never seen her look at a piece of foil as if it might bite her.

"It's for the eleventh," she says.

"The lieutenant."

"The Council's interrogators have had him four times. He hasn't given them anything they can use. Fleet dispositions. Picket rotations. Where the _Steadfast_ sits. They think he knows." She holds out the slip. "They want his allowance set to the floor. Today. And then below it. A step a day, until he does."

I take the slip and read it. It says what she says, in the Council's language, which is gentler. _Allowance to be adjusted in accordance with interrogation requirements._ There are numbers. The last number is ninety litres an hour.

"Ninety's not a floor," I say.

"No."

"Nobody can live on ninety."

"Not for long," Paske agrees. "That's rather the point." She looks at me with her long grey face. "You don't have to do it, Venn. I can send Ostrander down. He'd do it."

I know Ostrander. He'd do it. He'd set the valve and log it and go and get his dinner.

"I'll do it," I say.

The eleventh cell's valve is set into the wall beside the grille, a small brass wheel under a frosted window, like every valve on the Ninth. Mattias is sitting on his bunk when I come. He sees the slip in my hand and the key on my belt, and he doesn't say anything. He watches me fit the key.

I turn it to the floor. Two hundred and twenty. The needle goes down and settles on the red, and the cell's little fan changes its note, very slightly, the way they all do. I log it.

Then I take the key out and stand there with it in my hand, and don't turn it any further.

"That's not ninety," says Mattias.

"No."

"They'll check."

"They'll check tomorrow." I put the key back on my belt. "Today it's the floor. Nobody dies on the floor."

He laughs, a short surprised breath. "Is that true?"

"It's in the Ordinance."

"Then it must be true." He leans back against the wall and closes his eyes. Already his breathing has changed, a little slower, a little deeper; he's conserving, the way they taught him. "What do they want?"

"Fleet dispositions. Pickets. The _Steadfast_."

"I don't know where the _Steadfast_ sits. Nobody tells lieutenants where the flagship sits. I've told them that four times." He opens his eyes. "They don't believe me."

"They believe you'll say anything at ninety."

"They're right," says Mattias. "I would." He looks at me through the grille for a long moment. "I'll tell you something instead. Something they haven't asked. Because they don't know to ask it."

"Why?"

"Because you set it to the floor," he says simply, "and not to ninety. And because I've been lying in here three weeks thinking about it, and I'd like somebody in this city to know before they find it." He takes a breath. "Come closer. Sound carries."

I come close to the grille.

"I told you about the briefing," he says, low. "The commander, at the end. _If you find it, don't open it._ What I didn't tell you was that they gave us a card." He holds his hands up, a little apart. "A recognition card. Every lander crew. Laminated, the size of your palm, to keep in your thigh pocket with the survival kit, in case you came down somewhere. On one side there's a picture. On the other there's a number and four lines." He closes his eyes, as if reading them off the inside of his lids. "_Asset Seven. Halloran-Vey Combine. Sanitation Agent, Pattern H. Do not open. Do not approach. Report and withdraw._"

"Sanitation."

"That's the Combine's word. The Concord uses it too. It means—" He stops. His breath is coming a little harder now; he isn't used to the floor yet. "It's a weapon, warden. The old companies used them on workers who wouldn't work. You put it in the air and everybody in the habitat goes to sleep and doesn't wake up, and the machines keep going. That's what sanitation means. You clean the people out and keep the place."

The corridor is very quiet. At the far end, the tannery thief is snoring.

"The picture on the card," says Mattias, "was a grey egg. In a cryostat. With those four lines stencilled on it, and the Combine's wheel." He opens his eyes. "My captain on the _Steadfast_'s lander deck had been on the staff before. He talked, when he drank. He said the Combine lost it here, at Corva, the year it lost Corva. He said the Combine's register says _seized by mutineers_. Not sold. Not bought. _Seized._" He looks at me. "Your Purchase, warden. Your three hundred who bought their air. The Combine's books say they took it. And they took that with it."

I hold on to the bars of the grille.

"Why now?" I say. My voice sounds very far away. "Why come for it now? After two hundred and sixty years?"

"Because the lane opened properly again, last decade, after being half-shut a long time. Because the Concord bought the Combine's books and somebody read them. Because somebody worked out that whatever the mutineers did with it, the city they made is still here, and might still have it." He's breathing slowly, carefully. "That's what the siege is, warden. It's not the claim. It's not your accounts. It's that. They want it back. And the Commodore has orders. My captain told me that too, when he was very drunk." He swallows. "If the occupation has Asset Seven, recover it. If the occupation will not yield, _sanitise the claim_."

"Sanitise—"

"With it. On you. If they can get it." He holds my eyes through the grille. "They don't want this city with you in it, warden. They want it empty. With the docks and the refineries and the dipper fleet all still working. They'll ship in their own people from Corran in a year." He leans his head back against the wall again, tired. "That's the something else. That's what I didn't tell the interrogators. I'd like it if you didn't either. I'd like to live long enough to see what you do with it."

I stand at the grille for a long time after he's closed his eyes again. Then I go and do the rest of the round, every cell, every meter, every seal, reading each one aloud into the ledger in a perfectly steady voice, and at the end of the corridor the tannery thief wakes and asks me if the Lamp's still in the sky.

"Yes," I say.

"Good," he says.

* * *

Malachy has run the figures.

He's been running them for three days, it turns out, on an old reckoning engine in the back of the Archive that nobody else in the city remembers how to use, a big cabinet of brass and glass with a keyboard like a church organ's and a paper tape that comes out of its side in a long curling ribbon. The ribbon's all over the floor when I get there. Malachy's sitting in the middle of it on his stool, with a length of it in his lap, and when I come in he doesn't get up; he just holds the tape out to me, and his hand is shaking.

"It's the Lamp," he says.

I take the tape. It's columns of figures, meaningless to me.

"The elements on the Plate," he says. "Run forward, with the giant's pull, with the perturbations, the way the old dippers did it. They come out at this. At now. At the comet that's hanging over the Oculus." He takes the magnifier off his head and rubs his eyes. "Somebody in the founding year scratched the Lamp's orbit into the back of the Share Plate with an awl, and then painted it over, so that nobody would see it until it mattered. Until the Lamp came round."

I sit down on the floor in the paper ribbon.

"Malachy," I say. "I've got something to tell you."

I tell him. All of it: the slip, the valve, the floor, the grille, the card. _Asset Seven. Sanitation Agent, Pattern H. Seized by mutineers._ I tell him what sanitation means. I tell him what Grieve's orders say. He sits on his stool and listens with his eyes shut, and doesn't interrupt once, which in thirty years I've never known him do.

When I've finished he doesn't say anything for a long while.

"Mutineers," he says at last.

"That's what the register says."

"Not the Purchase. Not _we bought our air_." He opens his eyes. They're wet. "Do you know, I've half-known that for forty years? There's a gap in the Archive. Around the founding. There are the dip logs, right up to the November of ninety-one, and the Camp's ration books, and then there's a month with nothing at all, and then the Charter of the Shares, all very tidy, in a beautiful hand, describing the Purchase. And nothing in between. Not a receipt. Not a letter. Not a single record of any sale." He laughs, a dry crackle. "I used to tell myself the records had been lost. Archivists always tell themselves that."

"They took it," I say. "They took the ship. And they took that."

"And they hid it." Malachy looks at the Plate on its table under the lamps, the long cleaned strip of scratched figures, the grey paint over the rest. "They didn't keep it in the city. If they'd kept it in the city it would be in the Archive or in the Spine or under the floor of some shared family's chapel, and the Concord wouldn't need thirty-eight ships, they'd need one spy. They didn't use it. If they'd used it, we'd know; you don't use a thing like that and keep it out of the histories." He gets up off his stool, slowly, with a hand on the table, and stands over the Plate. "They wrote the Lamp's orbit on the back of the founding Plate and painted it over, Isaura. Why would you do that? Why would you make sure that whoever was standing here when the Lamp came round again could find out where the Lamp would be?"

I know what he's going to say. I can see him getting there, the way I used to watch him get to the end of a proof on the board when I was nine, the chalk squeaking.

"Unless," he says, "there was something on it."

We look at each other across the table.

"It's a guess," I say.

"It's a guess." He nods. "It's only a guess." He puts out one spotted old hand and lays it flat on the grey paint of the Plate's back, beside the cleaned strip, the first time I've ever seen him touch it with his bare skin. "The rest of this is words. Under here. I've got letters coming through in the middle now, a few. Not enough to read." His hand presses down on the steel. "I'd give everything I have to know what they say."

"Lucan will want to know," I say.

"Lucan will want the egg," says Malachy.

The words fall into the Archive like a dropped tray.

"He mustn't have it," says Malachy, quite calmly. "Whatever it is, if it's there, if it's anywhere. He mustn't. Do you understand me? Not Lucan, not the Council, not the Shares." He looks at me over the Plate. "You said reserve's at what, today?"

"Thirty-one."

"Thirty-one days. And the Concord's slugs every hour. And the Council in the old bridge with its _measures_." He shakes his head slowly. "A man like Lucan, with thirty-one days, and a thing that could make thirty-eight ships go quiet? He'd never think twice. He'd call it the Founders' Gift and he'd thank them for it in the nave."

"Then we don't tell him."

"I'm an archivist," says Malachy, and smiles, the thin bleak smile I've known since I was six. "My stewards report to his stewards. He's had a man in my outer office since the day the Plate came down. He knows I've had the reckoning engine running for three days." He looks at the door. "He'll know it's the Lamp before the end of the bell. And he'll send for us."

He's wrong. It's before the end of the half-bell.

* * *

The Council meets in the old bridge now, deep in the Spine, below the nave and above the Archive, where the old skimmer's officers once stood over their consoles watching the giant. The consoles are still there, dead and dusty, with brass rails round them; the shared families sit on benches between them, the heads of the forty oldest Shares, in their good coats, under work-lamps strung from the ceiling on cables. The bridge's forward window, which once looked out at Gall, looks out now at sixty metres of ice, black and shining.

They don't invite us to sit. Malachy and I stand at the back by the door, between two stewards, and Lucan Strand stands at the front by the old helm, with his son beside him, and listens to Malachy tell the Council what the numbers on the back of the Plate are.

Malachy tells them the truth, as far as it goes. He tells them the numbers are an orbit and the orbit is the Lamp's, written in the founding year, and painted over. He doesn't say anything about Asset Seven. He doesn't say anything about mutineers. He doesn't look at me. When he's finished, the forty heads of the Shares sit on their benches in the lamp-light very quietly, and then one of them, an old woman in a fur collar near the front, says "_The Lamp comes round_," in a wondering voice, and somebody else hushes her.

Lucan lets the silence go on a little.

"I'll tell you what I think it means," he says.

He doesn't raise his voice. He never does. He stands with one hand on the old helm, the rail polished bright by two centuries of hands, and talks to them the way he talks to the nave on feast days, warm and certain and sad.

"I think the founders knew," he says. "I think they knew that a day would come when this city would be in danger. They'd thrown out a company. They knew companies. They knew a company that has lost something never stops wanting it back. And I think that in the year of the Purchase they made provision for that day, as careful people do, and put it where no company could ever reach it, and wrote down for us where to find it, on the one thing in this city they knew would never be thrown away." He lifts his hand from the helm. "I think the founders left us a gift. And I think it's on the Lamp."

The bridge murmurs.

"What kind of gift, Lucan?" says a man on the left, heavy, red-faced, a refinery Share.

"I don't know," says Lucan. "A weapon, I'd think. Something the Company had, that the founders took from them. Something that would let a city of a hundred and eighty thousand stand against a company with ships." He smiles very slightly. "Something they were wise enough not to keep in the house."

"A weapon," the red-faced man says, "that's been sitting on a comet for two hundred and sixty years."

"In the cold. Where nothing changes." Lucan turns to his son. "Corin?"

Corin Strand steps forward. He's in his flight jacket, as always, and he looks the way he looks in the posters: tall, fair, all cheekbones, with a pilot's squint lines round his eyes even though he's twenty-eight. He doesn't look like a man who has ever been afraid of anything, and I think he probably hasn't.

"The Lamp makes its closest pass to Gall on the ninth of August," he says. He has a clear, quick, slightly impatient voice. "Twenty days. It's coming in fast and it'll go out fast; after the eleventh or twelfth, nothing we've got could catch it. But for about four days either side of the ninth it'll be close enough to reach with a dipper skiff, if the skiff goes in deep round Gall and uses the giant to throw it. The old way." He shrugs. "It's the run my great-grandfather used to talk about. Nobody's done it in a century. I've done it in the simulator forty times."

"And the Concord's pickets?"

"The Lamp's coma is thousands of kilometres across and full of dust. Their sensors will be half-blind in it. If we go out under the tail, with the Lamp between us and the fleet, we'll be a fleck in a snowstorm." He grins. "Mostly."

"You'd need to know where their pickets sit," says the woman in the fur collar.

"We have a Concord lieutenant in the Cells," says Lucan smoothly. "A lander pilot. He'll know."

I keep my face very still.

"And somebody who can read what the founders wrote," Lucan goes on. "Brother Malachy has done remarkable work, but the back of the Plate is still mostly under paint, and the Archivist tells me it will take a week or more of work to clear it. We don't have a week. The skiff must go by the end of the month. So the Plate goes with the skiff, and somebody goes with the Plate, to finish the work in flight and read us the founders' directions when they're needed." He looks across the bridge, over the heads of the forty Shares, at the door. "Brother Malachy is seventy-six and could not survive the sling. His pupil is thirty-one, a warden with surface rating, and has been at the Plate since the day it fell."

Every head in the bridge turns to look at me.

"You'll go, warden," says Lucan Strand. It isn't a question. "With my son, and a rigger of his choosing, and the lieutenant to show you the pickets. You'll find what the founders left us and bring it home."

I don't say anything. I stand there between the stewards and look at him, and he looks back at me with his silver tired face; and then, while I'm still looking for my voice, he turns back to the benches and goes on, in exactly the same voice, as if he were moving to the next item on a list.

"There is the other matter," he says. "Reserve stood at thirty-one days this morning. The Concord took out the fourth intake overnight. At the present rate of loss, reserve will fall below twenty within the week." The bridge has gone very still. "You all know what that means. We've discussed it in this room before. The Council adopted the provision in principle ten days ago. I'm asking you now to adopt it in fact, to take effect when the reserve reaches twenty."
