The red-faced refinery man looks at his hands. The woman in the fur collar looks at the black window. Nobody says anything.

A steward hands a slate down the front bench. It goes along the row, from hand to hand. I can see it from the back, over their shoulders: a list. A short list, a dozen lines. The Ninth Tier, North and South. The Eighth Tier, upper levels. Parts of the Seventh. Numbers beside each one. Thirty-eight thousand. Forty.

"Relocation to the surface shelters," says Lucan. "The old Rime domes. They're sound. They're not warm, but they're sound, and the Council will supply them with what air can be spared. It isn't what any of us would wish. It is a temporary measure, until the dipper fleet can fly again. It is the only way the city lives." He looks round the bridge. "The founders understood that. I think it's what they would have done. The few for the many. Cut the line to save the ship."

"_Cut the line to save the ship,_" says somebody on the back bench, quietly, the way you'd repeat a proverb, and a few others murmur it after him.

They vote. They vote by raising their hands. Thirty-four hands go up.

I watch them go up, one by one, the good wool sleeves of the forty oldest Shares, and I think of the Ninth South, and the eleventh door with its crooked painted number, and a grandmother with her hands folded on a blanket, and a girl in cracked grey gloves wiping the frost off a meter. I think of my mother at her bench under her lamp, picking soot out of a filter with a pick.

"First Shareholder," I say.

It's very loud. The whole bridge turns again. Malachy, beside me, closes his eyes.

Lucan raises his silver eyebrows. "Warden."

"My mother lives on the Ninth South," I say. "Ama Venn. Filter-mender. She's sixty-three and she's had the ice cough for twenty years." My voice is perfectly level. I have been an air-warden for twelve years. I know how to say a number in a room. "If I'm to go on your skiff, First Shareholder, I want her moved. Down. Today. To a shared tier, on shared air, where she won't be relocated anywhere."

The bridge is utterly silent.

Lucan looks at me for what feels like a long time. Then his face softens, very slightly, into the gentle sorrowful look he gave the Plate when it lay face down on the deck of the nave.

"Of course," he says. "Of course, warden. That's only right." He nods to a steward. "See to it. Ama Venn, Ninth South. To the Third Tier, on Strand air." He turns back to me. "Your mother will be looked after. You have my word."

* * *

That night I go up to the nave.

It's empty. It's been empty since the siege began. The benches stand in their rows with nobody on them, and the old drum lies on its side at the far end with its skin of names going brown under the resin, and the Plate's mount on the drum's end is bare, four sheared pins, a pale rectangle on the scarred metal where the Plate hung for two hundred and sixty years. Ice dust from the slugs lies on everything. My boots leave marks in it.

I stand under the Oculus and look up.

The Lamp fills it. It fills it completely: a blaze of white at the centre so bright I can't look at it straight, and round it, and away from it, the tail, the four tails now, splayed out across the whole great lens of fused glass like the ribs of a fan, green and gold and white, so that the nave is lit by it, faintly, as if by a moon. My shadow lies on the dusty floor at my feet. It's the first time in my life I've ever had a shadow from the sky.

Twenty days, Corin says, to its closest pass. Then out. Then two hundred and sixty years in the dark. The founders looked up at it, from the deck of the old skimmer, in the year they took their air. They looked up at it and they made a decision, and wrote it down with an awl, and painted it over.

_Seized by mutineers._

_Sanitise the claim._

_Cut the line to save the ship._

My mother is on the Third Tier tonight, on Strand air. A steward told me so at the end of the bell, at my door, very courteously. I believe him. I'm almost sure I believe him.

I don't know what's on the Lamp. Nobody does. It might be nothing. It might be an old dipper's bones and a joke scratched on a rock. It might be a gift. It might be a grey egg in a cryostat with four lines stencilled on it and the Combine's wheel. I stand in the comet's light in the empty nave and I find, to my surprise, that I'm not frightened of it, whatever it is. I'm frightened of the thirty-four hands. I'm frightened of the list on the slate.

"Whatever you are," I tell the Lamp, out loud, in the empty nave, "I'm coming."

It doesn't answer. It burns on, coming round, as it has come round for a million years; and somewhere under it, out past the Concord's pickets and Corin's simulator runs and the old dippers' sling, somewhere on that long bright falling body of ice, is the thing my city was built on.


# Chapter 12: The Register

*The Last Return · 2917*

On the twenty-fourth, six days from the Lamp, Hollis Vey sends Cato Marchetti forward to sit with Oz in the engine space, and asks Til to come aft.

She finds Juno already there. She's sitting cross-legged on the deck beside the cradle with one of the _Dray_'s old maintenance manuals open on her knees, the thick foil one for the winch assembly, and she looks up when Til comes in and then down again, and doesn't get up. Hollis is propped higher on his pillows than usual. His face is worse. In the last two days it's gone from yellow-grey to grey, and the skin over his cheekbones has gone tight and shiny, and his breathing, through the cradle's speaker grille, has a catch in it now on every inbreath, like a hinge.

"I asked Juno to stay," he says. "I hope you don't mind. I find I'd rather say this to two people than one. It's less like confession."

Til sits on the end of the bunk.

"There's a case," says Hollis, "in the rack under the cradle. A black one. Would you?"

She finds it. It's an old document case of black hide, scuffed white at the corners, with a brass catch. Inside, sealed in clear sleeves, there are papers. Real paper, some of it, brown and soft as cloth; and foil, old foil, the grey printed kind she saw on Platform Four, in Juno's hands, in the frozen duty office.

"The top one," says Hollis.

The top one is a single sheet of foil in its sleeve, printed in the same cramped old type. A heading, with the green wheel. Columns. Most of it is codes. Near the bottom of the page, a line has been marked, very long ago, with a pen, in the margin.

_CORVA OPERATION. Status: LOST, 2391. Assets not recovered:_

_Asset 7. Sanitation Agent, Pattern H (one unit). Seized by bonded mutineers 17.xi.2391. Not recovered. Disposition unknown._

Til reads it twice and hands it to Juno without a word. Juno reads it, her lips moving very slightly, the way her mother's used to.

"That's a loss register," says Hollis. "The Combine kept one for every operation. That's the page for Corva. It's the only page for Corva that survived the Concord's archives, and I paid more for it than I paid for my house." He breathes, with the hinge in it. "The next one down is a memorandum. From the Combine's operations board to its factors, about twelve years earlier. You should read it. Both of you. I've read it a thousand times and it doesn't get easier, so there's no point in waiting for it to."

Til reads it. It's longer, four pages. It's about a place called Tamsin's Rock, and a strike, and a factor who exercised something called Schedule H, and the results, which are given in a table. There are four thousand and eleven people in the table. There is a column headed _Time of death (est.)_, filled in by hand, by four different hands, for every one of them. At the end there's a paragraph about resettlement, and a figure for the operation's profit eighteen months later, and a sentence recommending the episode to all factors _as an example of what may be achieved by decisive use of contingency authority_.

Juno reads it after her. She reads it very slowly. When she gets to the table she stops, and Til watches her look at it, the long columns of names and times in faded ink, and sees her face change, sees her understand what kind of ledger it is.

"That's what Asset Seven is," says Hollis Vey. "That's what my family made, or bought, or had made for them; I've never found out which. That's what my family took to Corva, in twenty-three ninety-one, to use on its own workers if they didn't go quietly when they were sold. They didn't go quietly. They took it." His wet blue eyes go to the port, where the Lamp is a blaze. "And then nobody knows."

"And you think it's on the Lamp," says Til.

"I think it's on the Lamp."

"Why?"

"Because it isn't anywhere else." He smiles his too-large smile, but there's no amusement in it now. "Because the mutineers took it, and the Combine never got it back, and the Concord came for it two hundred and sixty years later in the next year the Lamp was in the sky, and the Concord's books on that year are a wall of ash. Because the dippers who came out of Corva carried a saying about the Lamp that's on the wall of a fuel depot in Gall orbit and on the wall of every Roundsman's chapel in Tarn: _what goes out comes back_. Because the Tarn docks tell a story about a hoard on a comet, and dock stories are always about something, even when they're wrong about what. Because if I'd been a dipper in twenty-three ninety-one with a thing like that in my hands and nothing I could do to kill it, and a comet going out into the cold for two hundred and sixty years passing close enough to touch, I'd have put it there." He closes his eyes. "I've had thirty years to think about it, Captain. That's where I'd have put it."

Nobody says anything. The _Dray_'s air plant coughs.

"You lied to us," says Juno. Her voice is very quiet. "You said it was your family's. Something your family lost. You let us think it was money."

"It is my family's," says Hollis. "And I did lose it. I told you no lies, child. I told you very little truth, which isn't the same." He opens his eyes and looks at her, and his face is suddenly very old and very tired. "Would you have come? Would your aunt? Through a raw lane, to fetch the thing in that table, for a dying man who wants to see it before he goes?"

"Why do you want to see it?" says Til.

"Because it's ours. Because it's the last thing of ours in the world." His hand moves on the blanket, the long bony fingers, as if feeling for something. "I don't want to sell it, Captain, if that's what you're thinking. I don't want to use it. I'm seventy-one years old and I'm dying of a disease the doctors in Tarn can name and can't spell, and I have no children and nobody to leave anything to. I want to find it. And I want it gone. Gone where nobody can ever find it again. Into the giant, with the rest of the Lamp, where it should have gone five hundred years ago." He takes a breath, the hinge catching. "My family has a long debt, Captain. I'd like to pay one line of it before I die. Just one."

* * *

She tells them in the galley. All of them, Oz and Juno and Marchetti, crowded round the little table with the cups of Oz's terrible coffee; Hollis on the intercom from the cradle, listening, saying nothing. She tells them plainly, the way she'd brief a salvage: what Asset Seven is, what it did at Tamsin's Rock, what the Combine's register says, what Hollis thinks. She shows them the memorandum. Oz reads it with his lips pressed tight together and puts it down very carefully, face down, on the table.

Marchetti doesn't read it. He sits with his hands round his cup and looks at Til with his quiet attentive face and says nothing at all, and his face doesn't change, and that's the thing Til notices, afterward, when she has time to notice things: that his face doesn't change, not even a little, not even the way a face changes when it hears about four thousand dead in their bunks. As if it had already heard.

"You'd have taken us to a plague," says Juno.

She's standing against the galley hatch with her arms folded. She hasn't sat down.

"I didn't know," says Til.

"He did." Juno jerks her chin at the intercom grille. "He knew the whole time. Thirty years, he says. He brought us all this way, through that lane, with the coolant going and the shears, to fetch _that_, and he let us think it was some old company's money." She looks round the galley. "And now what? Now we land on a comet that's coming apart, and dig up a thing that kills people in their sleep, and do what with it? Carry it home? In here?" She slaps the bulkhead. "Next to the galley?"

"We don't know it's there," says Oz mildly.

"We don't know it isn't."

"No."

"Juno," says Til.

"Don't." Juno turns on her. "Don't do your voice. Your weather voice. _The line got cut._ Don't."

Til stops.

"Here's what I know," she says, after a moment, in a different voice, an ordinary one. "Oz?"

Oz brings up the Lamp on the galley screen, the long cracked body in its coma, the diagram with the dashed circle round Gall. He doesn't need to explain it again. Everybody's seen it.

"If Hollis is right," Til says, "and it's on the Lamp, then on the thirtieth the Lamp goes inside Gall's limit and comes apart. Thirty pieces, Oz says. Maybe more. Most of them go into Gall on the second. Some don't. Some get flung. Some go round and out. Some go across the moons." She looks at Juno. "You asked about that. On the nineteenth. What happens if something's on one of the bits that gets flung."

Juno says nothing.

"If it's on a piece that goes into Gall, deep, it's gone. Hollis says that's the only thing that kills it, the deep of a giant. Fine. If it's on a piece that gets flung, it goes off into the dark, and the sun warms that piece, and the ice opens, and in a week or a month it's lying out in sunlight on a lump of gravel, warming. And then it vents." She taps the screen, where Gall's moons are little marks across the comet's path. "Into a debris stream that's crossing every one of those orbits. And there are people on one of them."

The galley goes very quiet.

"What people?" says Marchetti. It's the first thing he's said.

"I don't know. I've seen a light. On Kettle's night side, on the twentieth. Nine seconds and gone. And at the Throat, the night we came out, somebody painted us with a ranging radar. One sweep." Til looks round the table. "Somebody's alive down there. Somebody's been sitting under that ice for two hundred years not saying a word on the radio, and they saw us come in, and they looked, and they put their heads down again."

"You didn't tell me," says Oz.

"I wasn't sure."

"You're sure now."

"I'm sure now."

Juno has unfolded her arms. She's looking at the diagram on the screen, at the dashed circle and the little white mark that is Kettle, sitting right across the comet's road.

"So we turn round," says Til.

They all look at her.

"That's the sensible thing," she says. "That's what I'd do on any job. We've been paid ten thousand. The client lied about the cargo. The cargo's lethal. The site's coming apart under us in six days, and there's a repossession cutter sitting off our stern waiting to see what we bring up. Any salvager on the Ring would turn round, and call it a bad job, and nobody'd blame her." She looks at Juno. "And you'd go back to the counting-house, and I'd go back to hauling scrap, and the _Dray_ would be paid off, and we'd be where we were. And whatever's on that comet goes where it goes."

"And the people on Kettle," says Juno.

"And the people on Kettle breathe what they breathe."

Juno looks at her for a long moment. It's the look from the counting-house, from the fourth desk from the end, black and level and without any expression at all; only it isn't, quite, now. There's something under it. Til can't read it. She's never been able to read it.

"Is that what you want to do?" says Juno.

Til opens her mouth to say yes.

It's what she always says. It's what she's said for eleven years, on every job, every time the money and the risk came out on the wrong sides of the line. _Cut it. Call it. Go home._ She's good at it. She's alive because she's good at it. And she sits at the little galley table with her niece standing against the hatch and Oz's big soft face beside her and the dying man breathing through the grille, and she opens her mouth to say yes, and what comes out is: "No."

Juno blinks.

"No," says Til again, as if trying it out. "No. I don't." She looks at the screen. "I'm tired of things going where they go."

Oz lets out a long breath through his nose, and folds his delicate hands on the table, and says, very quietly, in the Roundsman cant, a word Til knows: the one you say at the chapel brazier when you put the salt in. It means something like _so be it_, or _on its way_.

Marchetti says nothing. He's looking at his coffee.

"We go on," says Til. "We land. We find it, if it's there. And then we work out how to put it in Gall, deep, where it can't come back." She looks round. "Anybody who wants off can have the cutter. I'll signal Mother."

Nobody moves.

"Thank you," says the intercom grille, in Hollis Vey's thin dry voice. "Thank you, Captain."

* * *

In the engine space afterward, Oz says, "How?"

He's lying on his back under the coolant manifold with a lamp in his teeth, re-seating the sleeve he put on in the lane, which has been weeping. Til's sitting on the deck by his feet passing him tools. It's the only place on the _Dray_ where two people can talk without being heard: the core's hum covers everything, and the hatch is heavy.

"How what?"

"How do you put a thing like that in Gall. Deep. Hollis says deep. Deep's a thousand kilometres. Two. We can't go a thousand kilometres into Gall, Til. The _Dray_'d be a crushed can at fifty. And if it wakes up on the way down, in the top of the clouds, it vents there, and it's sitting in Gall's upper air for a thousand years for anybody who ever dips the giant again."

"Hollis said that?"

"His register said it. One of the papers in the case. A factor's note. I read it while you were telling them." Oz takes the lamp out of his mouth. "_You'd have to get it there cold. Faster than it can wake._" He puts the lamp back. "Somebody knew. Five hundred years ago. They just didn't have a way."

Til sits with a spanner in her hand.

"We've got a way," she says.

Oz stops working.

"The Lamp," says Til. "The pieces. Thirty of them, you said. Going into Gall on the second, at sixty kilometres a second. What happens to a mountain of ice that hits a gas giant at sixty kilometres a second?"

Oz is silent for a long moment, under the manifold.

"It goes in," he says slowly. "It goes in very deep, very fast. A big piece—a kilometre—it'd go down hundreds of kilometres before it even started to break up. Before it even felt warm, inside. It'd be a fireball the size of Tarn on the cloud-tops and the core of it'd be down in the deep before the flash was over." He slides out from under the manifold and sits up, the lamp in his hand, and looks at her. "A falling mountain."

"A falling mountain," says Til. "We don't have to take it anywhere. The Lamp's taking it. We just have to make sure it's on the right piece. The big one. The one that goes straight in, not the ones that graze and skip and go out across the moons." She turns the spanner over in her hands. "If it's buried in a little bit, we move it. Dig it out, cold, and carry it, cold, to the big bit, and bury it again. If it's on a bit that's going to graze, we push."

"Push."

"The _Dray_'s a tug, Oz. That's what she does. She pushes things."

Oz looks at her for a long time. Then he starts to laugh. It's a big soft wheezing laugh, his real one, the one she hasn't heard since Cinderhaven, and it goes on for a while and makes him cough.

"You're mad," he says.

"I know."

"Mags'd have liked it."

"I know that too," says Til.

She goes forward afterward, toward the cockpit, past the galley, past the cargo bay. The bay's dark except for the work-lamps on the ribs, and the comms panel by the forward hatch, which is the _Dray_'s secondary, the one Oz uses to talk to the winch when he's working the boom. Its screen is lit. She doesn't notice that at the time. She notices it afterward, when Juno tells her.

Juno's in the bay. She's in the shadow behind the third cable drum, sitting on the deck with her knees up, in Oz's old jumper; she came down here to be by herself, she says later, after the galley, because the warm bunk was too close to the cradle and she didn't want to hear the old man breathe. She's been sitting there in the dark for twenty minutes when Cato Marchetti comes into the bay from the aft hatch, without a sound, and goes straight to the comms panel by the forward hatch, and stands in front of it, and types.

She can't see what he types. She can see the screen, over his shoulder. She can see the little icon in its corner light up, the one shaped like a narrow cone: a tightbeam, the _Dray_'s laser link, which only talks to one ship at a time, and only to a ship you aim it at. She can see the bearing it takes, swinging round, settling. Astern. Two hundred thousand kilometres astern, where the long black hull with the ram bow sits in the comet's light, running dark, waiting.

Marchetti types for about a minute. Then he waits. A line of text comes back, short. He reads it, and clears the screen, and the cone icon goes out, and he turns away from the panel and walks out of the bay the way he came, and as he passes the third cable drum, Juno says afterward, he doesn't look down at the shadow behind it, not even a glance; and she's never been sure, then or ever, whether that means he didn't know she was there, or that he did.


# Chapter 13: Shares

*The Bonded · 2391*

The Plate was Hale's idea.

He brought it to her on the twentieth, three days after the night in the cold hold, when the ship had begun to understand that it belonged to itself and did not yet know what that meant. The crew had been dipping, because Teska said they must: _we keep her flying, we keep the Camp breathing, the rest is talk._ They had been sleeping in shifts and eating whatever the _Clement_'s stores held, which was more and better than anybody aboard had eaten in years. Kettle Camp had sent up a shuttle-load of children to look at the ship that was theirs now, and the children had run shrieking up and down the drum gallery until Teska threw a boot at them. And the bonded had begun, in small groups, in corners, to argue.

They argued about everything. Who would run the bridge. Who would hold the stores. Whether the Camp's two thousand households were partners or passengers. Whether the prisoners in the _Clement_'s cargo bay should be fed from the ship's ration or the _Clement_'s. Whether anybody was in charge, and if so who, and if not why not. Maren had found herself in the middle of most of it, because people came to her, and she was too tired to send them away.

"They need something to hold on to," Hale said. He had come up to the gallery at the end of a dip, slow and heavy, with his slate under his arm, and stood beside her at the board looking down at the drum. "Something with their names on it. They've never owned anything in their lives. Not even their own debts; the Combine owned those." He nodded down at the drum hall. "Let them sign for the ship. All of them. Every one of the crew. One share each. Put it in steel, where everybody can see it."

"And the Camp?"

"The Camp's the Camp." Hale shrugged his big shoulders. "The crew took the ship. The crew owns the ship. The Camp can have the air. That's fair, I think." He looked at her mildly. "We can talk about the Camp after the _Admonition_. Nobody's going to argue about shares with a cutter off the bow."

She should have argued. She knew it at the time, and knew it far better afterward. But she was very tired, and the _Admonition_ was coming, and Hale was right that nobody would argue about shares with a cutter off the bow, and she said yes.

The welders cut the Plate from a section of the _Patience_'s inner hull on the twenty-first, a rough grey rectangle of plate steel the size of a door panel, with its edges black and wavy from the torch. They bolted it to the end of the drum, at chest height, where everybody could reach it. And on the twenty-second, at the change of shift, the whole crew of the _Patience_ came into the drum hall, three hundred less the dead, and lined up, and one by one, with whatever they had, they cut their names into it.

Hale wanted Maren's name first.

"You led them," he said, in front of the hall, with his soft voice raised just enough. "You went into the cold hold. You took the key. Your name goes first. That's only right." And the hall cheered, and somebody pushed her forward, and she stood in front of the blank grey steel with a rigger's scribe in her hand, a hardened awl for marking cable, and found she couldn't think how to spell her own name.

She cut it in the end. MAREN TSOLO, at the top of the first column, in shaky capitals, pressing so hard the awl skidded on the second T and left a long scratch. The hall cheered again.

After that it went quickly. Teska cut hers, with Odile holding her hand steady. Odile cut hers, and then stood looking at the steel for a long moment with the awl in her fist, and then cut, very small, beside it, LUSINE, and nobody said anything. Pirro. Senna and Ivo, side by side. Riggers, runners, dock hands, galley hands, the Bucket crews, the drum crews. Hale cut his own name halfway down the second column, neat and deep, like the hand on his forms. Bram Kovač cut his, frowning, his tongue between his teeth like a child. Some of them couldn't write; they cut a mark, a cross or a star or a little drawing, and somebody who could write wrote the name beside it. Somebody cut DANEK SORELL for Danek, and somebody cut the names of Jory from the Bucket crew and the dock girl whose name it turned out was Mira; and Teska, watching, said that was right, the dead ought to have their share, and the hall agreed.

Abel cut his near the end. He came up laughing, with a bottle of the _Clement_'s brandy in one hand, and made a show of it, bowing to the hall, flourishing the awl like a duelling sword; and the young riggers whooped, and he cut ABEL TSOLO in big careless sloping letters across a whole column's width, so that the next man had to squeeze his in underneath. Then he turned and grinned at Maren across the hall, the old grin, _don't worry, it's me_.

She smiled back at him. She would remember that, too.

When the last name was cut the hall drank. The _Clement_'s stores held a great deal of brandy. Ekene, who drank very little, stood beside her at the back under the drum with a cup he wasn't drinking, watching the crew dance on the deck of the drum hall to somebody's squeezebox, and said, "They're happy."

"They are."

"You're not."

"I'm counting," said Maren. "Two thousand households in the Camp. Three hundred names on the Plate."

"You agreed."

"I agreed." She looked at the Plate, at her own shaky capitals at the top, at Abel's big careless ones across the column. "I'll fix it. After."

"After what?"

"After the _Admonition_," said Maren. "After the Lamp."

* * *

Odile came to find her the next night, in the gallery, after the second dip, and she had a ledger.

It was the warden's ledger: a fat grey slate in a hard case with Hale's mark on the back, the one he carried everywhere under his arm like a priest's book. Except it wasn't that one, Odile said, setting it down on the board between them. That one was still under Hale's arm. This was its twin, the backup, the copy the warden's office kept in a locked drawer in case the first was dropped down a shaft. Odile had opened the drawer with a cable-cutter at the third bell while Hale was on the bridge.

"I wanted to see her account," Odile said. "Lusine's. The form he made me sign. I wanted to see it in his book." Her face was very white and quite steady. "So I looked."

"And?"

"And I found hers. And then I looked at the days before." She turned the slate toward Maren and touched it, and a page came up, and Maren read it.

It was the warden's daily record for the first of November, the day before the dip. Duty rosters. Ration issues. Two disciplinary notes. And near the bottom, a line in Hale's neat hand, in a column marked _Reports to Factor_: _Informant re: Runner L. ARDA (relay). Unauthorised access to Combine dispatch traffic. Discussing contents with crew. Forwarded to Factor-designate by beacon, 01.xi. Advised: contain._

"_Contain_," said Odile.

Maren read it again.

"He sent it to Quist," Odile said. "On the beacon. Before the _Clement_ even got here. That Lusine had read the dispatch and was talking. And Quist said contain." She took the slate back and touched it again. "And then I looked at the day after. The third. The day after she died. At the accounts page, where he writes up anything he's credited or debited on his own authority. Rewards. Fines."

Another page. Another line, near the bottom, in the same neat hand.

_TSOLO, A. (Rigger). Special duty, warden's discretion. Credit 2,400 marks against outstanding markers held by warden's office. Account cleared._

Maren looked at it for a long time.

The gallery was quiet. Below them, through the long window, the drum hall was empty, the drum still, the Plate a small grey rectangle on its end, too far away to read. Somewhere aft, faintly, someone was still playing the squeezebox.

"He owed Hale," said Odile. "For gambling. Everybody knows Abel owed Hale. Everybody knows Hale holds half the riggers' markers on this ship." Her voice was perfectly level. "And Lusine was seeing somebody funny who was always in debt and didn't care. And she told somebody about the dispatch, and she shouldn't have. And Hale reported her to the factor the next morning. And the factor said _contain_. And the next day her clip went. And the day after that, your brother's debt was paid."

"Odile."

"I'm not asking you anything, Chief." Odile picked up the slate and held it against her chest. "I'm telling you. Because you're the chief. Because you'd want to know." She looked at Maren with her swollen fierce young face. "And because I want you to know I know. So that when I do something, you won't be surprised."

She went down the gallery ladder without another word.

* * *

Abel was in the Belly.

Maren knew where to find him. He'd gone there to sleep off shifts since he was sixteen; he'd gone there the night their mother went into Gall, and she had crawled along the cable tray with a lamp in her teeth and found him curled up between two bearing housings, three years old, asleep with his thumb in his mouth. He was sitting in the same place now, between the same two housings, with a lamp on the deck plates beside him and a bottle beside the lamp, and when she came out of the end of the cable tray on her hands and knees he looked up at her and knew.

She could see him know. It went across his face like a shadow across a hull.

"Odile," he said.

"Odile."

"She broke into his office."

"She broke into his office." Maren sat down on the deck plates opposite him, cross-legged, with the lamp between them. "Tell me, Abel."

He didn't. He sat and looked at the lamp. His hands were shaking, the small fine tremor she'd seen in the blister, and he put them between his knees to stop them, and they didn't stop.

"Tell me," she said again. "Or I'll tell you, and you can say whether I've got it right. I'd rather you told me."

He told her.

He told it badly, the way he'd always told things he'd done wrong, in pieces, out of order, backing up, starting again. He'd owed Hale since he was twenty-two. Cards, mostly. Some dice. A bad night three years ago that had put him two thousand under on the markers, and Hale had held the markers and said there was no hurry, no hurry at all, and then asked him to keep his ears open in the rigger's mess. Just keep his ears open. Who was saying what about the rations, about the Combine, about the warden. Nothing much. Gossip. Hale took a little off the markers every time. Abel had told himself it wasn't anything; everybody knew Hale had ears everywhere; what was one more pair.

And then Lusine.

"She was lovely, Mar," he said. "She was so—she laughed at everything. She thought I was funny. Nobody thinks I'm funny any more, they think I'm Abel. And she came to me in the lock after shift, the first of November, all white and shaking, and she said she'd read something on the relay and she had to tell somebody and she couldn't tell Odile, because Odile would do something stupid. And she told me. About Sorrel and the Camp and everything." He swallowed. "And I went to Hale. That night. I don't know why. I do know why. I was frightened. I thought, if this gets out, there'll be trouble, and if there's trouble Hale'll want to know where it came from, and he'll know I knew, and I'll be—I don't know what I thought. I went to Hale. I told him."

"And he told you to cut her clip."

"No. Not like that. He'd never say it like that." Abel laughed, a horrible sound. "He said she was a danger to herself. He said if she went round the ship talking about Sorrel there'd be a riot and the factor would bring the marines down on us and people would get hurt. He said what she needed was a scare. Something to make her think twice. A slip in the lock, at her check, when she loaded her clip. Something to shake her up." He looked at Maren with wet red eyes. "He gave me a file. A fine one, a jeweller's file. He said, _take the spring two-thirds through. It'll go when she loads it at the lock check. She'll get a fright and a bruise and she'll think about what she says._ He said—he said he'd clear the markers. All of them. Twenty-four hundred."

"And you did it."

"I did it. The night before. In the runners' lock, while she was in the shower block." He put his face in his hands. "It was supposed to go at the lock check, Mar. That's what he said. It was supposed to go when she loaded it, in the lock, with the deck under her. I thought it would. I lay in my bunk the whole dip waiting for somebody to say Arda's clip went at the check and she's got a bruise. I was waiting for it. And then the shear came and the line walked and I heard her on the channel and I knew, I knew, I knew it hadn't gone at the check, it had held, it had held just long enough—"

He stopped.

Maren sat on the cold deck plates of the Belly, with the lamp between them, and looked at her brother.

She had carried him on her hip through the warrens of the third shaft. She had wiped his nose and taught him to read the duty board and stood between him and two drunk dock hands when he was fifteen and had said something smart. She had sat in the gallery for four hours and fifty minutes with her hand on the damper and Lusine Arda's voice going faint on channel four, and run the dip. _I don't think it was the clip._

"Hale told you it would go at the lock check," she said.

"Yes."

"Hale's been on this ship twenty years. He knows how a clip fails. He knows a spring two-thirds through holds in calm and goes in shock." Her voice was very far away. "He knew it wouldn't go in the lock, Abel. He knew it would go on the line."

Abel took his hands away from his face.

"He knew," said Maren. "He used you to kill her. Because she'd read the dispatch and she was talking. Because the factor said _contain_." She stood up. Her knees were stiff from the cold of the deck. "And then he came to the Belly and offered us his sticks."

"Mar—"

"Don't."

"What are you going to do?"

She looked down at him. Her little brother, twenty-nine years old, the same height as her and twice as handsome, with their mother's eyes.

"I don't know," she said, and it was true. "The _Admonition_'s overdue. She could come any hour. Hale has the bridge and the trusties and both the carbines. If I stand up in the drum hall and tell three hundred people that the warden's a Combine informer who had a runner killed, half of them won't believe me and the other half will go for him, and his trusties will fight, and there'll be blood on the deck with a cutter off the bow." She heard her own voice, the tether-chief's voice, doing the arithmetic. "So I don't know. Not tonight."

"And me?"

She didn't answer.

She crawled back along the cable tray in the dark without her lamp, and left him there with his.

* * *

The _Admonition_ came out of the black on the twenty-fourth, two days late, and hailed them on the open band at the first bell of the morning.

She was a small ship. That was the first thing everybody said, crowding the gallery windows and the blister and the forward ports to look: a slim grey dart with a short stubby bow, a third the length of the _Clement_, with the green wheel on her flank. She didn't look like anything. Then she turned, a few thousand kilometres off, showing them her length, and they saw the twelve dark mouths in her bow, in two rows, and the hall went quiet.

Captain Lachance had a pleasant voice. Elderly, unhurried, slightly amused, with the accent of the Halloran schools. He addressed himself, he said, to _the persons presently in unlawful occupation of the Combine vessels_ Patience _and_ Clement, and he hoped they were well, and he regretted the circumstances. He had been informed by beacon of a disturbance aboard the _Patience_. He would be grateful to speak to the factor.

They brought Quist to the bridge. They let him speak. He said, in his grave tired voice, that he was alive and unhurt and being reasonably treated, and that the bonded held the Sanitation Agent, and the key; and then a rigger took the handset away from him.

"Thank you, Factor," said Lachance. A short silence. "In that case, I'll speak to whoever is in charge."

There was a pause on the bridge. Everyone looked at everyone else.

"Nobody's in charge," said Maren, and took the handset.

"I see," said Lachance pleasantly. "Then I'll speak to nobody. My terms are these. You will return the factor and his staff and the _Clement_'s crew, unharmed. You will return the Sanitation Agent, unopened, with its key, in its cryostat, to my ship. You will surrender the vessels _Patience_ and _Clement_ to my officers. In return, the bonded complement of the _Patience_ will be relocated as previously planned, to Sorrel, with their accounts adjusted to reflect the costs of this disturbance. Persons identified as having led the disturbance will be returned to Halloran for the Combine's courts. There will be no other reprisals. You have until the twenty-sixth." A courteous pause. "I would ask you to consider that I am not obliged to offer terms at all."

"And the Camp?" said Maren.

"The Kettle installations are outside my instructions," said Lachance. "Good morning."

* * *

They argued all day. By the evening the argument had filled the drum hall.

Everybody came. The crew. The Camp's delegates, who had come up on the shuttle and would not go back down. The children, again, though Teska didn't throw a boot at them this time. They stood and sat on every inch of the drum hall's deck and hung off its galleries and its ladders, three hundred of the crew and twice that many from the Camp, with the drum still and silent at the end of the hall and the Plate on its end, three hundred names cut in steel, and the whole of it lit from above through the gallery windows by the Lamp.

Hale spoke first.

He stood on the gallery stair, three steps up, so that everyone could see him. He didn't raise his voice. He had never needed to. He talked to them the way he had talked to them every day for twenty years when he read out the duty roster, patiently, reasonably, as if he were sorry to have to say it.

"We have a weapon," he said. "We didn't ask for it. They brought it here, to use on us. It's in our infirmary now, in its box, with its key. And there's a ship off our bow with twelve torpedoes and orders to take it back." He spread his hands. "They're offering us Sorrel. They're offering to try our leaders in their courts. They're offering the Camp nothing. Nothing at all. And they'll keep their word on every bit of it. The Combine always keeps its word."

A rumble from the hall.

"But they can't fire on us," said Hale. "Not while we have it. The factor told us so; the factor knows. They daren't hole us in case it warms and vents and drifts back on them. So they sit there and talk. And they'll sit there and talk until we give in, or they'll sit there until another ship comes, and that one won't talk."

"So what do we do?" somebody shouted.

"We give it back to them," said Hale.

Silence.

"We give them exactly what they asked for," said Hale softly. "The Sanitation Agent, in its cryostat. Returned. With the key. We put it on a crawler, the way we send a dead runner down the line, and we send it across to the _Admonition_'s docking port, and we tell Captain Lachance we accept his terms. And when it's at his lock, we turn the key." He looked round the hall. "Eighty people. Marines who'd shoot us on the gun-decks at Sorrel. A captain who'd let the Camp freeze. And then there's no ship off our bow, and no torpedoes, and nobody to take anyone anywhere. The Combine won't send another for a year. Maybe never. And we'll be free."

The hall made a noise Maren had never heard it make. Not a cheer. Something lower and hungrier, like the drum spinning up.

"Cut them," said Hale, "before they cut us."

People were shouting now. Some of them were shouting yes. Maren looked out over the hall, at the faces in the Lamp's light, and saw the yes on a lot of them: on young riggers and old dock hands, on Camp men with children on their shoulders. She saw Bram Kovač at the foot of the stair with his shock-stick, nodding, his eyes very bright. She saw Abel at the edge of the crowd by the riggers' ladder, grey-faced, not shouting anything, looking at the floor.

She saw Hale look at her, across the hall, mildly, waiting.

She went up the gallery stair. She went past him, three steps higher, and turned round, and the hall saw her, and went quiet in waves.

"I've been in the cold hold," Maren said. "I've seen it. I've seen what's stencilled on it." She didn't raise her voice either. She used the tether-chief's voice, the one that went out on the line channel to runners at twenty kilometres with Gall under them. "Ekene Adu saw what it did. At a place called Tamsin's Rock. Four thousand people. He went through every bunk. Every child. He wrote down the times." She looked out over them. "If we turn that key on the _Admonition_, we're the Combine. That's all. We're them. We've got the box and we use it, the way they'd have used it on us. We'll have killed eighty people in their sleep to save ourselves and we'll be the people who did that, for ever."

"For ever's a long time, Chief," somebody called. "Sorrel's soon."

A laugh. An ugly one.

"There's something else," said Maren. "The _Admonition_'s got a beacon. Every Combine ship does; it talks to Halloran on the long band every day. If she goes quiet, Halloran knows. And the next ship they send won't be a cutter with a captain who talks. It'll be three cutters, and they won't hail us, and they won't care what we've got in a box. They'll put twelve torpedoes in the _Patience_ from ten thousand kilometres and twelve more in the Camp, and they'll write it up as an example of what may be achieved." She looked round the hall. "We can't keep it. If we keep it they'll come for it for ever. And we can't use it, unless we want to be what they are. So we put it where nobody can use it. Not them. Not us."

"Where?" shouted Hale's voice, below her. Mild. Interested.

Maren lifted her hand and pointed up, through the gallery windows, at the blaze in the sky.

"There," she said.

She told them. The Lamp, six days from its closest pass, going in by Gall close enough for the Gull to reach, if the _Patience_ threw her right. Out past the cold for two hundred and sixty years, where the ice was so cold the egg would sleep as long as the Lamp did. Out past the reach of the _Admonition_, of any ship, the moment it was going. Gone. Not used. Not kept. Gone.

"And when it comes back?" said Hale.

"Then it comes back to somebody else," said Maren. "And they'll have to decide. Like us. And maybe they'll be wiser."

The hall was very quiet.

"The tether-chief's right," said a voice from the bench by the drum.

Teska had got to her feet. She was holding on to Odile's arm. She stood there very small in her two coats with her one good eye shining, and the hall turned to look at her.

"I've been on this ship sixty-one years," said Teska. "I've seen the Lamp once before, in the old pictures my grandmother had. It takes the dead out. It brings them back." She looked up at it through the windows. "Let it take this out. Let it take this dead thing out, before it makes us dead things too."

They voted by hands. It was close. Afterward, nobody could agree how close; some said by forty and some by four. But the hands for the Lamp were more than the hands for the key, and Teska counted them, and said so, and nobody argued with Teska.

Hale, on the stair below her, looked up at Maren with his mild patient face and smiled, very slightly, the way he had smiled on the bridge when she told him to lock the prisoners in the cargo bay. As if she had made an interesting move.

"The tether-chief's plan, then," he said to the hall. "Of course."

* * *

Odile went for Abel the next night, in the Belly, with her line knife.

Maren had been waiting for it. She'd told Odile she'd want to know, and Odile had told her she'd know, and so for two nights Maren had slept in her clothes on the gallery floor with her boots on, and on the second night, at the third bell, she heard a shout in the drum hall below, and was down the ladder and across the deck and behind the number four brake and into the cable tray before she was properly awake.

They were in the hollow between the two bearing housings, where Abel always slept. The lamp had been knocked over and was lying on its side throwing a long low wedge of light across the deck plates. Abel was on his back. Odile was on top of him, kneeling on his arms, with her hook-knife in her fist, and there was blood on his face, a long line of it from his eyebrow down his cheek, black in the lamplight. He wasn't fighting her. That was the thing Maren saw. He was lying there with his arms under her knees and his face turned up to her, and he wasn't fighting.

"Odile," said Maren.

"Go away, Chief."

"Odile, put it down."

"He cut her line." Odile's voice was very calm. "He cut her line, and he lay in his bunk while she went into Gall. I'm going to cut his. That's all. It's fair. It's the only fair thing anybody's done on this ship since I was born."

"I know."

"You'd stop me."

"Yes," said Maren.

"Why?" Odile looked round at her, and her face in the wedge of lamplight was wet. "Because he's yours? Because he's your brother? She was my sister, Chief. She was my _sister_."

"Because I need him," said Maren. "For six days. That's all. The _Admonition_'s off our bow and Hale's got the bridge and I have to throw a tug at a comet, and I need every rigger on the drum who knows how to set a sling. He's the best I've got on the number three brake. You know he is." She came forward a step, slowly, her hands open. "After the Lamp, Odile. After. I swear it on the line. When it's gone and the Gull's thrown and the _Admonition_'s gone home, I'll give him to you. I'll put him in front of the whole drum hall and tell them what he did and what Hale did, and you can do what you want, and I won't stop you." Her voice cracked. "I swear it on the line. On Lusine's name on the drum."

Odile knelt there a long time with the knife in her fist.

Then she got up off Abel's arms, and stood, and wiped the blade on her leg, and walked past Maren into the cable tray without looking at either of them, and was gone.

* * *

Ekene stitched him in the infirmary, at the fourth bell, behind the curtain, beside the humming cryostat. Twelve stitches, from the eyebrow to the cheekbone. Abel lay on the surgical table and didn't make a sound.

Maren sat on the end of the nearest bunk outside the curtain and waited. When Ekene came out, drying his hands, she said it. She had decided on the gallery floor, two nights ago, without sleeping, and she had not told anybody, and she said it now, to him, in the quiet of the infirmary with the curtain half open and the green ring of the cryostat glowing through it.

"I'm going to fly the Gull."

Ekene stopped drying his hands.

"Somebody has to," she said. "The Gull goes out on the sling and she meets the Lamp near the bottom of its pass, and somebody has to be in her to land her, and dig, and put the egg deep enough in the ice that it stays cold. Somebody who knows how to work on a body that's falling. Somebody who can think when everything's walking." She looked at her hands. "And she can't come back, Ekene. The sling'll throw her fast enough to catch the Lamp, and then she's going the Lamp's way, and she hasn't got the fuel to turn round. Not by half. Whoever goes, goes out with it."

"No," said Ekene.

"It's the only way."

"Then somebody else."

"Who? Odile? She's nineteen. Teska? She's eighty-three. One of the riggers? A dock hand? Hale?" She laughed, a short hard sound. "I'm the tether-chief. It's my plan. I stood up in the drum hall and told them to put it on the Lamp. I'm not going to ask anybody else to ride it."

"Maren." He came and sat beside her, and took her hands, both of them, in his long gentle cold ones, and held them very hard. "Maren, please. There must be another way. Let the sling throw it. Let it go on its own. On a drone, a crawler—"

"A crawler can't land on a comet. A drone can't dig. If it just hits the Lamp it'll bounce, or break, or lie on the surface in the sun, and warm, and vent." She shook her head. "It has to be put down, Ekene. Deep. By hand. By somebody who'll stay with it till it's cold." She looked at their hands together on her knee. "Somebody has to carry it. It's my line."

He didn't say anything. He sat holding her hands and wept, without any sound, the way Odile had wept in the Belly, and she let him, and didn't weep, because she hadn't in nine years and wasn't about to start now.

Behind the curtain, on the surgical table beside the humming cryostat, with twelve stitches in his face and his eyes open in the dark, Abel lay very still and listened to every word.


# Chapter 14: The Rime

*The Siege · 2654*

The reserve board on Fourth Central says TWENTY at the start of the morning shift on the thirty-first of July, and by the middle of it, it says nothing at all, because somebody has smashed it.

I'm in the Cells when the order comes. I hear it before I see it: every speaker in the Spine clicking at once, the dry knuckle-on-a-table sound I haven't heard since the night of Lampfast, and then a voice that isn't Lucan's. A steward's voice, reading. _By order of the Council of Shareholders, the provision for relocation adopted on the twentieth of July is now in effect. All air-wardens to report to their section offices. All residents of the tiers listed below to prepare for relocation to the surface shelters. Residents may take one bag. Relocation will proceed tier by tier. Wardens will direct you. Please remain calm. This is for the common good._ Then the list. The Ninth Tier, North and South. The Eighth, upper levels. Parts of the Seventh. _Please remain calm._ Then it starts again.

The night before, a slug came down on the main air plant on the far ice. Not an intake. The plant. I felt it in my bunk on the Fourth, the long deep ring of it, and lay awake listening to the city's fans change their note, everywhere, all at once, as if the whole of Patience had caught its breath. The reserve went from twenty-six to twenty in one night.

At the end of the corridor the tannery thief is sitting up on his bunk.

"Is the Lamp still in the sky?" he says.

"Yes."

"Good," he says, and lies back down. He isn't on the list. The Cells aren't. Prisoners are the Council's property, like Mattias, and the Council doesn't relocate its property to the surface.

I'm not on the list either. The Fourth isn't. I stand in the Cells corridor with my ledger on my wrist and my key on my belt and listen to the steward reading the tiers, and I think: _the Third Tier, on Strand air._ My mother is on the Third Tier. A steward came to my door on the twentieth and told me so, very courteously, at the end of the bell. I've been almost sure I believed him for eleven days.

I go to the eleventh cell. Mattias is standing at the grille.

"The launch is tomorrow," he says. "First bell. They told me this morning. The Council's men." He looks at me. "Where are you going?"

"The Third Tier."

"Why?"

"To see my mother," I say, and I hear my own voice and understand, in the hearing of it, that I already know.

The Third Tier is shared. It's warm, and the corridors are wide and lit with real lamps, and the doors have brass plates on them with family names, and the air tastes of nothing at all, the way air should. I've been up here a dozen times in my life, on errands. The Strand household's guest rooms are at the end of the Third Central gallery, behind a door with a steward on it. I show the steward my warden's ledger and ask for Ama Venn.

He looks at a list. He looks at it for a long time, running his finger down it, frowning, the way people frown when they want you to see they're looking.

"No Venn," he says.

"She was moved here on the twentieth. From the Ninth South. By order of the First Shareholder."

"No Venn," he says again. He looks up. He's young, and shared, and he doesn't know anything, and I can see that he doesn't, and that he's sorry, a little, in the way you're sorry about weather. "I've got no orders about a Venn. Nobody's been moved up here from the Ninth. Nobody at all."

I don't remember the stairs. I remember the lift at Fourth Central being stopped, with a warden I knew standing in front of it saying _no movement between tiers_, and pushing past him, and him letting me, because he saw my face. I remember the stairs from the Fifth to the Sixth, which are the old tannery stairs, steep and wet, and going up them three at a time with the stink of the vats in my throat. I remember the Seventh, where the people on the list were already coming out of their doors with their one bag each, standing in the corridors in their coats, not moving yet, looking at each other.

I remember the Ninth.

* * *

They're already moving the Ninth South by the time I get there.

The wardens are doing it. My wardens. Ostrander, and Bek, and the Haldane twins, and old Pruitt who trained me, and three dozen others from the North and South sections, in their grey coats with their keys on their belts, walking down the long cold corridors of the Ninth door by door. Shutting the valves. Not to the floor. Off. A quarter-turn past the red, to the stop, the way you'd shut off a household that had died or moved away. Each household's fan coughs and sighs and stops, and the people inside come out with their bags, because there is nothing else to do, into a corridor where the air is already going stale and cold, and the militia in their blue armbands point them toward the stairs. Toward the South Lock ramp. Toward the surface.

Nobody's running. That's what I'll remember most. There's no riot. There's a woman screaming somewhere on the North side, on and on, but here on the South it's very quiet. People walk. They carry their bags and their children and their old ones, and they walk down the corridor toward the stairs in a long slow line, and the wardens shut the valves behind them, and the fans stop one by one, like a row of lamps going out.

Somewhere in the line, the Lampers are singing. Not loudly. An old tune, slow, in a minor key; a dipper's tune, my mother says, from before the city. The same four lines, over and over. _The Lamp comes round, the Lamp comes round. It takes the dead out and brings them back. The Lamp comes round, the debt comes due. It takes the dead out and brings them back._

The eleventh door on the South is shut. Its fan is silent. I don't look at it.

I find my mother at the bottom of the ramp, in South Lock.

The lock's full. Thousands of people, packed into the long ice chamber under its harsh lamps, with their bags at their feet, waiting for the outer doors to cycle; the Rime is a kilometre out across the surface, and they're taking people out on the big crawlers, a hundred at a time, to the old domes. The surface suits are long gone from their racks. There aren't enough suits in the city for forty thousand people. They'll go out in the crawlers' pressurised beds, and walk from the crawlers into the domes through the old connector tubes, and the Council will supply the domes with what air can be spared.

My mother is sitting on her filter bag against the lock wall, under the racks where the suits used to hang. She's wearing her old grey coat, the one with the patched elbows, and she's got Pilar Ocampo beside her, with her cracked grey gloves, leaning against her arm. Pilar's mother is there too, and her father, and the two little ones, all of them sitting in a row against the wall like birds on a wire.

My mother looks up and sees me, and smiles.

"Isa," she says. "There you are."

"Mama." I'm on my knees in front of her. I don't remember getting there. "Mama, they said you'd been moved. On the twentieth. The steward came to my door, he said the Third Tier, Strand air, he said—"

"Nobody came for me, love." She says it gently, the way you'd tell a child a story it already knows the end of. "I'd have gone if they had. I'd have gone, for you. But nobody came."

"Lucan gave me his word."

"I'm sure he did." My mother puts her hand on my face. Her fingers are cold and rough and smell of soot, the way they've smelled all my life. "He's a man who gives his word very easily, Isa. It's the keeping he's not so good at."

"Come on." I take her arm. "Come on. Get up. I'm a warden. I've got surface rating. I'll take you down to the Fourth, to my room, I'll—"

"No movement between tiers," says a militiaman behind me. He's very young. He has a rifle and a blue armband and he looks as if he wants to cry. "Warden, I'm sorry. Nobody listed leaves the lock except by the crawlers. Council's order."

"She's my mother."

"I'm sorry."

"She's sixty-three. She's got the cough. She won't last a week in the Rime."

"I'm _sorry_," he says, and doesn't move.

"Isa." My mother's hand on my arm. "Isa, stop."

I stop. I'm on my knees on the wet ice of the lock floor with my hands on her arm, and she's looking at me, and her face is very calm.

"You're going on the Lamp," she says. "Aren't you. In the morning."

"How do you know?"

"Dasha Imre told me. She came by last night. She's going too; the Strand boy picked her." My mother smiles. "Everybody on the Ninth knows, Isa. Everybody knows everything on the Ninth. That's the only thing we've got that the Shares haven't."

"I'm not going. I'm staying with you."

"You are going."

"Mama—"

"You're going," says my mother, "to the Lamp. And you're going to find out what's on it. And if it's a gift, you're going to bring it home, and if it's a debt, you're going to pay it. And you're going to stop that man getting his hands on whatever it is." She squeezes my arm. "That's what you're going to do, Isaura Venn. Because you're the only one of us who can."

Behind her, against the wall, Pilar Ocampo is watching us with her coin-flat face.

"I can't leave you here."

"I'm not here," says my mother. "I'm going up to the Rime. With the Ocampos, and the Bekkers from the ninth door, and old Sofi, and the Lampers. Somebody's got to mend their filters up there. You can't breathe through soot." She pats the bag under her. "I've got my picks. I've got my lamp. I've done it for forty years. I can do it for twenty days." She smiles. "The dipper fleet'll fly again. The siege'll end. Lucan says it's a temporary measure. He gave his word."

That's when I start to cry. I haven't cried since my father went. I kneel there on the ice in front of my mother with the Lampers singing on the ramp and the crawlers grinding in through the outer doors, and I cry the way Pilar didn't cry when her grandmother died, all of it at once, and my mother holds my face in her sooty hands and lets me.

When I've stopped, I take my coat off, my warden's coat, which is good wool, lined, the best coat I've ever owned, and put it round her shoulders over the patched grey one. Then I take my gauge off my belt, my flow gauge and valve key on their ring, the warden's tools I've carried for twelve years, and put them in her lap.

"What's this for?"

"The domes have valves," I say. "Old ones. Nobody's turned them in a century. You'll need a key."

My mother looks down at the gauge in her lap for a long moment. Then she closes her hand round it.

"Go on, girl," she says. "Go and get the Lamp."

* * *

The skiff is called the _Wick_, and she is the smallest thing I have ever seen that was meant to go into the sky.

She sits on her cradle at the bottom of the Spine's old dock shaft, in the dark under the nave, a hundred and twenty metres of vertical ice tunnel going up above her to the surface, where the old skimmer's docking ring used to open on to the stars and where the founders' engineers, two centuries ago, drilled a shaft for the dipper fleet to come and go. She's a dipper skiff: a short blunt-nosed hull like a seed, with a heat shield over her belly scarred black from a hundred runs into Gall's clouds, and stubby fins, and a single big drive bell at the back. Four seats in a cabin the size of a wardrobe. A cargo well behind them, the size of a coffin.

"She'll do," says Corin Strand, slapping her flank. He's in his flight suit, with his helmet under his arm, and he looks happier than I've ever seen anybody look. "She's the fastest thing in the fleet. I've had her eight years. She's been round the giant at sixty kilometres a second and come back with her paint on."

"And the Lamp?"

"The Lamp's just a bigger giant," says Corin, and grins.

Dasha Imre is already in the cabin, stowing gear. She's in a rigger's surface suit, very old, very patched, with comet-tails painted up both sleeves; she looks out of the hatch at me as I come up the cradle ladder and nods, without any surprise at all.

"Warden," she says. "Told you it gets to be a habit."

Mattias comes up last, between two militiamen. He's in a borrowed flight suit, too big for him, and his hands are cuffed in front of him with a length of chain, and his face, still yellowish from the floor, is set and pale. The militiamen walk him to the ladder and stop. Corin takes the chain off him himself, with a key from his pocket, and hands it back to the militiamen.

"He can't navigate with his hands tied," Corin says. "And if he tries anything, we're in a box the size of a cupboard with three people who'll kill him. He knows that." He looks at Mattias. "Don't you?"

"Yes," says Mattias.

"Good man." Corin jerks his head at the hatch. "In you go."

Malachy has come down to see me off. He's in his slippers again, and his old grey coat, and he's carrying the Plate himself, wrapped in a grey blanket, held against his chest in both arms like a child, though it must weigh as much as he can lift. He won't let the stewards take it. He walks it across the dock floor to the foot of the cradle ladder and stops there, breathing hard, and holds it out to me.

"Take it," he says. "Take it up. Clean it as you go. The solvent's in the kit, and the brushes, and my notes, everything I've got." His hands are shaking. "There's more in the middle now. I got the middle started. It's words, Isaura. I can see the shape of them. I haven't had time to read them."

I take the Plate. It's very heavy. I hold it against me in its blanket and look at him.

"Read it before he does," says Malachy, very low. He doesn't look at Corin. "Whatever it says. Read it first."

"I will."

"And Isaura." He puts one spotted old hand on my cheek, the way my mother did, very lightly. "Come back."

They fill the dock shaft with smoke at the first bell, from canisters on the surface, so that the Concord's watchers will see nothing come out but a plume like a vent letting go. That's Corin's idea. We sit strapped into our four seats in the wardrobe of a cabin, Corin and Mattias in front, Dasha and me behind, with the Plate in its blanket in the cargo well behind my seat and my hand reaching back to touch it. I can see nothing out of the little port beside me but the black ice of the shaft wall, and then the smoke.

"Hold on to something," says Corin. "This is the fun bit."

The _Wick_'s drive lights.

It's like being hit by a building. I've never flown. I've never been off Kettle; nobody unshared has. The noise comes up through the seat into my spine and my teeth, a roar so big it's not a sound any more, and something sits on my chest and pushes, pushes me down into the seat, harder and harder, so that I can't lift my hand from the armrest, can't turn my head, can't breathe. The ice wall outside the port goes from black to a grey blur. Then we're out of the shaft and out of the smoke and there's nothing outside the port at all, nothing, and then, slowly, sliding into the corner of it as we turn, a curve of ochre and purple that fills the whole world.

Gall.

"Picket at two-ten," says Mattias. His voice is quite steady. "High. That's the _Vigilant_, she's a frigate, she sits on the Kettle-Gall line. She'll see us when we clear the smoke."

"How long?"

"She's seen us," says Mattias.

The _Wick_ turns over. I feel it in my stomach, a long slow roll, and Gall swings past the port and is overhead, and then under us again. Corin is flying with both hands, whistling through his teeth.

"She's coming round," says Mattias. "She'll have a shot at us in four minutes. Go low. Go into the giant. Her captain's a careful man; he won't follow a skiff into the cloud-tops."

"How do you know?"

"I flew landers off her for a year," says Mattias. "He wouldn't let us go below eight hundred kilometres. He said the giant had eaten enough ships."

Corin laughs. "Then he's never had fun," he says, and puts the _Wick_'s nose down.

I don't remember all of what happens next. I remember Gall coming up under us, filling the port, closer and closer, until I can see the storms in the bands, and then the bands themselves, and then the tops of clouds, real clouds, towering, white and ochre and lit from underneath by lightning. I remember the _Wick_ starting to shake, a long fine buzzing tremor through the whole hull, and Dasha beside me saying _that's the shield, that's just the shield, she's kissing it_, very calm, as if talking to a frightened animal. I remember something going past the port, very fast, a streak of white light, and then another, and Mattias saying _she's firing, she's firing short, she can't get the angle_, and Corin saying nothing at all, whistling. I remember the push in my chest getting worse and worse, until the edges of the world went grey, and then a long time of grey.

I remember coming back. The push gone. The roar gone. A silence so complete my ears ring. And the port beside me full of black, and stars, and, swinging slowly into it from the left, so bright I have to shut my eyes against it, the Lamp.

"Through," says Corin Strand softly. "We're through. That's the sling." He lets go of the controls for the first time and flexes his hands. "My great-grandfather would have wet himself."

"We're on the Lamp's path?"

"We're on the Lamp's path," says Mattias. He's bent over the little nav screen, his fair head close to it, reading figures. "Six days to match. It'll be close." He looks round at me, over the back of his seat. "The _Vigilant_'s turned back. She won't follow us out here. Nobody will. There's nothing out here but the Lamp."

* * *

On the second night, Dasha tells the story.

We're all awake. You can't really sleep in the _Wick_; there's nowhere to lie down, and the seats only tilt so far, and the cabin's so small that every time somebody shifts their weight everybody else feels it. Corin's dozing at the controls with one hand on the stick. Mattias is curled sideways in his seat with his eyes open. I've got the Plate on my knees, unwrapped, face down, with the little work-lamp from the kit clipped to the bulkhead over it, and Malachy's brushes and his dish of solvent wedged between my thighs, and I'm cleaning.

It's slow. It's slower than slow. The middle of the Plate's back is where the paint is thickest, and the scratches under it are faint, and the solvent's so strong the fumes make my eyes stream even with the cabin fans on full. I work a patch the size of my thumbnail at a time. Dab. Wait. Wipe. Look. Dab.

"My gran told it to me," says Dasha, out of nowhere, in the dark beside me. "When I was little. On the Ninth North. She said her gran told it to her."

"Told what?"

"About the Tether-Woman."

I keep cleaning. Dab. Wait.

"In the founding year," says Dasha, "when the Lamp was in the sky, there was a woman on the old ship who ran the rope. The rope they used to let down into the giant. Forty kilometres of it. She was the chief of it. The tether-chief." Her voice is low and slow, a voice for telling things to children in the dark. "And the founders had a debt. A big one. Something they'd done to get their air, or something that had been done to them, my gran didn't know which. Something too heavy to keep in the city. And the Tether-Woman said, _I'll carry it._ And she put on her coat, her tether-chief's coat, with her name on the back, and she took her line knife, and she got in a boat, and they threw her at the Lamp with the rope. The way you'd throw a stone with a sling." Dasha's hand moves in the dark, a slow circle. "And she went out with it. On the Lamp. Into the cold. With the debt. So the city wouldn't have to carry it."

"And then?"

"And then she's there," says Dasha. "Waiting. With the debt. Two hundred and sixty years. And when the Lamp comes round, she comes round with it. And when it comes round, the debt comes due." She laughs softly. "That's what my gran said. The Lamp takes the dead out and brings them back. She's one of the dead. She'll be sitting there on the ice in her coat, waiting to see who comes."

Mattias, curled in his seat, says, "That's a ghost story."

"It's a Lamper story. Same thing." Dasha shrugs in the dark. "The Shares tell you the Purchase. _We bought our air._ We tell the Tether-Woman. One of them's true."

"What was her name?" I say.

"Nobody knows. My gran said she didn't have one any more. She said the Tether-Woman gave her name away, with everything else, when she went out." Dasha turns her head and looks at the Plate on my knees, at the grey back of it under the work-lamp. "Maybe it's on there. On the front. One of the three hundred."

"The first line's blank."

"Is it," says Dasha, and says nothing else.

I go on cleaning. Dab. Wait. Wipe. My eyes are streaming. The _Wick_ hums. Outside the port the Lamp is enormous now, so close and so bright that the cabin is full of its light without the work-lamp, a faint green glow on everything, on Dasha's tattooed sleeves and Mattias's open eyes and the back of Corin's sleeping head.

Under my brush, in the middle of the Plate, the paint comes away from a line of letters.

They're scratched like the numbers, with an awl, hard and shaky, pressing. I can see the skid in a T. I bend close. I have to tilt the Plate to the lamp to read them, and my hands are shaking so much I nearly drop the brush, and the letters are faint and half-gone and there aren't many of them, only a few words in the middle of a line whose beginning and end are still under the grey; but I can read them. I read them three times.

_—it sleeps in the cold—_

I don't say anything. I sit with the Plate on my knees and look at the words.

Beside me, Dasha's breathing has gone slow; she's asleep, or nearly. In front, Corin's asleep at the stick. But Mattias isn't. Mattias is curled sideways in his seat with his eyes open in the green light of the Lamp, looking at me, looking at the Plate; and I see him read it, upside down, over the back of his seat, the way I've learned to read meters upside down through frosted glass. I see his lips move. _It sleeps in the cold._

His face goes very still.

Then he turns his head away and looks out of his port at the Lamp, and doesn't say anything at all; and I put the brush down in the dish and sit there in the hum of the little skiff with my hands flat on the cold steel of the founders' Plate, and think of a grey egg on a laminated card, in a cryostat, in the cold, and of my mother in a dome on the Rime with my coat round her shoulders and my key in her lap, mending filters for people who can't breathe.


# Chapter 15: The Broker

*The Last Return · 2917*

Juno tells her on the twenty-fifth, in the cockpit, at the change of watch, in a flat clerk's voice, as if reading off a slate. Til listens without turning round. When Juno's finished, Til says, "Thank you," and Juno says, "I'm witnessing. It's my job," and goes aft.

For three days after that Til doesn't do anything at all.

She watches. She flies the _Dray_ down the long falling curve toward Gall, and lets Oz plan the sling, and checks the anchors and the cable and the suits, and eats in the galley with Cato Marchetti across the table, and talks to him about the weather on the Lamp and the state of the ice and whether the old man will last; and she watches him. She watches the comms panel in the cargo bay. On the twenty-sixth, at the eleventh bell, the cone icon lights again, for forty seconds. On the twenty-seventh, twice. She doesn't touch it. She doesn't say anything to Oz. She wants to see what he does when he thinks nobody's watching, and what he does, it turns out, is nothing at all, except sit by the old man's cradle with his hands on his knees and send forty seconds of light into the dark astern every night.

On the twenty-eighth, two days from the Lamp, she goes down to the cargo bay at the eleventh bell and is standing beside the comms panel when he comes in.

He stops in the hatch. He doesn't look surprised. He looks, if anything, faintly relieved, like a man who has been carrying something heavy for a long way and has just been told he can put it down.

"Captain," he says.

"Mr Marchetti."

"Your niece saw me. On the twenty-fourth."

"She did."

"I thought she might have." He comes into the bay, slowly, and stops at his courteous distance, and puts his hands in his coat pockets. "She's very quiet. I like that in her. I wasn't sure."

"Who are you talking to?"

"Mother Szabo."

"I know that. I can read a bearing." Til doesn't move from the panel. "Who are you talking to?"

Marchetti considers her for a moment with his quiet attentive face.

"The Office of Supply," he says. "Tarn Directorate. I hold a commission there. Major, as it happens, though I've not worn the uniform in eleven years." He tilts his head slightly. "I was assigned to Mr Vey when he began to buy Concord archives. The Directorate takes an interest in people who buy Concord archives. Most of them are looking for money. Mr Vey was looking for something else, and he was very thorough, and very rich, and the Office decided it would be economical to let him look, and to be beside him when he found it."

"Asset Seven."

"Asset Seven." He nods. "You understand the Reach is coming apart, Captain. You live in it. The Directorate has forty years of emergency powers and a navy it can't pay and six systems that would leave it tomorrow if they had anywhere to go. It needs something that no one can argue with." He says it without any emphasis at all, as if discussing a cargo rate. "A thing that empties a station and leaves it standing. One unit. Kept cold, kept quiet, kept in a vault on Tarn. Nobody would ever need to use it. That's rather the point."

"That's what the Combine said."

"I expect it is." He shrugs slightly. "The Combine was right. It worked for sixty years."

"It worked at Tamsin's Rock."

"It worked best of all at Tamsin's Rock," says Cato Marchetti. "Nobody struck in the Combine's holdings for the next thirty years. That was the point of Tamsin's Rock."

The intercom grille by the hatch clicks.

"Cato," says Hollis Vey's voice. Thin, dry, very tired. "I'm awake."

Marchetti doesn't turn his head. "I know, sir. I left the channel open."

"Eleven years."

"Yes, sir."

"You wiped my mouth." The voice through the grille is shaking, and not with laughter now. "You carried me to the window in the mornings. You read me the Observatory sheets when my eyes went. Eleven years, Cato. You were _mine_."

"I was the Directorate's for twenty, sir," says Marchetti, gently. "Before I was yours. I'm sorry. I've been very fond of you."

The grille is silent.

"What happens now?" says Til.

"Now?" Marchetti takes his hands out of his pockets. "Now nothing very much. Mother Szabo's ship has four turrets and a ram and sixteen of her house men, and the Directorate has paid her a great deal more than Mr Vey ever paid you. She'll come alongside tomorrow and dock. She'll take your niece aboard the _Hymn_, as collateral. Mother Szabo always holds collateral." He says it apologetically. "Then you'll fly us to the Lamp, as agreed. We'll land, as agreed. We'll find the asset, if it's there, and recover it, cold, in its cryostat. Your engineer will keep it cold. And you'll fly it to the _Hymn_, and hand it over, and Mother Szabo will hand your niece back." He spreads his hands a little. "And then the Directorate will clear your debts, Captain. All of them. The _Dray_. Your licence. Your engineer's nine hundred. And your niece's bond, the whole seventeen thousand nine hundred and forty marks of it, in Mother Szabo's stone vault. Burned. I've the authority in writing." He pauses. "And a new ship, if you want one. You'd never need to haul scrap again."

"And if I say no?"

"Then Mother Szabo still holds your niece," says Marchetti. "And sixteen house men. And four turrets." He looks at her almost sadly. "I'd rather you didn't say no, Captain. I asked you a question on the twentieth, in this bay, and I'd very much like to know the answer. But I'd rather not find it out that way."

* * *

The _Carrion Hymn_ comes alongside at the fourth bell of the twenty-ninth, with Gall filling half the sky and the Lamp a blaze of white and green so close now that its light throws hard shadows across both hulls.

She's three times the _Dray_'s length, long and black and smooth, with her ram bow and her four turrets and the little ledger-and-pen mark on her flank; she slides in on the _Dray_'s starboard side so neatly that Til, at the controls, feels it as nothing more than a faint bump through the deck, and then the clank of grapples, and then the long hiss of a hard dock sealing. She's flown with a good captain. Mother Szabo always pays for the best.

Szabo comes through the lock herself. She's in a quilted coat with a fur collar and good boots and a pair of thin black gloves, and she looks round the _Dray_'s cargo bay, with its ribs and its cable drums and its work-lamps, with exactly the expression she wears in the counting-house when a client opens a strongbox and it's lighter than expected. Four of her house men come through behind her, big quiet men in grey with stun-carbines, and two more after them, and they spread out round the bay without being told.

"Captain Brask," says Szabo pleasantly. "What an ugly ship."

"Mother."
