"I've been on the _Hymn_ for three weeks. Three weeks, Captain. At my age." She pulls off one glove, finger by finger. "I hope it was worth it."

"Ask your Directorate man."

"I have. He tells me it will be." Szabo smiles at Marchetti, who has come down the bay to stand at a courteous distance, as always. "Major. You look tired."

"I am tired, Mother."

"We'll all rest soon." She turns back to Til. "You've heard the terms."

"I've heard them."

"Generous ones. Very generous. I've never seen the Directorate so generous." She pulls off the other glove. "You'll keep your end, Captain. You'll bring it to me, cold and closed, and I'll give you back what's mine to give, and we'll all go home through the lane and never speak of it, and in a year you'll have a new ship and your niece will be free and you'll wonder what you ever worried about." She folds the gloves together. "Where is she?"

Juno is in the galley hatch.

She's in her new suit, the small one Til bought on Cinderhaven, with her helmet clipped to her shoulder. Oz is behind her, with his big hand on her arm. She looks at Szabo, and at the house men, and at Marchetti, and last at Til, and her face is very still.

"Come along, dear," says Mother Szabo. "You're coming back to the _Hymn_ with me."

"I'm witnessing," says Juno. "That's what you sent me for. I'm supposed to witness the recovery."

"Oh, there's been a change of plan." Szabo's voice is kind. "You're collateral now, dear. It's a promotion. You'll have a cabin of your own." She holds out her hand, the bare soft little hand, palm up, the way you hold out a hand for a child to take on a crowded dock. "Come on."

Juno doesn't move. She's looking at Til.

Til has thought about this moment for a day and a night, since Marchetti walked out of the cargo bay. She has thought about the four turrets on the _Hymn_, and the sixteen house men, and the stun-carbines; about the _Dray_'s one cutting lance and her one old flechette pistol, which is in a locker in the cockpit and which Szabo's men will have found within the hour. She has thought about what Marchetti said: _I'd rather not find it out that way._ She has run the arithmetic every way she knows, the way she runs it on every job, and it comes out the same every time.

"Go with her, Juno," she says.

Something happens in Juno's face. It's very small. A flicker, a sort of collapse, there and gone, the way a hull plate flexes once before it holds. And then her face is still again, the fourth-desk-from-the-end face, the counting-house face, without any expression in it at all.

"Of course," she says. "That's what you do."

She takes her arm out of Oz's hand and walks down the bay past Til without looking at her, and past the house men, and puts her hand in Mother Szabo's, and Szabo closes her soft fingers round it and pats it.

"Good girl," says Mother Szabo. "There. That wasn't so hard." She looks up at Til over Juno's shoulder with her rivet eyes. "You see, Captain? I told the Major. I said, she'll cut the line. She always does."

They go through the lock together, the old woman and the girl, and two of the house men behind them. The other four stay. The lock cycles. Til stands in the middle of the cargo bay and listens to it.

From the cradle, through the grille, Hollis Vey says, very quietly: "Captain. You'd give that thing to the Directorate."

"I'd give anything for her."

"Yes," says Hollis. "That's how my family started."

* * *

In the engine space that night, with the core humming and the hatch shut and two of Szabo's house men asleep in the galley and the other two at the cockpit door with their carbines across their knees, Oz says, "You didn't mean it."

He's sitting on the deck with his back against the coolant manifold, his knees up, his big soft face grey in the work-light. He hasn't said anything since the lock cycled. He's spent five hours in the engine space doing things that didn't need doing.

"Mean what?"

"_Go with her._ You didn't mean it. I saw your face." He looks at her. "She didn't, though. She was looking at you and she didn't see it. Nobody sees your face, Til. You've got it so nobody can."

Til sits down on the deck opposite him. Her legs are very tired.

"She thinks I cut it," she says.

"The line."

"She thinks I cut it on the Harrow and she thinks I cut it today. In the bay. In front of Szabo. _That's what you do._" Til hears her own voice and it's the weather voice and she can't make it stop. "She's right. That's what I do. It's what I've done every day for eleven years. Every bad job. Every time it comes out wrong. Cut it. Call it. Go home."

"You didn't cut it on the Harrow," says Oz.

Til doesn't say anything.

"I was on the boat," Oz says. "On the other side of the hulk. Minding the winch. I didn't see. I told the girl that and it's true." He folds his delicate hands on his knees. "But I had the winch, Til. I had the line on the drum. I felt it go. I felt it go through my hands on the brake. And I know what a line feels like when it's cut from the near end and what it feels like when it's cut from the far end, and I've known for eleven years which end that one was cut from, and I've never said it, because you never did." He looks at her. "Mags was nearest the hulk."

The core hums. Somewhere aft the air plant coughs.

"Her leg was in the coil," says Til.

She says it so quietly she isn't sure, for a moment, that she's said it aloud. Oz doesn't move.

"The line went round the hulk when it spun. It wound in from the hulk end. And Mags was nearest, and it took her first, and the coil caught her round the leg, round the shin, and pulled tight. She couldn't get it off. I watched her try. She had her hands on it, she was trying to get her boot out, and it was winding in, a metre a second, and I was on the line behind her, between her and the boat, and it was taking me too." Til's hands are flat on the deck. "And I had the knife. It was on my belt. Mine. I had the knife and she didn't, hers had gone, it was on her other side, under the coil. And she was shouting at me. _Cut it, Til. Cut it, cut it, cut it._ And I couldn't. I had my hand on it, I had it out, and I couldn't."

"So she did."

"So she reached back down the line and took it off me." Til's voice is quite steady. "Out of my hand. And cut it. Between her and me. And the hulk took her in, and the line went slack behind her, and I went off backward into the dark with my air on half, and the last thing I saw was her face. She was looking at me. She looked—" Til stops. "She looked annoyed. Like I'd been slow on a job. Like I always was."

Oz says nothing for a long time.

"Then why," he says at last, very gently, "does the whole Ring say you cut it?"

"Because I said I did." Til looks at her hands. "At the inquiry. At Cold Harbour. The Directorate's man asked who cut the line and I said I did. And Szabo was there, buying up Mags's paper, and she heard it, and she told the Ring, and the Ring believed it, because it's the kind of thing that's true about people." She shrugs. "It's true about me. I'd have cut it if she'd been slower. I'd have got there. I always get there."

"That's not why you said it."

"No."

"Then why?"

"Because Juno was six," says Til. "And waiting at the dock. And somebody was going to have to tell her what happened to her mother. And I thought—" She stops. She has never said this, not to anybody, not to herself, not even on the long hauls alone with the core humming. "I thought she could hate me. I'd be there to hate. I'd be alive, and she could hate me as much as she liked, and it would give her something to do with it. But if it was Mags—if she grew up knowing her mother took the knife and cut the line and went in, on purpose, and left her—" Til's voice breaks, finally, just slightly, at the edge. "You can't hate somebody for that, Oz. You can only miss them. For ever. And there's nothing to do with it. It just sits there."

"So you took it."

"It's lighter on me," says Til. "I'm used to carrying things."

Oz sits with his back against the manifold and looks at her with his wet old eyes, and for a long time neither of them says anything.

"She'll ask a third time," Oz says. "She's a Brask."

"She won't. She's done asking."

"Then you'll have to tell her without being asked." He gets up off the deck, slowly, with a hand on the manifold, and stands over her. "On the Lamp. When we've got her back." He holds his hand down to pull her up. "Because we are getting her back, Til. Aren't we. We're not handing that thing to Mother Szabo and her Major."

Til takes his hand and lets him pull her up.

"No," she says. "We're not."

"Good." Oz lets go of her hand. "Then tell me how. Because I've been sitting in here five hours trying to work it out and all I've got is that the _Hymn_'s got to come in close to take the cargo off us. She's got to dock, or near enough. Nobody hands off a cryostat across a kilometre of nothing."

"No."

"And the Lamp's going to be coming apart round us by then."

"Yes."

"And the _Dray_'s a tug." Oz looks at her. "And the _Hymn_'s a ship. And tugs push ships, Til. That's what they're for."

Til looks back at him. In the hum of the core, with the house men at the cockpit door and the Lamp outside blazing on the hull, she feels it for the first time since the Throat: the small cold click at the back of the skull, the one she gets on a hulk when she sees the angle. The way in. The bad job that might, if you're quick and lucky and do everything right and nothing goes wrong at all, come out.

"Tell me about the coil," she says. "On the winch. How much line have we got?"

"Hundred kilometres."

"And how fast can it run out?"

Oz begins, slowly, to smile.


# Chapter 16: The Sling

*The Bonded · 2391*

The Gull was a scoop-tug, which meant that she was not meant to go anywhere.

She was built to live in the _Patience_'s belly, in a cradle under the Bucket dock, and to go out on the end of the line when the Bucket fouled, or tore, or lost a scoop vane, and nudge it, and hold it, and bring it home. She was squat and ugly and enormously strong, a blunt armoured box with four big thrusters at the corners and a pair of grapple arms folded under her nose like a praying insect's, and a fuel tank that would carry her perhaps two hundred kilometres from the ship and back. She had a heat shield on her belly for the cloud-tops and an ice auger on her nose for clearing rime off the Bucket's intakes. She had one seat. She had air for four days, because the Combine's rules said a tug had to carry air for four days, and nobody had ever needed more than four hours.

For two days the dock crew had been making her into something she was not.

They had pulled the second grapple and put in a fuel bladder where it had been. They had stripped out everything inside her cabin that could be stripped, to save mass, and welded a cradle into her cargo well for the cryostat, with its own feed from her power cells. They had moved the auger to her belly, angled down, so that she could sit on ice and drill straight into it. They had fitted a spool of light line behind her seat, two hundred metres of it, and a winch. They had checked her thrusters four times. Senna and Ivo had not slept. Teska had sat in the dock on an upturned crate for the whole of both days, watching, wrapped in her two coats, saying nothing at all, which in Teska was a kind of prayer.

The cryostat went in on the twenty-ninth, at the second bell of the night. Ekene walked it down from the infirmary himself on its sled, with two riggers pushing, and watched it lowered into its cradle and clamped and plugged into the Gull's cells, and watched the ring of status lights go from amber to green. Then he stood in the dock with his hands at his sides and looked at it for a long time.

"It'll hold," said Senna. "Four days on the cells. Five if she's careful."

"She won't need five," said Maren.

She was in her coat. The tether-chief's coat, the heavy quilted pressure-coat that had been Hesketh's before her and somebody's before Hesketh, with its hood and its rings for the helmet and its patched knees, and TSOLO stencilled in white across the back where it had said HESKETH for twenty years, and before that some other name. She had put it on in the gallery at the start of the night and come down the ladder in it, and the dock crew had gone quiet when they saw her, the way the drum hall went quiet when a runner's crawler came back empty. Her line knife hung on its loop at her hip, the hook-bladed tether knife every chief carried, in its sheath. She had her helmet under her arm.

The plan was simple, as all the best plans on the line were simple. In the morning the _Patience_ would dip, deep, deeper than she had dipped in ten years, down into the high cloud on Gall's dawn side where the line could take the most strain. The Gull would go down the line, clamped to the Bucket's coupling like a crawler. At the bottom of the dip, with the Lamp coming in over Gall's limb at its closest, the drum would spin the line, faster and faster, with the Bucket and the Gull on the end of it, swinging them out and round in a great arc like a stone in a sling; and at the one right moment, the moment only a tether-chief could feel, the release would fire, and the Gull would fly. Off the end of the line. Up out of the cloud and away, faster than anything the _Patience_ or the _Admonition_ or any ship in Corva could make on its own, on a long curve that would meet the Lamp where it swung past Gall.

And then the Gull would land on the Lamp, and drill, and put the cryostat deep in the ice, where it would sleep in the cold for two hundred and sixty years.

And then the Gull would go on with the Lamp, out past the cold. Because the sling would throw her fast enough to catch it, and not one metre a second more, and she had fuel to land and none to come home.

"You'll run the throw from the Gull," Ekene said. He hadn't looked at her since she came down the ladder. "Not the gallery."

"Odile's running the gallery. Under Teska. I've taught her the count." Maren checked the rings on her collar. "Odile's the best feel for the line on this ship after me. She'll do it."

"Odile is nineteen."

"I was nineteen once."

"You were never nineteen," said Ekene, and turned round at last, and she saw his face, and looked away.

There was a sound at the dock hatch. Abel.

He had a bandage across his face, from the eyebrow to the cheekbone, white against his grey skin, and his eyes above it were red-rimmed and very bright. He was in his rigger's coveralls. He had a flask in his hand, a little dented steel one, and he held it up as he came across the dock floor, and smiled at her, the old smile, crooked under the bandage.

"For luck," he said. "It's the _Clement_'s brandy. The good stuff. The factor's own. I nicked it out of his cabin the night we took the ship and I've been saving it." He held it out. "Mum always said you drink before you go down the line. For luck. So Gall knows you're coming."

"I'm not going down the line."

"You're going further down than anybody ever went," said Abel. "Go on. One. For Mum."

She looked at him. He was standing in front of her with the flask held out, and the bandage, and the bright red eyes, and his hand was not shaking. It had been shaking for a month. It was not shaking now.

She took the flask and drank.

It was brandy. Real brandy, sweet and hot and smelling of fruit, the factor's own. She felt it go down and spread warm in her chest, and she handed him back the flask, and he put it to his mouth and tipped it, and wiped his lips with the back of his hand.

"For Mum," he said.

"For Mum."

"And for Lusine," said Abel quietly, and looked at her with his wet bright eyes over the bandage. "I'm sorry, Mar. I'm so sorry. I can't—there isn't enough sorry. There won't ever be." He put the flask in his pocket. "But I can do this."

"Do what?"

She knew, as she said it. She knew because the warmth in her chest had gone wrong, had gone heavy, had gone down into her legs and her arms like water into sand; because the dock floor had begun, very slowly, to tilt; because Ekene, beside her, had turned and was looking not at her but at Abel, and his face had gone from grief to horror to something else, something that was almost, terribly, relief.

"It's from the infirmary," said Abel. "The green bottle in the cage. Ekene keeps it for surgery." He caught her as her knees went. "I took it when he was stitching me. He didn't see. I'm sorry, Mar. Twenty drops. Ekene says that's eight hours for a big man. You're not a big man."

"Abel—"

"It's my line," said Abel. "It was always my line. You said so. In the infirmary, to Ekene. _Somebody has to carry it._" He was lowering her to the deck, very gently, his arm under her shoulders, the way she had lowered him into his bunk when he was three and asleep in the Belly. "I heard you. I was lying there behind the curtain with my face stitched up, and I heard you say it, and I thought, _no_. Not her. Not you. You've carried everything. You carried me." He was unbuckling her coat. "You can't carry this as well. Let me."

"No—"

"Shh." He was pulling the coat off her arms. She couldn't stop him; her arms were stone. "You've got to run the line. Nobody else can run the line. Odile's good, but she's not you. If you're on the Gull, who throws it?" He got the coat free and stood up with it in his arms, the heavy quilted pressure-coat with her name on the back. "And who's going to look after them after? The Camp. Teska. Odile. Him." He looked at Ekene. "You can't be on the Lamp, Mar. They need you here. Nobody needs me anywhere."

"Ekene," said Maren, or tried to. "Stop him."

Ekene didn't move.

He was standing by the cryostat's empty sled with his hands at his sides, and his long gentle face was wet, and he was looking at Abel, and he didn't move. And Maren, going down into the dark with her cheek on the cold deck plates of the dock, understood that he would not; that he had seen the flask, and seen Abel's steady hand, and seen what was in Abel's eyes, and had made his choice in the time it took her to drink, the way she made her choices on the line, and had chosen her.

Abel put on the coat. It fitted him. They had always been the same height.

He crouched down beside her on the deck, in her coat, with her line knife on its loop at his hip, and put his hand on her hair.

"Run the line, Chief," he said. "Throw me straight."

The last thing she saw was the white of his bandage in the dock lights, and the stencil on the back of the coat as he stood up and turned away toward the Gull, TSOLO in white capitals, going away from her.

* * *

She woke on the gallery floor, with the line singing.

It came up through the deck into her cheek, into her skull, the long low note of the tether under load, and for a moment she was nine years younger and a runner asleep on her crawler at twenty kilometres and somebody was shouting at her to clamp on. Then she opened her eyes and saw the board. The long green trace of the tension, high and steady. The fat amber band of the Bucket's intake, empty. And at the bottom of the trace, at forty kilometres, where the Bucket's dot sat in the cloud-tops, a second dot clamped beside it. Small and green. Labelled, in the board's neat capitals, GULL.

"She's awake," said somebody.

Ekene. He was kneeling beside her on the gallery floor. He had a cup in his hand, and a hypo in the other, and he looked as if he had not slept for a year.

"I gave you a counter," he said. "Twenty minutes ago. You've been out six hours. I'm sorry."

She sat up. The gallery swung round her once, sickeningly, and steadied. It was full of people. Teska on her stool by the board, very small, wrapped in her coats. Odile at the tether-chief's station with her hands on the damper and her face white and set, staring at the trace. Three of the runners. Senna. Two riggers she didn't know well. The long window full of Gall, ochre and purple, the dawn side, the great southern storm turning on the limb with its single dark eye; and over the storm, filling the whole upper half of the window, so bright that the gallery's own lamps looked dim, the Lamp.

"Where is he?" Maren said.

Nobody answered. Odile, at the board, didn't turn round.

"Chief," said a voice from the speaker over the board. Channel four. The chief's private, unlogged, where tether-chiefs had always gone to swear. "Morning, Chief. Sleep well?"

She got up. Her legs nearly went; Ekene caught her arm and she shook him off and went to the board and put both hands on its rim and looked at the trace.

"Abel," she said.

"Here, Mar."

"Come up. Come back up the line. Now."

"Can't do that. Sorry." His voice was quite cheerful, quite clear. Under it she could hear the Gull's cabin, the hiss of its air, the deep thrum of the line coming up through its clamps. "I'm clamped on at the bottom and Odile's had me here two hours waiting for you. We're eleven minutes from the throw. The Lamp's coming over the limb. Can you see it?"

"Abel, come up."

"If I come up," said Abel, "it's two hours up the line, and the Lamp's gone past, and that's it. No throw. Not ever. And the _Admonition_'s been creeping toward us all night, Odile says. She's eighty thousand kilometres off our bow and closing. If the Gull's still on the line when she gets here, she takes it." A pause. "You know all this, Mar. You worked it out. You just haven't caught up."

She looked at Odile. Odile was staring at the trace, her hands on the damper, her jaw set so hard Maren could see the muscle in it jump.

"You knew," said Maren.

"I found him in the dock," said Odile, without turning round. "At the third bell. In your coat. With you on the deck and the medic sitting by you. I had my knife." She swallowed. "He said, _let me go, and I'll go where she can't follow me, and nobody'll ever see me again_. He said, _that's better than you can do with a knife._" Her voice cracked. "He was right."

"Eleven minutes," said Abel on channel four. "Chief, I need you on the board. Odile's good but she says she can't feel it. Not the throw. She says it's yours."

"It's yours," said Odile. She took her hands off the damper and stepped back from the station, and looked at Maren for the first time, with her swollen fierce face. "I can't. I can feel it but I can't do it. Not to him. Not even to him." She held out the throw handle, the long brass lever on its cable that fired the release on the Bucket's coupling, and her hand was shaking. "You do it."

Maren looked at the handle.

The gallery door slammed open.

Hale came through it first. Then Bram Kovač, with his shock-stick, and four other trusties behind him, and one of them had a flechette carbine; and behind them in the doorway, with his hands tied in front of him with a length of medical tubing and a trusty's hand on his arm, Aurelian Quist, very pale.

"Reel it in, Chief," said Hale.

He said it pleasantly. He stood just inside the door with his slate under his arm, as always, and his big hands loose at his sides, and his soft voice raised just enough to carry over the singing of the line.

"Reel it in," he said again. "Bring the Gull up. Bring the cryostat back aboard. It's over." He nodded at the window, toward the _Admonition_, which was somewhere out there beyond the Lamp's glare, closing. "I've been talking to Captain Lachance on the long beacon. Since the night of the vote. He's a very reasonable man. He'll take the Sanitation Agent back, and the factor, and the _Clement_, and he'll leave us the _Patience_. All of us. And the Camp. On terms." He spread his hands. "We'll be the Corva Shareholders. We'll buy the operation from the Combine, the whole thing, for one share. The Combine writes it off and keeps a share and walks away, and nobody hangs, and nobody goes to Sorrel. We just have to give it back."

"You sold us," said Teska from her stool.

"I bought us, Grandam." Hale didn't look at her. He was looking at Maren. "The Lamp was always a beautiful idea, Chief. I voted against it because it's beautiful. Beautiful ideas get people killed. This one's going to get your brother killed, out on that ice, for nothing; because the Combine will still come. They'll come for the ship and the Camp and every one of us, the way you said, and they won't talk, because they'll have nothing to talk about any more. We'll have thrown away the only thing they want." He took a step forward. "Reel it in. Let me trade it. It's the only thing we've got."

"No," said Maren.

"Bram," said Hale.

Bram Kovač came across the gallery with his shock-stick, fast, and Odile went to meet him.

Maren didn't see it. She didn't see any of it, afterward, except in pieces, the way she remembered the night in the cold hold: the crack and blue spark of the shock-stick, a runner going down, Senna shouting; the carbine going off once into the ceiling with a sound like a fist on a drum, and plaster and ice dust raining down; Teska, unbelievably, up off her stool and swinging it by one leg into the knees of the trusty with the carbine; Ekene, who had never in his life hit anybody, standing in front of Maren with his arms spread like Danek in front of the cryostat, between her and the fight, and taking a shock-stick across the shoulder for it and going down on one knee and getting up again. She didn't see any of it, because she was looking at the board.

The trace was walking.

Not much. A slow long wave going down the line, from the drum to the Bucket, the way it went in the moment before a sling, when the drum began to spin up; somebody had put a hand on the spin control in the struggle. The wave reached the bottom. The Gull's dot shivered.

"Chief," said Abel on channel four. "Mar. What's happening up there?"

"Hale," said Maren.

"Ah." A breath. "He wants it back."

"He wants it back."

"Then don't let him have it." Abel's voice was quite calm. "Five minutes, Mar. The Lamp's right over the limb. I can see it out of the port. It's—God, it's big. It's the biggest thing I ever saw." A pause. "Spin me up."

She put her hand on the spin control.

Behind her in the gallery somebody was screaming. She didn't turn round. She rolled the spin control on, slowly, the way Hesketh had taught her twenty-six years ago, feeling it come up through the board into her palms; and down under the gallery the great drum began to turn faster, and faster, and the line began to swing.

You could not see it. You could only feel it, in the deck and the board and the soles of your feet: forty kilometres of carbon braid, with the Bucket and the Gull on the end, beginning to swing out and round from the keel of the _Patience_ in a vast slow arc through the dawn cloud-tops of Gall, like the arm of a man throwing a stone. The trace climbed. The note rose. The whole ship leaned into it. She had done this four times in her life, and never with anyone on the end.

"That's it," said Abel softly. "That's it, Mar. That's lovely. I can feel it."

"Two minutes."

"Two minutes." A pause. "I'm recording something. On the Gull's log. For whoever comes. When it comes round. Somebody ought to know what it is and who put it there and why." Another pause. "I'm going to tell them about Lusine. I'm going to tell them it was me."

"Abel—"

"I want to. Let me." The line was singing so high now that his voice came through it thin and strange. "And I took your coat. I want you to know that. It's warmer than mine. You never let me borrow it. Not once in my life."

"You can have it," said Maren. Her voice was gone. She was watching the trace. "You can have it, Abel."

"Ninety seconds."

There was a hand on her shoulder. Big, heavy. She knew it without looking.

"Chief," said Corwin Hale's soft voice in her ear, very close. "Take your hand off the board."

She didn't.

"I don't want to hurt you," said Hale. "I never wanted to hurt anybody. Take your hand off the board and let the drum run down and let him come home. That's all. He can come home, Maren. Nobody has to go out there."

"You had Lusine killed," said Maren, without turning round.

The hand on her shoulder went very still.

"With a jeweller's file," she said. "Two-thirds through. You told him it would go at the lock check. You knew it wouldn't. Twenty years on this ship, Hale. You know how a clip fails." She was watching the trace, the needle climbing to the mark. "You had her killed because she read the dispatch and the factor said _contain_. And then you came to the Belly and offered us your sticks."

"Sixty seconds," said Abel, very far away.

"I'm going to throw him," Maren said. "And when he's gone I'm going to stand up in the drum hall and tell them all what you did. All of it. And then you can do whatever you like to me."

Hale's hand left her shoulder.

She never knew why. Ekene told her afterward that Odile had come up behind Hale with her line knife, the hook-knife she'd cut Abel's face with, and laid the point of it against the side of his neck under the ear, and said something to him very quietly that Ekene didn't hear; and that Hale had stood there for a moment with the point against his neck and then taken his hand away, slowly, and stepped back. Maybe that was it. Maybe it was something else. Maybe Corwin Hale, who always dealt with whoever held power, looked at the board and the trace and the woman with her hands on it and saw, in that moment, who held it.

"Thirty," said Abel. "Mar. I love you. Throw me straight."

She put her hand on the throw.

"Twenty. Ten."

The needle touched the mark. The note of the line went up to a pitch she had never heard, a long high singing like a bell struck under water, and the whole ship leaned, and she felt it, the way she had always felt it, the way her mother had and Hesketh had and every chief back up the line to the first chiefs on the first skimmers: the moment. The one right moment. Not a number. Not a count. A thing in the hands and the spine and the soles of the feet that said _now_.

She pulled the throw.

Forty kilometres below her, in the dawn cloud of Gall, the release on the Bucket's coupling fired, a guillotine of hardened steel slamming through the clamp that held the Gull; and the line, which had been carrying the Gull's weight at the bottom of its great swing, let go of it.

It was a cut. That was what the old chiefs called it, the throw. _The cut._ You cut the line and let go.

On the board, the Gull's green dot left the Bucket's. It went out and up off the end of the trace, slowly at first, and then not slowly, in a long clean curve away from Gall and the _Patience_ and everything, up out of the cloud and into the black, toward the blaze of the Lamp.

The line, with nothing on its end but the Bucket, sang down from its high note to a low one, and to a lower, and settled.

The gallery was silent.

"Straight," said Abel's voice on channel four, thin and full of static and very happy. "Oh, Mar. That's straight. That's the straightest throw anyone ever threw."

* * *

The _Admonition_ tried.

Maren watched her on the board, all that day, a small grey mark coming round from beyond the Lamp's glare, burning hard, swinging in toward the Gull's track. Captain Lachance had good engines and a good crew and a ship built for speed, and for three hours she closed. And then she stopped closing. She was going the wrong way, as Maren had told the drum hall she would; she had to kill all her own speed and build it again in the Lamp's direction, and the Gull had the whole of a forty-kilometre sling behind her, and the Lamp's own fall to ride. At the fourth hour the _Admonition_'s mark turned, very slowly, and fell back, and lay off in the dark, doing nothing at all.

"She's given up," said Odile. She hadn't left the gallery. None of them had.

"She's waiting," said Teska. "That's different. Lachance is waiting to see what he can get." She sniffed. "Let him wait."

Abel talked.

He talked all day. Channel four held as long as the Gull was in range of the _Patience_'s big antenna, and he talked into it, to Maren, as the Gull fell away from Gall and the giant dwindled behind him and the Lamp grew ahead. He talked about nothing. About the Gull's terrible seat. About the cryostat in its cradle behind him, humming, green. About Mum, and the third shaft, and the time he'd fallen into the refinery sump at seven and Maren had gone in after him. About Lusine, a little. Not much. About the Lamp.

"It's not white," he said, at the sixth hour. "Close up. It's black. Did you know? It's all black, like soot, like old cable, with white coming out of it. Jets. Big ones. Like breath. And there's a crack down the middle where the two halves join, like it's been put together out of two stones. And the light's coming off it everywhere." A pause. "It's beautiful, Mar. It's the ugliest beautiful thing I ever saw."

He landed at the ninth hour, near the bottom of the Lamp's swing past Gall, at its closest. He talked her through it, the way a runner talked a chief through a fouled Bucket: thrusters, contact, the grapple biting into the ice. The Lamp had almost no gravity. The Gull sat on it like a fly on a wall.

"There's a ridge," he said. "On the south end. The bottom lobe. A big black ridge, like a spine, like a broken wall. It throws a shadow. It's cold in the shadow, the Gull's thermometer says eighty kelvin on the surface and colder down. I'm going to drill under it. The south side of the black ridge. Out of the sun."

"How deep?"

"As deep as the auger goes. Forty metres, near enough." She heard him unclip from the seat. "Then the cryostat goes down. And then I go down."

"You don't have to go down."

"I'm not sitting out here on top of it in the sun watching Gall go away, Mar." His voice was gentle. "I'll go down with it. I'll cut a room at the bottom, out of the shaft, a little one, and sit with it. Keep it company. Till its cells run out and it's just the cold keeping it." A breath. "Till mine do."

She didn't say anything.

He drilled. It took him six hours. She listened to it, on channel four: the deep shuddering whine of the auger biting down, the pauses while he cleared the cuttings, his breathing in his suit. At the tenth hour Ekene brought her a cup of something and she held it until it was cold. At the twelfth Teska fell asleep on her stool and Odile put a coat over her. At the thirteenth the auger stopped.

"Forty-one metres," said Abel. "That's the end of the auger. That's as deep as she goes."

"Can you get it down?"

"I've got the winch. I'll lower it on the line." She heard him working. Clamps. A grunt. The cryostat's hum, louder, as he unclipped it from its cradle and walked it, in that nothing gravity, to the hatch. "God, it's heavy. Even here. It doesn't want to go."

"Go slow."

"I'm going slow, Chief." Almost a laugh. "I'm not new."

He lowered it. Forty-one metres down a shaft the width of a man's shoulders in the dark black ice of the Lamp's south limb, under the shadow of the ridge, on two hundred metres of light line. Then he went down after it, on the same line, hand over hand, the way a runner goes down a tether; and at the bottom, he told her, he cut a room, with the Gull's hand-pick, a little room off the side of the shaft, big enough for a man to sit in with his back against the wall and his knees up, and a cryostat beside him.

"It's quiet down here," he said, at the seventeenth hour. His voice was very faint now, through the relay he'd left on the Gull at the top of the shaft, and through all that ice, and across the widening gulf between the Lamp and Gall. "It's quiet. I can't hear the jets. I can't hear anything. Just the cryostat. It's got a hum like the drum, when the drum's running slow, at the end of a dip. You know the one."

"I know the one."

"It's cold. It's not so bad. The coat's warm." A long pause. "I've done the recording. On the Gull's log, and on my own, the little one, the rigger's log. I'll keep that one with me. In case somebody comes down." Another pause. "I told them everything. Lusine. Hale. The jeweller's file. You. I told them your name, Mar. Maren Tsolo. I said you were the tether-chief. I said you threw me straight."

"Abel."

"I said it was your plan and your line and you were going to come yourself and I wouldn't let you. I said it so they'd know. Whoever comes. So they'd know it wasn't you sitting here. So they wouldn't think you'd—" The static rose and swallowed it. "—you're there. You're on the board. Somebody needs to be."

"I'm here."

"I know. I can tell. You've got your chief's breathing." He laughed, very faintly. "Mar. Is the Lamp round yet? Have we gone round?"

She looked at the board. At the long curve of the Lamp's track on the projection the riggers had drawn up for the throw, the falling curve coming in past Gall's limb and swinging round the giant, closest, closest, and beginning, now, to go out.

"We've gone round," she said. "It's going out."

"Good," said Abel. "That's good. That's the long round." A breath, very slow. "Teska says it brings them back."

"Teska says a lot of things."

"I hope she's right," said Abel. "I'd like to come back. I'd like to see what they make of it." The static rose. "Mar. Tell Odile I'm sorry. Tell her I said it. Tell Ekene thank you; he'll know. Tell Teska—"

And the channel was full of static, and stayed full of it.

She sat on at the board for a long time, with her hands on its rim. The gallery was quiet around her. Somebody had turned the lamps down. Through the long window Gall turned, ochre and purple, the dawn side gone round to day, and over it, already a little smaller, already a little further, going out, the Lamp burned on, with its tails fanned across half the sky; and somewhere on its black south limb, under a broken ridge, forty-one metres down in the cold, her brother sat in her coat beside a grey egg in its humming cryostat, and kept it company.

At some point Ekene came and sat on the deck beside her chair and put his head against her knee, and she put her hand on his hair, and they stayed like that.

At some point, much later, she found she was crying. She had not cried in nine years. She cried at the board, with her hand on Ekene's hair and the static hissing on channel four, without any sound at all, for her brother, who had killed Lusine Arda with a jeweller's file and taken her coat and gone out on the Lamp in her place; and Odile Arda, at the next station, sat with her hands in her lap and watched the Lamp going out, and after a while, without saying anything, reached across and took Maren's other hand, and held it.


# Chapter 17: The Black Ridge

*The Siege · 2654*

Up close, the Lamp is black.

I don't know why that's the thing that surprises me most. Everything else about it has surprised me so long and so steadily that I've stopped noticing; the size of it, the silence, the way it fills every port in the _Wick_ at once so that there's nowhere to look that isn't Lamp. But I've spent my whole life, and three weeks of this one, thinking of it as a light. A lamp. A smear of green in the Oculus. And on the seventh of August, when Corin brings the _Wick_ in under its tail through the last of the dust and the coma thins and we see the body of it for the first time, bare, two kilometres off, it isn't light at all. It's black. Black as soot, black as the inside of a filter, black as the old cable that hangs in coils in the dipper docks, a vast lumpy black thing like two stones pressed together, turning very slowly, with a deep dark crack running round the narrow waist where the stones meet. The light comes out of it. That's all. It comes out of cracks and pits all over the black surface in long white jets, like breath on a cold morning, and goes streaming away from the sun to make the tail.

"It's ugly," says Dasha, at her port.

"It's beautiful," says Corin.

"It's both," says Mattias, at the nav screen. "Matching in four hours. Then we need to know where."

They all look at me.

I've been cleaning the Plate for five days. I've cleaned it through the sling and the long fall out from Gall and three sleepless nights in a seat that doesn't tilt, with my eyes streaming from the solvent and my fingers raw. I've cleared the strip of numbers down the left side, and the line in the middle, _it sleeps in the cold_, and a little either side of it, not enough to read. The rest is still grey. I've got perhaps a third of it.

"I need light," I say. "And I need everybody to stop looking at me."

They stop looking. I clip the work-lamp to the bulkhead and lay the Plate face down on my knees and take up the brush.

I go down from the numbers. That's what Malachy said, in the notes he gave me, in his neat old hand: _The numbers say where the Lamp is. Whatever follows them says where on it. Go down from the numbers._ So I go down. Below the six lines of elements there's a gap of bare steel I've already cleared, a finger's width, and then the grey again. I dab. Wait. Wipe.

It comes up letter by letter. The same awl, the same hard shaking hand, the same crossed sevens. A word. Two. I can't stop my hands shaking and I have to put the brush down and hold my own wrist to steady it.

_SOUTH LIMB_

Dab. Wait. Wipe.

_BENEATH THE BLACK RIDGE_

Dab. Wait. Wipe. The lamp's hot on my face. The solvent's in my eyes. Corin, in front, has stopped whistling.

_FORTY METRES_

I sit back.

"Isaura?" says Dasha.

I read it out. I read it the way I'd read a meter. _South limb, beneath the black ridge, forty metres._ The cabin's silent. Then Corin swings the _Wick_ round, without a word, so that the Lamp's lower lobe comes sliding into the forward port, the bigger of its two stones, the one that faces away from the sun; and there, along the bottom of it, catching the edge of the light like the crest of a wave, there's a ridge. A long jagged wall of something blacker than the black around it, broken and uneven, like the spine of some enormous animal half-buried in the ice, throwing a long dark shadow across the ground beside it.

"The black ridge," says Mattias softly.

"There's something on it," says Dasha.

There is. I see it as she says it. Near the ridge's eastern end, in its shadow, almost lost against the black: a speck that isn't black. A grey speck. A small hard angular shape with a faint dull glint where the edge of the sunlight touches it, sitting on the ice at the foot of the wall as if it had been put down there by a careful hand.

"That's a ship," says Corin.

"That's a tug," says Dasha. Her voice has gone strange. "That's a scoop-tug. An old one. That's the old pattern. My gran had a picture."

Nobody says anything else for a long time. The _Wick_ drifts in toward the Lamp's south limb on her thrusters, and the grey speck grows in the forward port, and becomes a box, a squat armoured box with thrusters at its corners and an arm folded under its nose like an insect's, frosted white along every edge, sitting at the foot of the black ridge where somebody landed it two hundred and sixty-three years ago, and left it, and never came back.

* * *

We go out at the start of the eighth, all four of us, on lines.

There's no walking on the Lamp. Corin tells us that over and over in the suit lock, checking our harnesses, his face very serious for once behind his visor. The Lamp has almost no pull at all; you weigh less than a feather. Jump and you won't come down. Push off too hard from anything and you'll go off the surface and keep going, slowly, gently, for ever, until your air runs out. You move by the lines. Hand over hand. Clipped on, always, to the _Wick_, or to an ice-anchor screwed into the ground, or to each other. Never unclip. Never, never unclip.

"Never," says Dasha, nodding. She's done this on the giant's moons, she says, on the ice-rafts that the dipper fleet sometimes chases for water. "My gran lost a cousin that way. He unclipped for a second to get round a rock. They saw him go. They watched him for two days."

Mattias says nothing. He's in the spare surface suit, the _Wick_'s emergency one, an old orange thing with a cracked faceplate that's been taped. His face behind the tape is very pale.

The surface of the Lamp is like nothing I have words for. It's black, and soft, like old snow gone grey with soot, a crust you push your glove into and it gives, and under the crust it's hard and glittering, ice, white and blue and very old. It's broken everywhere into pits and humps and little cliffs, and the light of the sun lies across it so low that every hump throws a shadow a hundred metres long. The jets are all round us. You can't hear them. You see them: a pit fifty metres off suddenly spitting a long silent plume of white straight up into the black, so fast and so high you can't see the top of it, and then subsiding, and another going off somewhere else. The ground trembles, very faintly, under my boots. It's like walking on the back of something asleep.

And above it all, over the horizon, which is so close here that it curves away from us like the top of a hill, there's Gall. Huge, banded, turning. We're three days from its closest pass. It fills a third of the sky.

The tug is fifty metres from where Corin set the _Wick_ down.

We go to it hand over hand along a line Dasha strings from anchor to anchor across the soft black crust, and stand round it, and nobody says anything. It's smaller than it looked from above. Squat and armoured and very old, its paint long gone to bare grey metal pitted like the skin of an orange, its corners rimed with frost. Its nose is down. Its folded arm is buried to the elbow in the ice, a grapple biting deep. Its hatch, on the near side, stands open.

Corin puts his helmet lamp inside.

It's empty. A single seat, very battered, with its straps hanging loose. A board, dead, frosted over. A space behind the seat where something big was once clamped: a cradle, welded in roughly, its clamps standing open. A spool on the wall, empty. Nothing else. Somebody stripped this cabin bare before it left.

On the outside of the hull, by the hatch, half-hidden under the frost, there are letters. Corin wipes them with his glove. Old stencilled capitals, the paint almost gone, just the ghost of them in the metal.

_GULL_

And under that, smaller:

_S.V. PATIENCE_

Dasha makes a sound in her helmet. Corin straightens up very slowly.

"_Patience_," he says.

"It's ours," says Dasha. "It's off the ship. Off the Spine. It's off _our_ ship." Her voice is shaking. "They came here. The founders. They came here in the founding year, in the old ship's tug, and they landed here, under the ridge, like the Plate says." She turns round, clumsily, on her line, and looks along the foot of the black wall. "Forty metres. Forty metres where?"

Mattias has found it.

He's gone ten metres along the ridge's foot from the tug, clipped to Dasha's line, and he's standing over a place where the crust is different: a shallow round depression in the black, a metre and a half across, like a dent in snow where something heavy once lay. In the middle of it there's a hole. Not open. Filled, almost, with a plug of grey frost, glittering, the kind of frost that grows over two and a half centuries of cold. But round its rim, frozen into the ice, there's a line. A light line, thin, pale, the kind riggers use for small loads, coming up out of the frost and running across the surface to a little anchor screwed into the ridge's foot, an old anchor, corroded, with something hung on it: a small grey box with a stub of aerial.

"Relay," says Corin. "That's a radio relay. For talking up out of a hole."

"Out of forty metres," says Mattias.

We clear the shaft. It takes us four hours. The frost isn't hard, not the way ice is hard; it comes away under Dasha's pick in soft glittering flakes that float up round us and hang in the black and sparkle, like being inside a shaken globe of snow. Under it the shaft is a clean round hole the width of a man's shoulders, going straight down into the dark, with the old line running down its side frozen into the wall. Corin drops a lamp down it on a cord. We watch it go down and down, a little spinning star, smaller and smaller, until it stops.

"Forty," says Corin, reading the cord. "Forty-one."

Nobody moves.

"I'll go," I say.

Corin looks at me. "You?"

"I've got the Plate. I've read it. I'm the one who's meant to find out what's there." I don't know where the words are coming from. My heart is going so hard I can feel it in my visor. "Malachy said. _Read it first._"

"I'm coming," says Corin.

"And me," says Mattias.

Corin looks at him for a long moment through the two visors. "Why you?"

"Because I've seen the card," says Mattias. "And you haven't. If it's what I think it is, I'm the only one of us who'll know it."

Dasha stays at the top. Somebody has to, Corin says, to mind the lines and talk us down and haul us up if anything happens, and Dasha has done more lines than anybody. She screws a fresh anchor into the ridge beside the old one and clips three lines to it, and checks each of ours twice, and pats my helmet, once, the way you'd pat a child going into school.

"Say hello for me," she says. "To the Tether-Woman."

* * *

It's not dark at the bottom.

That's the first thing. I come down the last metres of the shaft hand over hand on my line, with my helmet lamp swinging, and Corin's below me already and Mattias above, and I expect dark, a well's dark. But the ice down here glows. Faintly, blue, the deep strange blue of very old very clean ice, lit from somewhere far above by the sun on the surface, the light coming down through forty metres of it like light through deep water. The walls of the shaft are blue glass. My breath is very loud.

At the bottom, on the left, there's a door.

Not a door. An opening. Somebody cut it into the side of the shaft with a pick; you can see the marks, the long rough scallops, like the marks in the ice on the surface of the Ninth's old water tanks. It's low, waist-high, a rough arch. Corin's lamp is shining through it into a space beyond. He's not moving. He's kneeling on the bottom of the shaft with his lamp held out in front of him through the arch, quite still.

I come down beside him and look.

It's a small room. A little round room cut out of the blue ice, big enough for one person to sit in with their back against the wall and their knees up. Somebody is.

They're sitting against the far wall in the blue light, with their knees drawn up and their arms round their knees and their head bowed forward on to their arms, the way you sit when you're very tired and you're waiting for something and you've decided to rest your eyes. They're wearing a coat. A heavy quilted pressure-coat, old, patched at the elbows and the knees, with its hood up over a helmet and rings round its collar for a helmet seal, the quilting gone stiff and white with frost. A knife hangs on a loop at their hip, in a sheath: a short hooked blade, a line knife, the kind riggers still carry. Their gloved hands are clasped round their shins. In one of them, held loosely against the knee, there's a small dark box with a strap.

Beside them on the floor of the little room, against the curve of the wall, there's a cylinder. Grey brushed steel, waist-high, on a squat base, with a ring of little lights round its upper rim. The lights are dark. Its lid has a window in it, small, frosted solid white.

Nobody says anything.

"The Tether-Woman," says Dasha's voice in my helmet, from forty metres up, very small. "Isaura? Is she there?"

I can't answer. I go in through the arch on my hands and knees, slowly, and Corin lets me. I kneel in front of the figure in the coat. I don't touch them. I can see nothing of a face; the hood's up and the helmet visor under it is turned down toward the knees and frosted over, solid white, like the cryostat's window. They're very still. They're so still they're not like a person at all; they're like something that grew here, out of the ice, slowly, over a very long time.

I lean round, carefully, and put my lamp on the back of the coat.

There's a word stencilled across the shoulders, in old white paint gone grey. The same kind of capitals as the GULL on the tug's flank. One word.

_TSOLO_

"It's her," says Dasha, in my ear. She's been listening. She can hear my breathing, she must, she must hear it stop. "Isaura, it's her, isn't it. It's the Tether-Woman. With her name on her back. Like my gran said."

"I don't know," I say.

Mattias has come in behind me. He doesn't look at the figure. He goes past me on his knees to the cylinder against the wall and bends over it, and puts his gloved hand on the frosted window in its lid, and rubs. The frost doesn't come away; it's too old, too hard. He takes his knife from his belt and scrapes at it, very gently, with the back of the blade, and the frost comes off in a little white curl, and he bends close with his lamp and looks through the window.

He stays like that for a long time.

"Isaura," he says. "Come and look."

I come.

Inside the cylinder, in a nest of old grey foam gone hard and brittle with cold, there's an egg. Grey. Matte grey, without any shine at all, so that my lamp seems to sink into it rather than lie on it. Smooth. Bigger than my forearm. With a fine seam round its widest part, like the join between a cup and a saucer. On its upper face, stencilled in black, in neat old capitals, three lines.

_SANITATION AGENT_\
_PATTERN H_\
_FACTOR'S AUTHORITY ONLY_

And under the lines, very small, a green wheel with eight spokes.

"That's the card," says Mattias. His voice is quite steady. "That's the picture on the card. That's Asset Seven."

Behind us, Corin Strand lets out a long breath.

* * *

I take the recorder out of the figure's hand.

I don't know if I'm allowed to. I don't ask. The gloved fingers are curled round the strap and they don't want to let go, they've been holding it for two hundred and sixty-three years, and I have to work them open one by one, very gently, the way you'd open the hand of someone asleep without waking them. It's a rigger's log. A little voice recorder in a hard black case, the kind the dipper crews still carry to make notes on runs, with a jack for a suit. Its old cell is long dead. I put it inside my suit, against the chest heater, and hold it there with my arm, and sit with my back against the cold blue wall of the room beside the figure in the coat, and wait for it to warm.

Nobody says anything while I wait. Corin kneels in the arch with his lamp. Mattias kneels by the cylinder. Up above, Dasha has gone quiet.

After twenty minutes I take it out and plug its jack into my suit, and switch it on, and the little light on its case comes on, very dim, amber; and I open the suit channel so that the others can hear, all of them, Dasha too, forty metres up on the black surface of the Lamp; and a voice comes into my helmet out of two hundred and sixty-three years.

It's a man's voice.

"_My name is Abel Tsolo,_" says the voice. "_I'm a rigger on the skimmer_ Patience, _of the Halloran-Vey Combine, Corva operation. Or I was. I don't know what the_ Patience _is now. I don't know what anything is. It's the thirtieth of November, twenty-three ninety-one, and I'm forty-one metres down in a comet, in the cold, and I'm going to tell whoever's listening what I've done and what this is, because somebody ought to know._"

A breath. A small laugh, very tired.

"_I'm wearing my sister's coat._"

* * *

He talks for forty minutes.

I'm not going to set it all down here. It's his. I've kept it, and it's in its box, and whoever comes after can listen to it for themselves the way I did, sitting in the blue light beside him with his voice in their ear. But I'll say what he said, the bones of it, because the bones are what matter, and because I sat there with my back against the ice and listened to two hundred and sixty-three years of my city come apart in my helmet, and I'd like somebody to know what it felt like.

He said the Combine was closing Corva. He said the crew of the _Patience_ was being sold to a war, and the Camp on Kettle left to die in the ice, two thousand households, with nobody coming. He said a runner named Lusine Arda found out, and told him, because she loved him, and he told the chief warden of the ship, a man named Corwin Hale, who was the Combine's informer; and Hale gave him a jeweller's file and told him to take her harness clip two-thirds through, to scare her, and he did it, and she died on the line. He said it plainly. He didn't ask anybody to forgive him for it. He said her name three times.

He said the bonded took the ship. He said they took it by force, in the night, with blood: a rigger called Danek Sorell, shot in a cold hold; a clerk called Ferrand, with his hands up; marines; dock girls; Jory from the Bucket crew. He said the Combine had brought a weapon to use on them if they didn't go quietly, a Sanitation Agent, Pattern H, the thing the bonded called the Hush, and he said what it did, and he said a name, Tamsin's Rock, and four thousand. He said the bonded took the Hush too. He said Corwin Hale wanted to use it on the Combine's cutter, the _Admonition_, eighty people, to put them to sleep in their bunks; and that the bonded nearly voted for it. And that his sister stood up in the drum hall and said no. And that his sister said, put it on the Lamp. Put it where nobody can use it, not them and not us. And they voted for her.

"_Her name's Maren Tsolo,_" he said. "_She's the tether-chief. It was her plan and her line, and she was going to come herself, because that's what she does. And I didn't let her. I put her to sleep in the dock with the factor's brandy and Ekene's green bottle and I took her coat and I came instead. So if you find me here, and somebody's told you a story about the tether-chief going out on the Lamp, it isn't true. It's me. She's back there on the board. She threw me. Straightest throw anyone ever threw._"

A long pause, there, on the recording. Breathing. The hum of a cryostat, faint.

"_Don't wake it,_" said Abel Tsolo. "_Whoever you are. Don't take it home. I don't know what you've got there now, back on the ice, if there's anything. I hope there is. I hope they made something. But don't take this back to it. It's not a gift. It was never a gift. It's the worst thing anybody ever made, and we couldn't kill it, so we sent it round. Send it round again. Or kill it, if you know how. But don't wake it._"

And then he said goodbye to his sister, and to somebody called Odile, and somebody called Ekene, and somebody called Teska, and the recording ran on for a little while with nothing on it but his breathing and the hum, getting slower, and then it stopped.

* * *

When it's finished, nobody moves for a long time.

I'm sitting with my back against the wall beside him, beside Abel Tsolo, with his recorder in my lap. Mattias is on his knees by the cryostat with his hands flat on the frosted lid, his head bowed. Up on the surface, Dasha is crying, a long low raw sound in my helmet that she's not trying to hide. I'm not crying. I'm thinking of the Plate, face down on my knees in the _Wick_, and the line of words in the middle, _it sleeps in the cold_, and the grey paint over everything else. I'm thinking of the Blank Share.

I'm thinking of a man in a fur collar on a back bench in the old bridge saying _cut the line to save the ship_, as if it were a proverb.

Corin Strand is kneeling in the arch.

He hasn't moved since the recording began. His lamp's still in his hand, held out in front of him, shining on the cryostat. In its light I can see his face behind his visor, and it's not the face from the posters any more. It's very white and very young and quite blank, the face of somebody who's been hit very hard and hasn't fallen down yet.

"Hale," he says.

Nobody answers him.

"Corwin Hale." His voice is strange. "The chief warden. Of the _Patience_." He laughs, a short broken sound. "Do you know who Edda Hale was, warden? You won't. They don't teach it on the Ninth. Edda Hale was the first woman of my family. Edda Hale-Strand. My seventh grandmother. Her father was a founder. It's on the family tree in the Strand house, in gold, the first name on it. _Corwin Hale, First Shareholder, a Purchaser of the_ Patience." He swallows. "That's us. That's where we come from. The Strands. We come from him."

"Corin—"

"An informer. A company man. He had a girl killed with a file." He looks at the figure in the coat, and then at me. "And the Purchase. _We bought our air._ It's in the catechism. I've said it every Lampfast since I could talk." He shakes his head inside the helmet. "They didn't buy anything. They took it. In the night. With a dead clerk with his hands up."

"Yes," I say.

"And they had this." He turns the lamp on the cryostat. "All along. They had this. And they sent it away, and they painted it over, and they told us we'd bought our air."

He's quiet for a moment. Then he gets up, slowly, in the nothing gravity, off his knees, and stands bent over in the low arch with his lamp, and when he speaks again his voice is different. Quite steady. Quite calm. His father's voice.

"It doesn't matter," says Corin Strand.

"What?"

"What they were. What they did. Any of it." He's looking at the cryostat. "It doesn't matter now. It's two hundred and sixty years ago. They're dead. All of them. Him." A jerk of the lamp at the figure in the coat. "And there's forty thousand people on the Rime, warden. Your mother's one of them. And twenty days of air, if that, and thirty-eight ships between them and the giant, and a commodore with orders to empty the city if he can find this thing first." He moves the lamp to the egg in its window. "And here it is."

"Corin."

"_Sanitise the claim_, the lieutenant says. Fine. We'll sanitise his." He's moving now, bending over the cryostat, feeling round its base for the clamps that hold it to the floor of the ice room. "One shot. The _Steadfast_. Their flagship. We take it back through the pickets, under the tail, the way we came. My father has a fleet of dippers with grapples that can put a cryostat on a hull. We put it on the _Steadfast_'s hull, by her intakes, and we open it. That's the end of the siege. Then the others go home. Then the dippers fly. Then everybody comes down off the Rime."

"It's not a gift," I say. "You heard him. _Don't wake it._"

"He was a murderer," says Corin, "and a rigger, and he's been dead two hundred and sixty years. My father's alive. Your mother's alive. For now." He finds a clamp and works it. It's frozen; it doesn't move. "Help me with this, Ebb."

Mattias doesn't move.

"Lieutenant."

"No," says Mattias.

Corin looks at him.

"There are eleven thousand people on the _Steadfast_," says Mattias quietly, on his knees by the cryostat with his hands still flat on its lid. "Conscripts, most of them. Bonded. They owe the Concord for their fathers' fishing boats. I flew landers off her for a year. I know the cooks." He looks up at Corin through his taped faceplate. "And if it vents on her hull, by her intakes, it goes in. And she's docked, half the time, to the _Vigilant_ and the _Resolute_, for supply. It goes through the collars. Like it would have done here." He looks at the figure in the coat. "Like he said."

"Then they shouldn't have come," says Corin.

"No," Mattias agrees. "They shouldn't. I shouldn't have. None of us asked."

Corin takes his sidearm out of the thigh pocket of his flight suit.

I didn't know he had one. I suppose I should have; the dipper pilots carry them, for the moon-rafts, for things that go wrong. It's small and black and he holds it quite steadily, pointing at Mattias, in the blue glow of the ice.

"Help me with the clamp, Lieutenant," he says.

Mattias doesn't move.

Corin shoots him.

It's very quiet. That's the thing about the Lamp. There's no air in the little room; there's no sound. There's a flash, and a jerk of Mattias's orange suit, and a spray of something that sparkles in my lamp and freezes as it flies and hangs in the air like the frost we cleared from the shaft. Mattias folds over against the cryostat with his hand on his side. In my helmet, on the suit channel, I hear him make a small surprised sound, and then breathe, fast, hissing.

"Suit's holed," he says. "Left side. It's—I've got a patch. I've got it."

"Then patch it," says Corin, not unkindly, and turns the sidearm on me. "Warden."

I'm still sitting against the wall beside Abel Tsolo. My hand is on the floor of the room. Beside it, at the figure's hip, on its loop, is the line knife in its sheath.

"Help me with the clamp," says Corin, "and we'll all go home."

I help him with the clamp.

* * *

It takes an hour to get the cryostat up the shaft.

Corin rigs it. He's a dipper; he knows how to haul a load up a line in no gravity, and he does it well, quickly, without any wasted movement, with the sidearm in the pocket of his flight suit where he can reach it. He clips the cryostat to the old line, Abel's line, and to one of Dasha's, doubled, and goes up ahead of it, hand over hand, hauling it after him, a grey steel cylinder floating up the blue shaft like a bucket from a well. He tells me to stay at the bottom with the lieutenant. He tells Dasha, up on the surface, that if she so much as touches the anchor he'll shoot her when he comes out. Dasha says nothing.

Mattias has patched his suit. He's lying on the floor of the little ice room with his back against the cryostat's empty place, his hand pressed over the patch, breathing in short hard hisses. He's lost some blood. Not a lot, he says. Enough.

"Go," he says. "Go up. I'm fine. Go."

I go up.

I come out of the top of the shaft into the sun, into the long hard shadows and the glittering black crust and the silent jets, with Gall filling a third of the sky; and Corin's already halfway to the _Wick_, hand over hand along Dasha's surface line, with the cryostat floating on its tether behind him like a dog on a lead. Dasha is standing by the anchor at the shaft mouth. She looks at me through her visor and shakes her head, once.

"He's unclipped," she says on the suit channel. Very low. "From the anchor. He needed both lines for the hoist. He's on the surface line now. Just the surface line. One clip."

I look. She's right. There are three lines on the anchor by the shaft: mine, and Mattias's, going down into the hole, and the long one Dasha strung across the crust to the _Wick_. Corin's on the long one. One clip, at his harness, on a short lanyard, sliding along it as he goes. The cryostat's clipped to his harness beside it.

I don't think. I want to be honest, as I've tried to be honest all through this. I don't make a decision. I don't do the arithmetic. My hand is at my hip, and at my hip, where I put it in the little ice room while Corin was working the clamp with his back to me, there's a line knife on a loop: a short hooked blade, very old, very sharp still, the kind tether-chiefs carried on the old skimmers, with a sheath of leather gone hard as wood. I don't remember taking it off him. Off Abel. I remember the sheath under my fingers, and the hilt.

I go along the surface line after Corin, hand over hand.

He hears me on the channel. He turns round, clumsily, on the line, with the cryostat swinging slowly behind him, and sees me coming, and puts his hand on the pocket where the sidearm is.

"Go back, warden."

"Corin, don't take it home."

"Go back to the shaft." He's got the sidearm out. "I don't want to. I'll do it. You saw me."

"I saw you."

"Then go back."

I'm three metres from him. Between us, along the line, there's his lanyard, his one clip, sliding. On the other side of him, the cryostat, on its own short tether clipped to his harness. On the other side of the cryostat, the line runs on across the crust to the _Wick_, thirty metres off, its hatch open. Under all of it, the soft black ground of the Lamp, so close and so slight that it barely holds us; and over all of it, the sky, black and enormous and full of the giant.

"My mother's on the Rime," I say.

"Then help me."

"She gave me her blessing," I say, "to stop you."

I don't know if he understands what I'm going to do. I think he does, at the last; I think he sees my hand come off the line with the knife in it. He brings the sidearm up. I'm already moving. I don't go for him. I go for the cryostat's tether, where it's clipped to his harness, and I catch it in my free hand and pull myself to it along it and get my glove round the cryostat's cold steel flank, and hold on; and with the other hand, the hand with the knife, I reach across him and hook the curved blade round his lanyard, where it runs from his harness to his one clip on the surface line, and pull.

It's a good knife. It's the best knife I ever held. It goes through the lanyard like it isn't there.

He's holding the cryostat's tether, too, at the clip, with the hand that isn't holding the gun. That's the thing. For a moment we're both holding the same steel cylinder, him at one end and me at the other, and I've got my boots locked round the surface line and he's got nothing. And the cryostat weighs almost nothing here, and he weighs almost nothing here, and the only thing holding him to the Lamp is his grip on the cryostat that I'm holding on to.

"Let go," I tell him.

He looks at me through his visor. His face is very young.

He fires. The shot goes past my helmet; I see the flash and nothing else. He fires again and I feel it hit the cryostat, a jolt through my glove, a spark off the brushed steel. And then he does what I told him to, without meaning to, the way anybody would who'd just felt a gun go off in a hand that was also trying to hold on to something: his grip opens.

I hold the cryostat. He goes.

He doesn't go fast. That's the worst of it. Nothing goes fast on the Lamp. He goes up and away from the surface of it, very slowly, with his arms and legs spread out, turning a little, end over end, as gently as a seed going up from a dandelion in the wind tunnels on the Third; and the line is out of his reach before he understands that it's out of his reach, and then he understands, and reaches for it anyway, for a long time, and keeps reaching. He's shouting on the channel. He's shouting my name, and his father's, and then nothing, just breathing, hard, and the sound of a man trying with his whole body to swim in something that isn't there.

I watch him go. Dasha comes along the surface line behind me and puts her hand on my shoulder, and watches him go too. Neither of us says anything. Neither of us has anything to say. He goes up into the long black shadow of the ridge and out of it again into the sun, small, and smaller, a little tumbling figure against the banded face of Gall, getting further; and his breathing on the channel gets faster and then slower, and then, after a long time, fainter, as he goes out of range of our little suit radios, out into the coma of the Lamp, the dust and the glittering ice, going out with it.

The last thing I hear him say is _Dad_.

I'm holding the cryostat in one hand and the knife in the other. There's a bright new scar on the cryostat's flank where his second shot hit it, a long scrape through the brushed steel. Its lights are still dark. The frost on its window has cracked across.

Through the crack, very faintly, in my helmet lamp, I can see the egg, grey and smooth and asleep, exactly as it was.

"Cut the line to save the ship," says Dasha Imre, very quietly, beside me.

I don't answer her. I can't. I stand on the black ice of the Lamp, clipped to the line, with the founders' weapon in one hand and a dead man's knife in the other and a living man's last word in my ears, and I look at Gall turning in the sky, two days from its closest pass, and at the long bright river of the Lamp's tail going out behind us into the dark; and somewhere under that, very far away, too small to see, a white cracked moon, with a city in it, and forty thousand people on its surface in old domes, breathing what's been spared.


# Chapter 18: Roche

*The Last Return · 2917*

On the thirtieth of April, at the second bell, the Lamp crosses Gall's limit, and begins to die.

Til watches it happen from the bottom of the sling. The _Dray_ is as deep in Gall's sky as anything built in the Tarn Reach has ever gone and come out again: so deep that the cloud-tops are a floor under her belly, ochre and white and boiling, close enough to see the shadows of the thunderheads, and the old tug is shaking from end to end with a long fine buzzing tremor that Oz says is the shield and Til hopes is the shield. The drive's at full. The board's red in four places. The giant is swinging them round its flank like a stone on a string, faster and faster, and Til has both hands on the yoke and her teeth locked and is watching, in the upper port, the Lamp.

It's coming apart.

Not all at once. Not the way a ship goes, with a flash. Slowly, almost lazily, the way a cake of old ice breaks in the bottom of a tank when the water's warmed: the long dark crack round its waist widening, widening, a black seam opening into a black gap with light pouring out of it, white jets roaring silently out of the raw new faces on either side. The two lobes of the Lamp, the two stones pressed together, beginning to drift apart. And all round them, shaken off by the pull of the giant, smaller pieces. Dozens. A slow-motion explosion of black boulders the size of stations and houses and drift-bergs and rooms, glittering where the sun catches their new-broken faces, scattering out along the comet's path like grain from a split sack.

"Thirty seconds to burn-out," says Oz, from the engine space. His voice is hoarse. "Twenty."

"I see it."

"I know you see it. Twenty seconds."

The drive cuts. The shaking stops. The _Dray_ comes up off the bottom of the sling out of Gall's sky like a fish breaking water, with the giant falling away behind her and the black ahead full of the Lamp's breaking body, and for a long moment nobody in the cockpit says anything at all.

"We're on it," says Oz. "We're on its path. Four hours to match." He coughs. "That's the sling. My mother's people's sling. Nobody's done it in five hundred years." Another cough. "I'd like a drink."

Behind them, high and to port, the _Carrion Hymn_ comes out of the giant's glare on her own long curve. She's made the sling too, on her own engines, which are better than the _Dray_'s; she's been flown by Szabo's captain round the bottom of Gall at a distance just safe enough to show that Szabo's captain is very good and very careful. She settles in five kilometres off the _Dray_'s starboard quarter, black against the white of the Lamp, and stays there.

"Captain Brask," says Mother Szabo's voice on the open band. "That was very exciting. I've had to have a little lie-down."

Til doesn't answer her.

Hollis Vey is at the cabin port, in his cradle, propped high on the pillows. He hasn't slept. He hasn't, Til thinks, slept for three days. His face in the light of the breaking comet is grey and shining and so thin it's almost not a face any more, and his pale eyes are wide.

"Which piece?" he says. His voice through the grille is barely there. "Captain. Which piece is it on?"

"That's the question."

"It's the question I've asked for thirty years," says Hollis. "I'd like to know the answer before Gall eats it."

Oz has the scope up. He's been running it since the bottom of the sling, sweeping the Lamp's body, both lobes and the cloud of pieces round them, looking for the one thing out here that isn't ice or rock or dust. Metal. Old metal, the kind that gives a hard bright return on the _Dray_'s salvage radar, the kind it was built to find in the wreck-fields of the outer Reach.

He finds it on the ninth sweep. On the southern lobe, the bigger of the two stones, the one that's kept most of its shape: a small hard return, very bright, at the foot of a long dark ridge on its underside.

"That's not ice," says Oz.

He puts the scope on it at full magnification. It's a smudge. A grey speck at the foot of a black wall, too small to make out. But it's square. It's got corners. Nothing in nature has corners.

"There," says Hollis Vey. "Oh, there."

"What is it?" says Cato Marchetti, in the cabin hatch. He's been there for some time. He doesn't come in.

"It's a ship," says Oz. "A little one. Sitting on the ice. Under that ridge." He sits back. "Somebody landed there, Til. Somebody landed a ship on the Lamp."

"How long ago?"

"Long enough," says Oz, "that it's still there."

Til looks at the speck, at the long black ridge, at the great southern lobe of the Lamp turning slowly in the sun with its raw white faces and its silent jets.

"Oz," she says. "Where's that lobe going? When it goes in?"

He's already running it. He's been running every piece he can track since the bottom of the sling; it's his habit, the way checking anchors is hers. It takes him a minute. When he looks up his face has changed.

"Not in," he says. "Not straight in. Not that one." He puts it on the screen: the long curve of the southern lobe's path round Gall, and the dashed circle, and the giant's limb. "The north lobe's going straight down. Dead centre. Gone. But the south one's skimming. It'll go through the cloud-tops at a slant, high, and the heat'll break it, and the pieces—" He traces them with his finger. "Some of them go in. Some of them skip. Out. Across the moons. Across Kettle." He looks at her. "It's the worst one, Til. Of all of them. It's on the worst one."

* * *

They land on the first of May, at the fourth bell, on the southern lobe of the Lamp, two hundred metres from the old ship under the ridge.

There are five of them on the ice. Til, and Oz, and Cato Marchetti, and two of Szabo's house men, big quiet men in grey surface suits with stun-carbines slung across their chests, called Riis and Gant. Juno is on the _Hymn_. Hollis is in his cradle in the _Dray_'s cabin, on the open channel, listening to their breathing. The _Dray_ sits on the ice behind them with her grapple boom unfolded under her belly and its claws sunk deep into the black crust, holding her down, the only way to hold anything down on a thing with no gravity; her big salvage winch is run out to a ground anchor, and from the anchor a surface line runs across the ice toward the ridge, and they move along it hand over hand, five figures in a row, clipped on.

The Lamp's surface is soft and black and crusted like old snow gone grey, and under the crust it glitters. The jets are everywhere. Silent white plumes going up out of pits and cracks, sudden as coughs, and coming down again in slow glittering curtains of ice dust that fall on their visors and their shoulders and sparkle in the sun. The ground trembles under their boots, a long deep shudder every few minutes that Til can feel through her soles and her knees. Over the close curved horizon of the lobe, a few kilometres off across a gap that wasn't there yesterday, the north lobe hangs in the sky like a black mountain upside down, turning, breathing white fire out of its broken face. And over everything, Gall. So close now it isn't a planet; it's a wall of weather filling half of everything.

The old ship's a scoop-tug. Oz knows it the moment he sees it close. An old pattern, a very old one, squat and armoured, lying on its side on the ice at the foot of the black ridge, with one grapple arm half torn out of the crust, frosted white along every edge. Its hatch stands open. Its cabin is empty.

There are letters on its flank, under the frost. Til wipes them with her glove.

_GULL_

And under it, smaller:

_S.V. PATIENCE_
