"That was the skimmer," says Hollis's voice, very faint, on the channel. "The _Patience_. The register names her. She dipped Gall for my family for sixty years." A breath, with the hinge in it. "This was hers."

Ten metres along the ridge from the tug there's a hole.

It's half-filled with frost, glittering, a shallow round dent in the black crust a metre and a half across, with a shaft going down through the middle of it, round and clean, the width of a man's shoulders. Beside it, screwed into the foot of the black wall, there are anchors. Two. Old ones, both of them, corroded and furred with frost; one older than the other, a different pattern, with a little grey box hanging from it on a strap. A line, thin and pale and frozen stiff, runs from the older anchor down into the shaft.

"Somebody dug," says Oz.

"Somebody dug," says Til.

Cato Marchetti kneels at the shaft's lip and looks down it. He doesn't say anything. He drops a lamp on a cord, and watches it go, a little spinning star going down and down into the dark, and reads the cord when it stops.

"Forty," he says. "Forty-one." He looks up. "Riis."

Riis goes down. That was always the plan, Til understands, watching him clip on and swing his big grey body into the hole: the house men go down and bring it up, and Marchetti stands at the top with Til and Oz in front of him where he can see them. Riis goes down the shaft hand over hand, his helmet lamp going away into the dark, and they hear him on the channel, breathing.

"Ice is blue," he says, after a while. "It's blue down here. It's lit. Funny." A pause. "There's a door. At the bottom. Somebody cut a door."

Under their feet, the Lamp shudders.

It isn't like the other shudders. It goes on. It goes on and on, a long deep grinding tremor that comes up through the crust into Til's boots and her knees and her teeth, so hard that for a moment she can't see properly; and along the black ridge, fifty metres off, a jet goes up that isn't a jet at all but a sheet, a long ripping curtain of white fire tearing up out of a crack that wasn't there a second ago and roaring silently into the sky. Ice dust comes down on them like snow. Through it, Til sees the ground beyond the ridge tilt. Very slightly. The whole horizon tilting, a degree, two, the way a floe tilts when it's cracked from underneath.

"It's coming apart," says Oz, very calm. "The lobe. Under us. It's going to come apart."

"Captain Brask," says Mother Szabo, on the open band, sharply, without any sweetness in it at all. "Major. We're seeing a great deal of activity from out here. We're seeing pieces coming off that lobe. Big ones." A pause. "I would like my cargo now, please. Now."

"It's forty metres down, Mother," says Marchetti.

"Then I shall come and get it. Bring the _Hymn_ in to five hundred metres, Captain Alder. We'll take it off them on a line." A pause. "And the girl can come across on the same line, Captain Brask, the moment it's aboard. Fair exchange."

Til looks across the ice at the _Dray_.

She sits on her grapple, two hundred metres off, squat and ugly and unkillable, her winch run out to the anchor. On her belly, folded up under the boom, there's a thing Til has used perhaps a dozen times in her life, on the biggest and deadest hulks in the Harrow: the harpoon. A salvage harpoon. A great barbed bolt on the end of a hundred kilometres of cable, fired by a charge, made to punch into the hull of a dead ship and hold it while the tug hauls.

Oz is looking at it too. Til can see his face through his visor. He looks at her and his eyes are wet and very happy.

"Tugs push ships," he says, on their private channel. "And pull them."

The _Carrion Hymn_ comes in.

She comes in slow and careful and beautiful, the way her captain does everything, sliding down out of the dust-fall on her thrusters toward the Lamp's southern lobe, long and black against the white curtain of the jet, her ram bow lit by Gall, her four turrets swivelling to cover the ice. She stops at five hundred metres, exactly, and holds there, sitting in the sky over the black ridge like a great dark bird.

"Line, please, Captain," says Szabo.

"Oz," says Til.

Oz is already moving. He goes along the surface line to the _Dray_, hand over hand, fast for a big old man, faster than Til has seen him move in years, and Marchetti turns his head to watch him, and frowns, and says, "Mr Mbeki," and Oz reaches the _Dray_'s flank and the exterior winch panel by her grapple boom, and opens it, and puts his hand inside.

"Mr Mbeki," says Marchetti again, and draws his gun.

The harpoon fires.

There's no sound. There's a flash under the _Dray_'s belly, and a long silver streak going up from it into the sky, so fast Til can barely follow it, trailing a thread of cable behind it like a spider's line, up and up through the falling ice dust; and then, five hundred metres up, the streak meets the long black flank of the _Carrion Hymn_ just aft of her bow, and stops, and the cable behind it goes taut.

"_What_," says Mother Szabo.

"Mother," says Til, on the open band. "Your ship is now tied to the Lamp. By a hundred kilometres of salvage cable, with a barbed bolt through your forward hull plating and into your frame. That's my winch at the other end of it, and my tug, and my tug's grapple is in the ice of this lobe up to the shoulder." She can hear her own voice, the weather voice, steady as a floor. "This lobe's coming apart, Mother. In an hour, maybe. And when it does, it's going into Gall. At a slant. And so are you, unless you cut that cable. And you can't cut that cable from your end, Mother, because it's tungsten-cored salvage cable and you haven't got anything that'll cut it." She takes a breath. "But I have."

There's a long silence on the open band.

"What do you want?" says Szabo.

"Juno. Across the cable. Now. In her suit. Hand over hand."

"And then you'll cut it."

"And then I'll cut it."

"And the cargo?"

"The cargo stays where it is," says Til.

On the ice beside the shaft, Cato Marchetti has his gun on her. He's been holding it on her for a minute, very steady, the way he holds everything. Now he swings it round toward the _Dray_, toward Oz, standing by the open winch panel with his hand still inside it.

"Mr Mbeki," he says. "Take your hand away from the winch."

"No," says Oz.

Marchetti shoots him.

Til sees it. She'll see it for the rest of her life. A small flash at the end of Marchetti's arm, and nothing else, no sound, the Lamp swallows sound; and Oz, two hundred metres off, by the _Dray_'s flank, jerks once, and his hand comes out of the winch panel, and he sits down on the ice against the tug's armoured hull, slowly, the way a tired man sits down at the end of a shift, with his back to the steel and his legs out in front of him and his hand on his chest.

"Oz," says Til.

"It's all right," says Oz on the private channel. His voice is quite clear. "It's all right, Til. He's too late. The brake's off. It's on the free spool. It'll run till you stop it." A breath. "Get the girl."

Then Til is moving. She doesn't decide to. She's along the line toward Marchetti before she knows it, hand over hand, three metres, two, and he's turning the gun back to her, and Gant, the house man, is unslinging his carbine, and the Lamp chooses that moment.

The ground under the shaft opens.

It goes the way the jet went, all at once, with no warning but a long rising shudder: a crack, black, a metre wide, tearing across the crust between the shaft's lip and the ridge, right under Cato Marchetti's boots; and white fire comes up out of it in a sheet. Marchetti's still on the line. He's clipped on. Til sees it, the clip at his harness, sliding. But the jet's under him, a column of gas and ice coming up out of the Lamp's raw inside at hundreds of metres a second, and it takes the line, and the anchor, and him, all together, and throws them up.

It isn't fast. Nothing on the Lamp is fast. It's very slow and very gentle and quite final. The anchor comes out of the ice like a tooth. The line goes slack and then whips. And Cato Marchetti goes up into the white fire of the jet with his arms out and his gun still in his hand, turning over and over, a small grey figure in a column of glittering light, up and up into the falling dust, and keeps going, and doesn't come down.

He doesn't say anything on the channel. Til will think about that, later. Not a word. As if he'd been expecting it, all along, for twenty years, and had only been waiting to see when.

Gant has dropped his carbine. It's floating away from him, turning slowly. He's clinging to the surface line, the part of it that's still anchored, by both hands, staring up at the jet.

"Get back to your ship," Til tells him. "Along the harpoon cable. Now. And take your friend."

Riis is coming up the shaft. She can hear him on the channel, swearing, climbing fast. When he comes out of the hole he looks once at the jet and once at Til and doesn't argue. The two big grey men go along the surface line to the _Dray_ and up the harpoon cable, hand over hand, toward the _Hymn_, five hundred metres up, as fast as she's ever seen two men climb anything.

Halfway up, they meet Juno coming down.

* * *

She comes down the cable from the _Hymn_ in her small new suit, the one Til bought on Cinderhaven without saying anything, hand over hand, clipped on with a single lanyard, against a sky that's half Gall and half fire.

Til watches her all the way. She stands at the foot of the cable by the _Dray_'s flank, clipped to the tug's hull, beside Oz, who's sitting with his back against the armour and his hand on his chest and his visor turned up toward the sky; and she watches the small figure come down out of the falling ice dust, and she doesn't breathe.

The cable's moving. That's the trouble. The _Hymn_ is five hundred metres up and the Lamp's lobe under them is shaking itself to pieces, and every time it shudders the cable whips, a long slow wave running up it from the _Dray_'s winch to the _Hymn_'s bow like a wave down a forty-kilometre tether, and Juno, on it, swings out and back, out and back, like a weight on a pendulum.

"Hold on," Til says on the channel. "When it whips, hold on and count. It goes by."

"_Counting_," says Juno, between her teeth.

"Captain Brask." Szabo's voice, on the open band, sharp and fast. "Captain Brask, there's a piece the size of the Ledger Ring coming off the north side of your lobe. It's coming round. My captain says it will pass within a kilometre of us in four minutes. Cut the cable."

"When she's down."

"_Now_, Captain. Or it hits us, and we go, and the cable goes, and she goes with it."

"When she's down," says Til.

"You'll kill her. You'll kill all of us." Szabo's voice has gone high. "Cut the line, Captain Brask. That's what you do. Everybody knows it's what you do. Cut it."

Til doesn't answer.

Juno is halfway. She's a hundred metres up, two hundred, swinging; Til can see her face now, a pale shape behind the visor, her mouth moving as she counts. Above her, over the curve of the lobe, something huge and black and glittering is rising into the sky, slowly, turning, a mountain of ice with white fire pouring out of its broken underside: the piece Szabo saw, coming round. It isn't going to hit the _Hymn_. Til can see that already. It's going to pass behind her. Close. Close enough to fill the sky behind her with black. Close enough that its jets will hit her.

"Juno," says Til. "Faster now."

"I'm _going_ faster."

"Faster than that. Pretend it's me."

A sound on the channel that might, very nearly, be a laugh.

The piece comes round. It passes behind the _Hymn_ at what can't be more than six hundred metres, a black mountain blotting out half of Gall, and its jets hit the _Hymn_ broadside: a long silent blast of white that shoves the black ship sideways in the sky like a hand pushing a door. The _Hymn_ rolls. Her thrusters fire, all of them, a crown of blue sparks round her hull. The harpoon cable goes taut with a jerk Til feels through the _Dray_'s armour, through her boots, through her spine, and then slack, and then taut again, and the wave runs down it toward the ground, fast, much too fast.

Juno's on it.

"Hold on," says Til.

The wave hits her. Til watches it hit. The cable snaps out sideways in a long white arc, and the small figure on it goes with it, swung out over the black ridge like a stone in a sling, out and out, her lanyard holding, holding, her gloves on the cable, and her voice on the channel, very high, counting. _Nineteen. Twenty. Twenty-one._ And then the cable comes back. And she's still on it.

"Twenty-two," says Juno. "Twenty-three."

"Good girl."

"_Don't_ call me that."

"Keep counting."

Twenty metres. Ten. Til has her hands up. Juno comes down the last of the cable to the _Dray_'s flank like a runner coming in off a line, hand over hand, and Til gets her by the harness and clips her to the tug's hull with her own spare lanyard and holds her there, both of them pressed against the old scarred armour of the _Dray_ with the Lamp shaking under them, and for a moment neither of them can say anything at all.

"Cut it," says Juno, breathing hard. "Cut it now. They'll go. They'll all go."

Til has the knife out already. Not a line knife. A salvager's cable-shear, a short heavy thing on her belt that the Directorate's patrols call a tin-opener; it's the only thing aboard that'll go through tungsten-cored cable, which is why she bought it. She sets its jaws on the harpoon cable where it comes out of the _Dray_'s winch, and looks up, at the black ship rolling in the sky five hundred metres overhead with fire behind it.

"Mother," she says on the open band. "Go home."

She cuts the line.

The cable goes up and away from the winch like a released spring, a long silver whip uncoiling into the sky. The _Carrion Hymn_, free, rolls once more and steadies, her thrusters flaring, and turns, slowly, scarred and smoking along her starboard flank where the jet hit her; and lights her drive, and goes. Up and out and away from the Lamp's breaking body, with a hundred kilometres of salvage cable trailing from her bow like a broken kite's string, back toward the Throat.

Mother Szabo doesn't say anything else. Her ledgers will go on. They always do. But not here.

* * *

Oz is still sitting against the _Dray_'s flank, with his hand on his chest and his visor turned up to the sky.

Til kneels beside him on the ice. Juno kneels on his other side. Up close, through his visor, his face is grey and wet and smiling, his old bag-of-stones face, and his eyes are open, looking up at the black where the _Hymn_ has gone and the white where the Lamp is burning.

"There," he says. His voice is very faint. "Told you. Tugs pull ships."

"You told me."

"Good harpoon. Good cable." He breathes, slowly. "Is she down?"

"She's down, Oz. She's here."

"Hello, Juno."

"Hello, Oz," says Juno, very quietly.

"You held the clamp," says Oz. "In the lane. I told her. You held it." His hand moves on his chest, the delicate engineer's fingers, as if feeling for something. "Til. Tell her. Tell her about the knife. Not on the Ring. Here. On the Lamp. Tell her."

"I will."

"You won't."

"I will, Oz."

"Good." He looks up at the sky. Gall fills half of it, enormous, banded, turning; and across the other half the Lamp is breaking, slowly, beautifully, its pieces spreading out along its path like a necklace with the string cut, each one trailing its own small tail of white fire. "Look at that," says Ozias Mbeki. "Look at it go. My mother would've—" He stops. He takes a breath, the last one Til hears him take, and lets it out in the old Roundsman cant, the word you say at the chapel brazier when you put in the salt. _On its way._

Then he's quiet.

They sit with him a while, on the ice. There isn't time, and they do it anyway. Juno holds his hand. Til unclips his lanyard from the _Dray_'s hull, and clips it to her own, and doesn't let it go.

"Captain," says Hollis Vey's voice, very faint, from the cradle in the _Dray_'s cabin. He's been there all along, on the channel, listening to everything. "Captain, I'm so sorry."

"I know."

"The lobe," says Hollis. "Captain. The lobe's coming apart. The shaft. Whatever's down there—"

"I know," says Til.

She stands up. Juno stands up with her.

* * *

The shaft is where it was. The jet that took Marchetti has died back to a long glittering curtain of dust, and the crack beside the shaft's lip has stopped widening, for now; the older of the two anchors is still in the ice, and the stiff pale line from it still runs down into the dark. Til clips them both to it.

"You don't have to come," she says.

"I'm witnessing," says Juno. "It's my job."

They go down.

It isn't dark. Riis said so, and Riis was right. Ten metres down, the black of the crust gives way to ice, and the ice is blue, and lit: a deep strange glowing blue, like the inside of a lamp, the sun forty metres overhead pouring down through it like light down through deep water. The walls of the shaft are blue glass. Their breathing is very loud in their helmets. Every few seconds the whole shaft trembles, a long grinding shudder that comes up from somewhere far below, and ice dust drifts down past their lamps, glittering.

At the bottom, on the left, there's a door.

An arch, waist-high, cut roughly into the shaft's side with a pick; you can see the marks. Through it, a little round room in the blue ice.

Til goes in first, on her hands and knees. Juno comes after her.

Somebody is sitting against the far wall.

They're sitting with their back to the blue ice and their knees drawn up and their arms round their knees and their head bowed forward on to their arms, the way you sit when you're very tired and you've decided to rest your eyes for a moment. They're wearing a coat. An old heavy quilted pressure-coat, patched at the knees, with its hood up over a helmet, gone stiff and white with frost. They're so still they're not like a person at all, but like something that grew there, out of the ice, very slowly, over a very long time.

Juno stops in the arch behind her. Til feels her stop.

Neither of them says anything. The Lamp shudders round them, and the blue light trembles on the walls, and ice dust falls past the arch like snow; and in the little round room forty metres down in the breaking comet, the figure in the coat sits on, with its head on its knees, waiting, as it has waited for longer than anybody alive has been alive, to see who would come.


# Chapter 19: The Purchase

*The Bonded · 2391*

For two days after the throw, Maren kept the board.

She didn't sleep on it, exactly. She sat at the tether-chief's station in the gallery with channel four open and the static hissing out of the speaker, and when she was too tired to sit she lay down on the gallery floor beside the chair, and when she woke she sat up again. Ekene brought her food. She ate some of it. Odile brought her tea. She drank it cold. The crew dipped Gall around her, twice a day, because Teska said they must, and Maren ran the dips without thinking, the way you breathe, her hands on the damper and her eyes on the trace; and between the dips she sat with the static and listened to it.

There was nothing in it. She knew there was nothing in it. The Gull's relay at the top of the shaft would have gone out of the _Patience_'s range a few hours after Abel's last words, as the Lamp went out round Gall's far side and away; and Abel himself would have stopped talking long before that, forty-one metres down in the cold, in her coat, with his knees drawn up. She knew all of that. She listened anyway.

On the second day, Ekene sat down on the floor beside her chair, with his bad shoulder strapped up where Bram Kovač's shock-stick had caught it, and said, "Maren. You have to come down."

"I'm down."

"You're in a chair in the gallery with your hand on a speaker. Come down to the drum hall. They need to see you."

"For what?"

"Because the _Admonition_ is still out there," said Ekene, "and Hale's men still have the bridge and the carbines, and the crew are frightened, and they keep coming up to the gallery door and looking at you and going away again." He paused. "And because you made a promise. To Odile. In the Belly."

Maren took her hand off the speaker.

She had made it. She remembered every word. _After the Lamp. I'll put him in front of the whole drum hall and tell them what he did and what Hale did._ She had sworn it on the line, on Lusine's name chalked on the drum. Abel was gone; she could not put Abel in front of anybody. But Hale was not gone. Hale was on the bridge with his slate under his arm, mild and patient and reasonable, and nobody in the drum hall but her and Odile and Ekene and, perhaps, Teska knew what he was.

"Tomorrow," she said. "At the change of shift. Call them for tomorrow. All of them. The Camp too."

Odile, at the next station, looked round. Her face was very white.

"I'll tell them," she said.

"Tell them everything," said Maren. "I'll tell them the rest."

That night, while the crew slept and the _Patience_ swung on her long orbit over the giant with the drum still and the line reeled in, the _Clement_'s little cargo boat cast off from the forward collar without anybody on the bridge logging it, and crossed eighty thousand kilometres of dark to the _Admonition_, and docked. There were two people aboard. One of them was Aurelian Quist, with his wrists still tied in front of him in a length of medical tubing. The other was Corwin Hale.

* * *

He came back at the second bell of the morning and called the drum hall himself, an hour before Maren's.

She heard it from the gallery: the warden's whistle in the corridors, three long notes, the old summons that had called the crew to the duty roster every shift for twenty years. _All hands to the drum. All hands._ By the time she came down the gallery ladder the hall was full. The crew. The Camp's delegates. The children again, on their parents' shoulders. They stood packed on the deck under the still drum and the chalked names and the Plate on its end, three hundred names cut in steel, and they were very quiet, the way a hall is quiet when it has been called by someone it is afraid of and does not yet know why.

Hale was on the gallery stair, three steps up, with his slate under his arm. Quist stood beside him, no longer tied. Bram Kovač and four other trusties stood at the foot of the stair with the two carbines.

Maren stopped at the edge of the crowd.

"I've been to see Captain Lachance," said Hale.

He didn't raise his voice. He never had to.

"I went last night, with the factor, because somebody had to, and because the _Admonition_ has been sitting off our bow for nine days with twelve torpedoes and nobody has been talking to her. I've talked to her." He looked round the hall. "The Sanitation Agent is gone. You all know that. You were all there, or you heard. It's on the Lamp, and the Lamp is going out, and nobody will ever get it back. Not the Combine. Not us." He paused. "The tether-chief did that. And her brother. And I want to say before anything else that it was a brave thing, and that it was done, and that it can't be undone."

A murmur. Maren stood very still.

"Captain Lachance," said Hale, "has had orders from Halloran overnight, on the long beacon. New orders. Because there's nothing left here for the Combine to take back." He lifted the slate and read from it, without looking at the hall, the way he read the duty roster. "_The Corva operation is written off. The Combine has no further interest in the vessel_ Patience, _in the Kettle installations, or in the accounts of their personnel, beyond the following. The Combine will transfer its title to the vessel and to the Corva claim, and to all installations and accounts therein, to a body representing the bonded complement of the_ Patience, _for consideration of one share. The Combine will retain that share, nominally, until the transfer is complete, and will then relinquish it. No personnel will be relocated. No reprisals will be taken. The escort will withdraw._"

The hall didn't move.

"It means," said Hale gently, "that we've bought her. The ship. The claim. The Camp. Our air." He lowered the slate. "For one share. Nobody goes to Sorrel. Nobody's account is sold. The _Admonition_ goes home, and doesn't come back, and nobody comes after her. We're the Shareholders of the _Patience_. All three hundred of us. Every name on that Plate."

For a moment the hall said nothing at all. Then somebody at the back made a sound, a sort of gasp, and somebody else laughed, high and cracked, and then it went through the hall like a wave through a tether, a great ragged roar, three hundred people and twice that many from the Camp all shouting at once, hugging, weeping, children shrieking on their parents' shoulders without knowing why. Teska, on her bench by the drum, put her face in her claw of a hand.

Maren stood at the edge of the crowd and didn't move.

She was watching Hale. Hale was standing on the stair with the slate under his arm and his mild face lifted to the noise, letting it go on, letting it wash up against him; and when at last it began, slowly, to die, he lifted his hand.

"There's one more thing," he said.

The hall went quiet again in waves.

"The Combine has lost a Sanitation Agent," said Hale. "One unit. Pattern H. It's never lost one before. Somebody at Halloran has to write that down, in a register, and somebody has to answer for it. The Combine's courts require it. That's the condition." He looked down at the slate. "_The person or persons responsible for the mutiny aboard the_ Patience _and for the loss of the Asset will be surrendered to the escort for trial at Halloran._" He looked up. "That's all. One name. One person, to stand for all of us. And then it's done."

Nobody said anything.

"I didn't ask for this," said Hale. "I want you all to know that. I argued. The factor will tell you I argued." Quist, beside him, said nothing at all, and didn't look at anybody. "But Captain Lachance has his orders, and I've seen them, and they're very clear. The Combine needs a name in its register. And there's only one name it'll accept."

He turned his head and looked across the hall at Maren.

They all looked at her. Six hundred faces, more, turning in the light from the gallery windows, the way the riggers had turned their faces up to the glass on the morning Lusine went into Gall, as if she were a god in a box with her hands on the weather.

"It was her plan," Hale said softly. "Her line. Her throw. Everyone heard her say so. Everyone in Corva knows who took the key out of the cold hold."

She understood it then. All of it. The whole shape of it, at once, the way you see the whole shape of a line walking on the board when the trace finally kinks. She understood that Hale had known, on the gallery, with Odile's knife at his neck, that she would stand up in the drum hall and tell them all what he was; and that he had gone to the _Admonition_ in the night and come back with this, so that he could stand on the stair first. So that if she spoke now, if she told them about Lusine and the jeweller's file and the line in the warden's ledger that said _contain_, it would not be the truth about Corwin Hale. It would be the ringleader, the one they wanted, trying to save herself by pulling the deal down on everybody else. On the Camp. On the children on their parents' shoulders.

And she understood that he was right. That it would. That half of them would believe her and half of them wouldn't, and the half that did would go for him, and his trusties had the carbines, and the _Admonition_ was still off the bow with her twelve tubes; and Lachance, watching a mutiny tear itself apart over the terms of its own surrender, would do what he was told.

She had done that arithmetic before. On the line, at the board, with Lusine going faint on channel four. You ran the dip, or you lost them all.

"Maren," said Ekene, beside her, very low. "Don't."

Odile had come up on her other side. She didn't say anything. She had her line knife in her fist, at her side, where nobody could see it but Maren.

"No," said Teska, from her bench, loudly, to the whole hall. "No. Not her. Give them him." She pointed a shaking claw at Hale. "Give them the warden. He's the Company's dog. Twenty years. Everybody knows it."

"Grandam," said Hale gently, "the Combine doesn't want me."

"I'll go," said Maren.

The hall was silent.

"I'll go," she said again, a little louder, in the tether-chief's voice. "It was my plan. It was my line. I threw it." She looked round at them, at all of them. "Nobody else goes. Nobody else's name. Mine. That's the share. That's the one share." She looked up at Hale on the stair. "Done."

Corwin Hale looked down at her for a long moment, with his mild patient face.

"Thank you, Chief," he said.

Afterward, when the hall had broken up and the crew had gone off in its small stunned groups, and the trusties had come for her, quite politely, with the carbines held low, he came and walked beside her down the corridor toward the _Clement_'s cargo bay, where she would wait for the boat. He walked slowly, with his slate under his arm. He didn't look at her.

"They'll love you for ever," he said. "You know that. I'll see to it."

She said nothing.

"We'll say you went with it," said Hale. "On the Lamp. The Camp's already saying it; half of them think it was you on the Gull, they heard _Tsolo_ on the channel. We'll let them. The Tether-Woman, who went out with the Lamp to carry it. That's a story they can tell their children." He paused at the cargo bay hatch. "It's better than a rope, Maren. It's better than the Kessel mines. It's better than the truth, which is that their tether-chief was sold to the Combine for their air. Nobody wants to tell their children that."

"You sold me."

"I bought them," said Corwin Hale. "With the only coin I had." He opened the hatch for her. "That's all a purchase is."

* * *

Quist came to see her in the cargo bay that evening.

They'd put her in a corner of it, behind a stack of crates, with a bunk and a blanket and a bucket, the same as the _Clement_'s crew had had for the two weeks they'd been locked in there; the crew themselves had been let out that morning and were back on their own decks, sullen and astonished, getting their ship ready to go home. Nobody guarded her. There was nowhere to go. The bay doors were locked from the outside and the only other way out was the maintenance hatch to the cold hold, and the cold hold was empty now.

Quist sat down on a crate across from her bunk. His wrists were free. He looked older than he had looked on the day he came aboard, by a good deal more than a month.

"I didn't argue," he said. "In the hall. He said I'd tell you he argued. He didn't. He went to Lachance and offered him you before Lachance had asked for anybody. I was there."

"I know."

"I thought you'd want to know for certain."

"I knew for certain," said Maren, "when he said _one name_."

Quist nodded slowly. "He'll do well," he said. "Your warden. He's the kind who does. I've met a great many of them. Every operation has one." He looked at his hands. "The Combine will give him a very good reference."

They sat for a while without speaking. Somewhere aft, through the bulkheads, the _Clement_'s engines were being warmed, a deep rising hum.

"I'll be on the _Admonition_ with you," said Quist. "Going home to be disciplined. Lachance will put me in a cabin and you in the brig. When we get to Halloran there'll be an inquiry, and a court." He looked up at her. "I'm going to tell them the loss of the Asset was mine. That I had the key, and the authority, and the opportunity, and didn't use it. That's true. That's in my log, every night for fifteen nights. They'll want to know why I didn't, and I'll tell them I didn't know. They won't like that." He paused. "It won't save you. But it'll muddy the register. With a factor in it, the court will want to be careful. It'll probably mean Kessel rather than the rope."

"What's Kessel?"

"A mine. A long way in. The air's bad and the work's worse and people last, on average, about eleven years." He said it plainly, the way he'd said everything to her since the first day. "I'm sorry. It's what I've got."

"Why?" said Maren.

Quist was quiet for a long time.

"I've been thinking about gardening," he said at last. "About my friend in his greenhouse, with the roses. I used to think he went into the greenhouse because of what he'd done at Tamsin's Rock. Because of the four thousand." He turned his hands over and looked at the palms. "I think now he went in there because it was the only place he could make something grow. Instead of the other thing. I think it took him too long to find it, and when he did, it was too late, and that's why he—" He stopped. "I'd like to find mine before it's too late. That's all. That's what this is. It's very small. It's a rose."

"It's a rose," said Maren.

"It's a rose," said Aurelian Quist, and got up off the crate and went to the bay door and knocked to be let out.

* * *

Odile came for her at the fourth bell of the night, the dead hour between dips, when the _Patience_ was asleep.

She came through the cold hold's maintenance hatch, the way Maren and Danek and she had come the night of the seizure, outside along the hull in a runner's suit with a spare one over her arm; and she opened the hatch into the cargo bay without a word, and threw Maren the suit, and waited while she put it on. Then they went out together on to the hull, hand over hand along Odile's old running line, under the stars, under the long pale streamer of the Lamp's tail, which was so far out now that the head of it had shrunk to a point, and in along the collar fairing, and down through the forward suit lock into the dark of the _Patience_.

Ekene was waiting in the drum hall.

He was alone, with one lamp turned low on the deck by the drum's end, and his arm in its sling. The Plate was off its bolts. He'd taken it down himself, one-handed, and laid it on the deck face down on a folded blanket, its grey back up. Beside it, on the blanket, there was a rigger's scribe, a hardened steel awl for marking cable. And a pot of hull sealant, and a brush.

Maren stood and looked at it.

"Odile told me," said Ekene. "What you'd want. Before the boat."

"I didn't tell her."

"You didn't have to," said Odile, at the hatch, keeping watch. "You've been doing sums with your fingers for two days, Chief. On the gallery. Against the rail. Like you do on the board when you're working out a throw." She shrugged. "I watched you. I've watched you since I was sixteen. You were writing down where it went."

Maren knelt down by the Plate.

She'd watched the throw on the board. She'd watched the Gull's track and the Lamp's, and she'd sat at the tether-chief's station for two days with nothing to do but listen to static and look at the old projection the riggers had drawn for the throw, the long falling curve of the Lamp's orbit coming in past Gall and going out, with its numbers down the side. A tether-chief remembers numbers. It's most of the job. She had them all.

She picked up the scribe and began to cut.

It was slow. The back of the Plate was raw steel, rough and pitted, and the scribe was meant for marking cable, not cutting plate; she had to press with all her weight on every stroke and drag it, slowly, so that the point would bite and not skid. It skidded anyway. On the sevens. On the T. She did the numbers first, in a column down the left-hand side, six of them, with their old dippers' labels, the elements of the Lamp's orbit, the way Hesketh had taught her to write an orbit down when she was a runner of twenty and had asked how the old chiefs found things in the sky. At the bottom of the column, the time of the Lamp's closest pass. Her hand slipped on the last line, and the awl skated across the year and left a long bright gouge through it. She left it. Anybody who could read the rest could work it out.

Then, underneath, after a gap, she cut where.

_SOUTH LIMB_

_BENEATH THE BLACK RIDGE_

_FORTY METRES_

Then she sat back on her heels for a while, with the scribe in her hand, and looked at the rest of the bare grey steel.

"What else?" said Ekene.

"I don't know."

"Yes, you do."

She did. She bent over the Plate again and cut it, in the middle, in her shaking capitals, line by line, pressing until her wrist ached and her fingers bled inside her gloves.

_WE SENT IT ROUND_

_IT SLEEPS IN THE COLD_

_DO NOT WAKE IT_

_DO NOT USE IT_

_FORGIVE US_

And then, after a long time, smaller:

_FORGIVE ABEL_

And under that, her initials, and the year.

When she'd finished she put the scribe down on the blanket and sat with her hands in her lap. Her hands were shaking. Ekene put his good hand over them.

"Hale will see it," said Odile from the hatch. "When he puts it back up. Or someone will. And tell him. And he'll grind it off."

"Not if he can't see it," said Maren.

She picked up the pot of hull sealant. It was the thick grey paint the dock crews used on the outside of the ship, over seams, against the cold: a paint that went on like cream and dried as hard as the steel under it and lasted, the dock crews said, for ever. She opened it and dipped the brush.

"Paint it over," she said. "All of it. Edge to edge. Every line. So it looks like the back of a plate." She held the brush out to Ekene. "And let it keep. Under the paint. Till somebody needs it."

"When will that be?"

"When the Lamp comes round," said Maren. "Two hundred and sixty years. When somebody's standing in front of this Plate and the Lamp's in the sky and they need to know where it is and what it is and what we did." She looked at him. "Somebody will. Somebody always does. There's always one."

Ekene took the brush.

He painted it one-handed, slowly, carefully, the way he did everything, edge to edge, while Maren held the pot and Odile kept watch at the hatch. The grey went on thick and smooth over the bare steel and the scratched lines, the numbers and the words and the initials, and they disappeared under it one by one, like names under resin. When he'd finished, the back of the Plate was a plain grey rectangle, featureless, gleaming wet in the low light of the lamp. You would never have known.

They waited for it to dry. It took two hours. They sat together on the deck of the drum hall, the three of them, under the still drum and the chalked names of the dead, LUSINE ARDA 2391 and Teska's little drawing of the Lamp beside it, and didn't say much. At some point Ekene put his good arm round Maren and she leaned against him, against his stiff cotton coat that smelled of the infirmary's smoked tea, and closed her eyes.

"I'll come," he said into her hair. "To Halloran. I'm not bonded. I can pay my own passage on the _Clement_. I'll find out where they send you. Kessel. Wherever. They always need medics in the mines."

"Don't."

"I'm going to."

"They need a medic here," said Maren. "The Camp. The ship. There's two thousand households on this moon that are going to need somebody who can set a bone and grow tea in a tray, now there's nobody coming." She opened her eyes. "Stay. Please. That's the one thing I'll ask you. Stay, and look after them, and look after the Plate." She paused. "And when Teska tells the children I went out with the Lamp, don't tell them it isn't true."

He was silent for a long time.

"Why?" he said at last.

"Because it isn't a lie," said Maren. "Not really. Somebody went out with it. Somebody called Tsolo, in my coat, with my knife." She looked at the drum, at the names. "Let them have him. Let them have the Tether-Woman. He'd have liked that. He always wanted to be somebody people told stories about."

When the paint was dry they hung the Plate back on its bolts, face out, three hundred names in steel, and Odile took her back along the hull to the cargo bay before the change of shift, and nobody ever knew.

* * *

They came for her at the first bell of the morning.

Bram Kovač and two trusties, with the carbines held low and their eyes on the deck. They took her out of the cargo bay and through the collar and along the _Patience_'s forward corridors toward the boat dock, the long way, through the drum hall, because the short way was blocked by the dock crews loading the _Clement_'s last cargo for home. She didn't ask them why. She knew why. Hale would want them to see.

The drum hall was full. Not the way it had been full for the vote, or for the terms, packed and shouting. Full the way a room is full at a funeral: people standing along the walls, and on the gallery stair, and round the drum, quite quiet, in their work coveralls, the dippers and the riggers and the runners of the _Patience_. They didn't say anything when she came in. They didn't look at her, mostly. Some of them did. Teska did, from her bench, with her one good eye, and her face was wet. Odile did, from the gallery rail, very straight, with her hands at her sides. Ekene did, from the infirmary hatch, with his arm in its sling.

Hale was at the Plate.

He was standing at the end of the drum with his back to the hall, in front of the three hundred names, with a cold chisel in one hand and a mallet in the other. He was working at the top of the first column. At the first name. MAREN TSOLO, in shaky capitals, with the long scratch where the awl had skidded on the T. He was cutting it off. Not cleanly. He didn't know how to cut steel any better than she had; he was just scraping, gouging, with the chisel's edge, back and forth, so that the letters went ragged and then blurred and then were only a roughened, gouged, scarred patch of grey metal at the top of the column, where something had been written once and somebody had taken it off again.

He heard her come in. He didn't turn round until he'd finished. Then he did, with the chisel still in his hand, and looked at her across the drum hall with his mild patient face; and then he looked past her, at the crew, at all of them, standing along the walls.

"The Company's share," said Corwin Hale, quite gently, and tapped the gouged patch with the chisel's end. "Struck off. As agreed. The Purchase is complete."

Nobody said anything.

Nobody stopped him. She hadn't expected anybody to. She walked through the hall between Bram and the trusties, past the drum, past Hale, past the Plate with its three hundred names and its blank first line, and nobody stopped her either. At the gallery rail, as she passed under it, Odile Arda put her hand flat on the rail, palm down, the way runners put their hands on the drum before a dip, for luck. That was all. And then she was through the hall and out of the far hatch and along the corridor to the boat dock, and the _Clement_'s little cargo boat was waiting with its hatch open, and she got in.

* * *

The _Admonition_ was very clean.

That was what she noticed. After sixty years of the _Patience_, of grease and frost and old cable and the smell of three hundred bonded living in each other's breath, the cutter was clean and quiet and smelled of nothing, and its corridors were grey and narrow and lit very evenly, and the marines who walked her from the dock to the brig did it without speaking and without touching her more than they had to. Captain Lachance did not come to see her. She heard his voice, once, on a speaker in the corridor, pleasant and unhurried, giving an order she didn't understand.

The brig had a port. She hadn't expected that. A small round port the size of a dinner plate, very thick, set in the outer wall at head height, so that a prisoner could look out at what she'd left.

She stood at it as the _Admonition_ turned for the Throat.

Kettle went by first. Small and white and cracked, with the long dark smudge of the Camp's warrens along its equator, two thousand households under the ice, breathing. Then Gall, filling the port, enormous, banded ochre and purple, with Old Purple turning on its southern limb and its single dark eye looking up at her. And the _Patience_, very small against it, a long rust-coloured whale hanging over the giant on her orbit, with her drum still and her line reeled in, and somewhere inside her, on the end of the drum in the hall, a steel plate with three hundred names on it and a blank where the first one had been.

And beyond them all, very far out now, so far that she had to press her face to the cold thick glass and look for it, the Lamp.

It was going. It was nothing but a faint smear now, a thumbprint of pale light low in the black beyond Gall's limb, with the barest suggestion of a tail, like a breath on glass. The way it had been on the day Lusine went into the cloud. Going out. Going out past the cold, where the sun was only a star and the ice was so cold it rang, for two hundred and sixty years, carrying in its black south limb, forty-one metres down under a broken ridge, a grey egg asleep in its cradle and a man in her coat beside it with his knees drawn up, keeping it company.

_Teska says it brings them back._

Maren Tsolo stood at the port of the _Admonition_'s brig with her palm flat on the glass, the way you put your hand on the drum before a dip, and watched the Lamp until she couldn't tell any longer whether it was there or whether she was only remembering where it had been.

Then she sat down on the bunk, and folded her hands, and began, because she was the tether-chief and that was what tether-chiefs did, to count.


# Chapter 20: Sent Round Again

*The Siege · 2654*

We bring Mattias up out of the shaft between us, Dasha and I, on two lines, and get him into the _Wick_ and out of his suit, and he bleeds on the cabin floor for an hour while Dasha does what she can with the skiff's kit and her rigger's hands. The shot went in under his ribs on the left side and out again at the back. His patch held. It held well enough that he's alive, and not well enough that he'll be alive for very long without a medic, and the nearest medic is three days and a giant away.

"I'll fly," he says, when he can say anything. He's lying across the two back seats with his head in Dasha's lap and his face the colour of the frost on the cryostat. "Strand's gone. Somebody has to fly. I'm a lander pilot."

"You've got a hole in you."

"Landers don't care," says Mattias, and tries to laugh, and stops.

The cryostat is out on the ice, where I left it, at the foot of the black ridge, clipped to Dasha's surface line beside the anchor. I didn't bring it into the _Wick_. I couldn't. I stood on the ice with it in my hand after Corin went, after his voice went out of my helmet, and I looked at the _Wick_'s open hatch and the little cargo well behind the seats, the size of a coffin, and I couldn't put it in there. It's out on the Lamp in the shadow of the ridge with its new bright scar down its side and its lights dark and its window cracked, and the cold of the Lamp all round it. That's where it lives. That's where it sleeps.

I have the Plate on my knees.

I don't know why I'm doing it now. There's a man bleeding on the floor at my feet and another drifting out into the coma somewhere above us and my mother on the Rime and thirty-eight ships between me and home, and I'm sitting in my seat in the _Wick_ with the work-lamp clipped over my head and the founders' Plate face down on my knees, cleaning. Dab. Wait. Wipe. My hands won't stop shaking. I think it's the only thing I can do with them that isn't the other thing, the thing they did on the ice.

There's still paint on the middle of the back, round the line I cleared on the second, _it sleeps in the cold_. Above it and below it. I work outward from the line. I've stopped using Malachy's brush; it's too slow. I'm using the knife. Abel Tsolo's knife. Maren Tsolo's knife. The hook-bladed line knife that hung at the hip of a dead man for two hundred and sixty-three years and cut a living one off the Lamp an hour ago. I hold it by the hilt and use the back of the curved blade to lift the softened paint, and it comes away in long grey curls, like wood under a plane.

Above the line, two words.

_WE SENT IT ROUND_

Below it, four lines, one under another, in the same hard shaking capitals, the awl skidding on every T.

_DO NOT WAKE IT_

_DO NOT USE IT_

_FORGIVE US_

And then, after a gap, smaller, as if whoever cut it had stopped for a long while before they could:

_FORGIVE ABEL_

And under that, two letters and a year.

_M.T. 2391_

I sit with the knife in my hand and look at it.

"What does it say?" says Dasha, from the floor.

I read it to her. All of it. From the numbers at the top down to the initials. My voice is quite steady. When I get to _forgive Abel_ she makes a small sound, and Mattias, with his head in her lap and his eyes shut, says, very faintly, "Who's M.T.?"

"His sister," I say. "Maren Tsolo. The tether-chief."

"The one who didn't go."

"The one who threw him."

"She wrote that," says Dasha slowly. "After. She was here. On Kettle. After he went out. She knew where he was and what he'd done and she wrote it down on the back of the Plate." She looks up at me. "And then somebody painted it over."

"Somebody," I say.

I turn the Plate over.

I haven't looked at the front of it since the Archive. I've been looking at its back for six days, inch by inch, under the work-lamp, and I've almost forgotten it has a front. The three hundred names. Fifteen columns, three hundred hands, deep and neat and scratched and shaky. I look at them now in the green light of the Lamp coming in through the port, and I find what I knew I'd find. Halfway down the second column, neat and deep, like the hand on a warden's form: _CORWIN HALE_. Lower down, big and careless and sloping across a whole column's width, so that the next name has had to squeeze itself in underneath: _ABEL TSOLO_. A little drawing of a fish next to a name I can't read. _DANEK SORELL_. _LUSINE_, very small, beside _ODILE ARDA_.

And at the top of the first column, where every child in Patience has been told for two hundred and sixty-three years that the Company kept its share until the Purchase was done, a gouge. A rough, ragged, scraped patch of grey steel, where something was cut once and somebody took a chisel to it.

_It was her plan and her line_, Abel said, _and she was going to come herself_.

I don't know what happened to her. Nobody will ever know, now; it isn't in the Archive and it isn't in the recording and it isn't on the Plate. I know her brother took her coat and her place and went out on the Lamp, and that she threw him straight. I know she lived long enough to cut the Lamp's road and her brother's name into the back of the founders' Plate with an awl. I know that somebody painted it over. I know that somebody scraped her name off the front and called it the Company's, and that the man whose name is halfway down the second column, neat and deep, was a company man, and that his great-great-great-great-great-great-grandson pointed a gun at me an hour ago and said _Dad_ as he went out into the dark.

I know that's enough.

"What are you going to do?" says Dasha.

I put the knife down on the Plate. I look out of the port, at the black ridge, at the cryostat sitting in its shadow on the ice. At Gall, filling half the sky, one day from the Lamp's closest pass. At the long bright tail streaming out behind us into the black, toward where, very small and very far, I know Kettle is, white and cracked, with forty thousand people on its surface in the old domes of the Rime.

"I'm going to talk to them," I say.

* * *

The _Wick_ has a dipper's radio, which means it has a very big one. Dipper skiffs go down into Gall's cloud-tops where the giant's radio belts roar like a furnace and anything weaker than a shout gets swallowed; so the skiffs carry transmitters that could be heard, Corin told me on the first night out, from one end of the system to the other, if you aimed them at it. I don't know how to aim them. Mattias does. He tells me, lying across the back seats with his eyes shut and his voice going in and out like a signal, which switches and which dials, and Dasha sets them, and on the ninth of August, at the moment the Lamp makes its closest pass by Gall, at the bottom of its long fall from the dark, I sit in Corin Strand's pilot seat with Abel Tsolo's recorder in my lap and its jack in the board, and open every band the _Wick_ has got, and talk.

I don't know who hears it. I find out later. Everybody hears it. Every speaker in Patience, on every tier, the Council's own emergency channel that clicks like a knuckle on a table; every receiver on every one of the Concord's thirty-eight ships, from the _Steadfast_'s command deck to the lander bays to the cooks; the crawlers on the Rime, the domes, the dipper fleet sitting grounded in its docks. Malachy tells me afterward that he heard it in the Archive, alone, at his table, and sat there with his hands flat on the empty place where the Plate had lain, and listened to all of it, and couldn't move.

I start with the recording.

I don't play all of it. Forty minutes is too long; the Lamp doesn't wait. I play the beginning. _My name is Abel Tsolo. I'm a rigger on the skimmer_ Patience. _I'm wearing my sister's coat._ I play the part about the jeweller's file, and the part about the bonded taking the ship, and Ferrand with his hands up, and Danek Sorell in the cold hold. I play the part about Tamsin's Rock. I play the part where he says _Hale wanted to use it on the cutter, eighty people, and my sister stood up in the drum hall and said no_. I play the end. _Don't wake it. Whoever you are. It's not a gift. It was never a gift._

Then I switch it off, and lean forward to the microphone, and say my name.

"This is Isaura Venn. I'm an air-warden of Patience. Ninth South, then the Cells. Unshared." My voice is very level. I've been an air-warden for twelve years. I know how to say a number in a room. "I'm on the Lamp. I'm sitting on its south limb, in a dipper skiff, under a black ridge, two hundred metres from a tug that was put down here in twenty-three ninety-one by the man you just heard. He's forty metres under me in the ice. He's been there two hundred and sixty-three years."

I breathe.

"There's something else here. The thing he brought. It's sitting on the ice outside my port, in a steel cryostat, with a scar down its side where Corin Strand shot at it yesterday. It's a grey egg. It's got four lines stencilled on it and a green wheel. It's called a Sanitation Agent, Pattern H. The bonded called it the Hush. The Concord calls it Asset Seven." I look at it through the port, in the shadow of the ridge. "Commodore Grieve. This is what you came for."

The cabin is silent. Mattias has opened his eyes.

"The men and women on your ships should know what it is," I say. "So I'm going to tell them. Your briefing cards say _do not open_. They don't say why. It's because it kills people. Everyone in a ship or a station or a city. It goes through the air and they go to sleep and they don't wake up. It doesn't touch the machines. That's what sanitation means. The Combine used one on a place called Tamsin's Rock, on four thousand of its own workers, because they struck. They filled the place with new workers inside a year." I wait a moment. "Commodore, you have orders. A man on your own lander deck told me about them. If the city of Patience has Asset Seven, you're to take it back. And if the city won't surrender, you're to _sanitise the claim_. With this. On Kettle. A hundred and eighty thousand people. You'd ship in new ones from Corran."

On the open band, very faint, somebody says something. A voice, a man's, not Grieve's. A babble behind it. Then silence.

"I'm not going to give it to you," I tell the Commodore. "And I'm not going to bring it home."

I take a breath. I look at Dasha, at Mattias.

"First Shareholder," I say. "You'll be listening. Your son tried to take it home. He wanted to put it on the _Steadfast_'s hull and open it. Eleven thousand people. I stopped him. He's gone. He went out into the coma off the Lamp, an hour after we found it, because I cut his line with a dead man's knife. I'm sorry. I'm sorry for it. His last word was for you."

Nothing on the band. Nothing at all.

"And you should know where your family comes from, First Shareholder," I say, "because everybody else in the city knows now. Corwin Hale. Chief warden of the _Patience_. A company informer. He gave a boy a jeweller's file to cut a runner's harness clip, because she'd read the Combine's mail. He wanted to use this thing on eighty sleeping people to win a vote. His name is halfway down the second column of the Share Plate. I'm holding it. His is very neat." I look down at the Plate on the seat beside me. "And at the top of the first column, where you've taught every child in Patience that the Company kept its share, there's a scrape. Somebody took a chisel to a name there. And on the back, under the paint, in the same hand that wrote down where this thing is, there are two letters. M.T. Maren Tsolo. The tether-chief. Her brother went out with the Lamp in her coat, and she threw him, and then somebody took her name off the Plate and told us all we'd bought our air."

I put my hand flat on the Plate.

"We didn't buy anything," I say. "We took it. In the night, with blood. And then they had this thing, the founders. In their hands. They could've used it on the cutter and been free that night, and they nearly did, and a woman stood up in the drum hall and said no. They sent it round instead. On the Lamp. _Do not wake it. Do not use it. Forgive us._ That's what she wrote on the back of the Plate." My voice has gone at last; I let it. "That's the founding of Patience. That's what we come from. Not the Purchase. Her."

In the back of the cabin, Dasha is crying.

"There are forty thousand people on the Rime," I say. "On Council orders. _The few for the many. Cut the line to save the ship._ My mother's one of them. I gave her my coat and my key in South Lock. She's mending filters for the Ocampos." I hear myself say it and I can't stop. "Open the valves. Shared, unshared, anybody who can hear me. Go up to the Rime and bring them down. The siege is about this, and only this, and this isn't coming home. There's nothing left to cut the line for." I look at the egg on the ice. "Commodore, your asset is going back where it came from. Into the cold. You can't follow it. Nobody can. I'm going to put it forty metres down in the ice beside the man who brought it, and the Lamp's going to take it out past the cold for another two hundred and sixty years, and you can go home and tell the Concord it's gone. That's the truth. You can check. Look at the Lamp in a month."

I take my hand off the Plate.

"That's all," I say. "This is Isaura Venn. On the Lamp. Out."

I switch it off.

Nobody in the _Wick_ says anything for a long time.

* * *

Grieve answers four minutes later.

He sounds exactly the way he sounded on the speakers at Lampfast: dry, tired, educated, as if he's reading. He doesn't deny anything. He says that he has heard the transmission from the comet and that he notes its contents. He says that the Concord's orders regarding Asset Seven are not a matter he is able to discuss on an open band. He says that if the person speaking has the Asset in her possession, she is in possession of Concord property, and that she is required to surrender it, unopened, to the Concord's officers, who will come for it. He says that if she does not, he will be obliged to take such steps as are necessary.

"Come for it, then," says Mattias, from the back seats, very faintly, though Grieve can't hear him. "You'll never match it now. Not with the _Steadfast_. Not with anything. It's past the bottom. It's going out."

He's right. I find out later that the Commodore tried. That two of his frigates lit their drives and turned toward the Lamp, the _Vigilant_ and the _Resolute_, and burned hard for an hour, and fell back, the way Maren Tsolo said in the drum hall two hundred and sixty-three years ago that the _Admonition_ would.

What I don't know, sitting in the _Wick_, is what's happening on the _Steadfast_'s lander deck. I learn that later too. From Malachy, who heard it on the Concord's own fleet band, which some lander crew had left open in the confusion, so that half of Patience heard it as well. A man's voice, a junior officer, and then another, and then a lot of them, all at once, in the big bay where the lander crews eat, where a commander from the Commodore's staff once told them _you'll know it if you see it_. Questions. Shouting. Somebody asking whether it was true. Somebody saying _Tamsin's Rock, I know Tamsin's Rock, my grandmother was from the Penner lanes_. Somebody saying the Concord could go and sanitise itself. And then the _Steadfast_'s lander deck going off the fleet band altogether, and the _Constant_'s, and the _Fortitude_'s, and three of the Concord's thirty-eight ships not answering the Commodore's orders for the rest of the day.

"They're bonded," Mattias says, much later, when I tell him. "Most of them. Like me. Somebody's fishing boat." He closes his eyes. "Nobody tells them anything. It's always already happened."

* * *

The first slug hits the Lamp three hours later.

We're in the shaft. Dasha and I, with the cryostat between us on two lines, lowering it back down. It goes down easier than it came up; nothing weighs anything on the Lamp, and the shaft is clean now, and the blue light comes down through the ice to meet us. I've got the Plate strapped on my back in its blanket. I've got the knife at my hip, in its old hard sheath, on a loop I've cut from my own harness. Mattias is in the _Wick_, alone, at the controls, with the hole in his side and his hand on the stick, because somebody has to be.

The slug hits a kilometre off, on the far side of the ridge. I don't hear it. Nobody hears anything on the Lamp. I feel it. The whole shaft jumps, a hard sharp jolt like the Spine being kicked, and the blue walls ring, and ice comes down past us in a glittering sheet, and the cryostat swings on its lines and bangs the wall.

"He's shooting at it," says Dasha. Her voice is very calm. "Your commodore. He can't catch it, so he's going to break it."

"He can't break it. Not the egg. Not with slugs."

"No," says Dasha. "But he can break us."

"Isaura," says Mattias, in my helmet. Faint. "That was a slug. Off the _Steadfast_, from a long way out. They're walking them in. Ten minutes between. They'll be close in thirty." A breath. "You've got thirty."

"Thirty," I say.

We get the cryostat down.

We put it back where it was. Beside him. In the little round room cut in the blue ice with a pick, against the curve of the wall, where it sat for two hundred and sixty-three years. Its lights are still dark. The crack across its window has spread a little. Inside, through the crack, in the light of my helmet, the egg lies in its brittle foam, grey and smooth and indifferent, as if it's never been moved at all. Abel sits beside it in his frosted coat, with his knees up and his head on his arms, as if he never moved either.

The second slug hits. Closer. The room rings.

"Twenty," says Mattias.

I unstrap the Plate from my back and take it out of its blanket and kneel in the little room with it face up on my knees, three hundred names in steel, and the gouge at the top of the first column. I take the knife off my hip. It's sharp. It's the best knife I ever held. It's not meant for cutting steel; nothing that size is. But the Lamp's cold makes the old steel of the Plate brittle, and the edge is hard, and I'm an air-warden, I've spent twelve years turning frozen valves with my whole weight on them, and I put my weight on it.

I cut her back in.

It takes me twelve minutes. Dasha holds the lamp and counts the slugs. The letters are bad. Worse than hers; worse than the awl. They go across the gouge and through it, ragged, deep, the knife skidding on the scars Hale's chisel left, so that the name sits on top of its own erasure like a word written over a word. But you can read it. Anybody could read it.

_MAREN TSOLO_

"Ten," says Mattias.

I turn the Plate over. Under her lines on the back, under _FORGIVE ABEL_ and _M.T. 2391_, there's a hand's width of bare grey steel, where the paint's come off and nothing was ever written. I cut there too. Faster. Smaller. I don't think about the words. I've been thinking about them for six days without knowing it.

_SENT ROUND AGAIN 2654_

_WE DID NOT USE IT EITHER_

_ISAURA VENN AIR-WARDEN_

_FOR THE UNSHARED_

The third slug hits. It's very close. The room jumps and goes dark for a second, all the blue gone out of the walls, as if something's passed over the sun; and then it comes back, flickering, and Dasha has her hand on my shoulder.

"Isaura. Now."

I stand the Plate against him. Against his knees, where they're drawn up, the way you'd lean a picture against a wall, face out, so the names show. So whoever comes will see it first. Hers at the top, cut over the scar.

I put the knife back in its sheath on his hip. It took me a long time to decide about that, in the _Wick_, with Mattias bleeding at my feet. I don't know who'll need it. Somebody might. Somebody always does.

Last, I take my own recorder out of my suit, the little warden's log I've kept every round of every shift for twelve years, and that I talked into for an hour last night while Dasha slept, in the _Wick_, with the Plate on my knees. All of it. The Ocampos and the floor and the grandmother. The bottle at South Lock. Mattias at the grille. The Rime. My mother. Corin. The knife. All of it. I don't know who it's for. Whoever comes. I put it in his hand, his gloved hand, folded round his shin, beside his own.

"Forty metres again," I tell him. "Sorry. We tried."

"Isaura," says Dasha.

We go up the shaft as fast as I've ever gone up anything, hand over hand, with the blue walls ringing round us and the ice coming down. At the top the sky is full of fire. There's a new crater a hundred metres along the ridge, black and steaming, a great raw wound in the Lamp's south limb with a jet roaring out of the bottom of it straight up into the dark; and the old _Gull_ has been knocked over on to her side by the shock, her grapple arm torn half out of the ice, but she's there, still, at the foot of the ridge, where she's sat for two hundred and sixty-three years. The _Wick_ is lifting already, on her thrusters, rocking, her hatch open, a metre off the ground.

We go in through the hatch and Dasha slams it and Mattias puts the nose up.

* * *

We lift off the Lamp at the bottom of its pass, as the fourth slug comes down where we were.

I watch it hit. I'm strapped into Corin's seat beside Mattias, with my helmet still on, and I watch the place on the open ice where the _Wick_ was sitting a minute ago, two hundred metres from the ridge, jump and flash and vanish in a great slow fountain of black and white, ice and soot and fire going up together into the sky. The _Wick_ lurches. Something hits her, a lump of ice, a stone, something; I feel it go through the hull behind my seat like a fist, and the cabin fills for a moment with a high thin shriek of air going out, and then the patches Dasha has had ready for three days go on, and hold.

"Hit," says Mattias. His voice is very quiet. "Not bad. We're going."

We go. Up and away from the Lamp's south limb, out of its shadow into the sun, with the Lamp falling away behind us, its black body breathing fire from a dozen new wounds and its tails fanned across half the sky; and then Gall, swinging into the forward port, vast and banded and close, and Mattias puts the _Wick_'s nose down toward it, toward the sling.

"I'm going to take us round," he says. "Same way we came. The giant'll throw us home. Three days." He breathes. "You'll have to land her. At the other end. I'll show you the switches."

"You'll land her."

"I'll show you the switches," says Mattias.

He shows me on the way down into Gall's sky, with the cloud-tops rising under us and the shield beginning to buzz. He shows me with one hand, the other pressed to his side, where the blood has come through the patch and through the flight suit and is shining on his fingers. He tells me what each one is for and when, and makes me say it back to him, the way Malachy made me say my sums back when I was eight. He says it all very carefully. He doesn't hurry.

We go round the giant. I don't remember it. I remember the push in my chest, and the grey at the edges, and his voice in my helmet through all of it, saying the switches.

When we come up out of the sling on the other side, on our long curve home toward Kettle, I look back. The Lamp is behind us now, a great white blaze going out, beginning already to dwindle. And far beyond it, back toward the Throat, there's a flash.

It's very small. A spark of blue-white light at the edge of the dark, where the lane mouth hangs behind Gall; there and gone. Then another, smaller, beside it. Then nothing.

"What's that?" says Dasha.

Mattias has his eyes on the board. He doesn't need to look.

"The anchor," he says. "The Throat's anchor station. That was the _Steadfast_'s guns." He breathes out, slowly. "He's going. The Commodore. He's taking the fleet home. And he's shutting the door behind him."

"Why?"

"Because he thinks you've got it," says Mattias. "Or that you could get it. A city with Asset Seven in its back pocket, at the end of a lane, that just told his whole fleet what he was ordered to do with it. He can't take it back. So he can't let it out." He smiles, very faintly. "He's quarantined you. The lane'll go raw now, without its anchor. It'll close. Ten years. Twenty. Nobody'll come through it for a long time." He closes his eyes. "Nobody'll go out, either. You'll be on your own."

"We were always on our own," says Dasha.

"Yes," says Mattias. "That's what I thought."

He dies on the second day out, in the morning, in the pilot's seat, with his hand on the stick.

He's been quiet a long time. He's stopped talking about the switches; I know them now. I've been sitting beside him in Corin's seat watching his face go greyer and his breathing go slower, and Dasha's been behind us with her hand on his shoulder, and nobody's been saying anything. Gall's behind us. Kettle's ahead, white and small and growing.

"Warden," he says, near the end. His eyes are open, looking at the port.

"I'm here."

"What's my allowance?"

I look at the board. At the cabin air. At him.

"Three-twenty," I say.

"Good," says Mattias Ebb. "I won't conserve."

* * *

I land the _Wick_ on Kettle on the fourteenth of August, badly, on the surface ice a kilometre from South Lock, because I can't find the dock shaft and I'm too frightened to try. She comes down hard on her belly in the snow and slews round and stops, and Dasha and I sit in our seats with our hands shaking and laugh until we cry, and then just cry.

They come out for us on a crawler. Riggers, in orange, and wardens, in grey, and Malachy, in a surface suit three sizes too big for him with his old grey coat over the top of it, sitting on the crawler's bed with his slippered feet dangling. He's the first one through the _Wick_'s hatch. He doesn't say anything. He looks at me, and at Dasha, and at the pilot's seat, where Mattias is still sitting with his hands folded in his lap because we didn't have the heart to move him; and then he puts his arms round me, in his too-big suit, and holds on.

He tells me on the crawler, on the way in. All of it. He tells it the way he told me my history lessons on the Fourth when I was nine, in order, very clearly, leaving nothing out.

He tells me that everybody heard. He tells me that the nave filled, that afternoon, the ninth, with people who hadn't been inside it in weeks: shared and unshared together, standing on the benches and the old tether gallery and the drum's own scarred flank, under the Oculus, with the Lamp burning over them; that they stood there and listened to it all again, because somebody in the Council's radio room had recorded it and played it on every speaker in the city, every hour, until the First Shareholder's stewards stopped trying to stop them. He tells me that the forty heads of the Shares met in the old bridge that night and did not vote for anything, because the old bridge was full of people who weren't Shares, standing between the dead consoles, waiting to see what they would do; and that Lucan Strand stood at the helm with his hand on the rail and his face like paper and didn't say a word, not one, for the whole of the meeting, and at the end of it walked out of the bridge and down to the Strand house on the Third Tier and shut the door, and hasn't opened it since.

He tells me the crawlers went up to the Rime at dawn on the tenth. Every crawler in the city. Driven by dippers and wardens and refinery men and anybody who could drive one, with the South Lock doors standing open and nobody, Malachy says, nobody at all, standing in front of them saying _no movement between tiers_. He tells me they brought them down all day and all night, forty thousand people, out of the old domes and down the ramp and into the warm, and that the shared tiers opened their doors, some of them, a lot of them, more than he'd have believed, and took people in.

He tells me the Concord's ships went back through the Throat on the eleventh, all of them, the three that weren't answering and the thirty-five that were, and that on the twelfth the dipper fleet flew for the first time since June, eighty skiffs going up out of the dock shaft one after another into Gall's sky in a long glittering line, and came back on the thirteenth with their tanks full; and that the reserve board on Fourth Central, which somebody's put back up, said twenty-two this morning, and climbing.

Then he stops.

"Isaura," he says.

I know. I've known since he put his arms round me in the _Wick_. I've known, I think, since South Lock.

"The sixth," says Malachy. "Before you'd even reached the Lamp. In the Ocampos' dome, on the Rime. She'd been mending their filters. The Bekkers' and the Ocampos' and old Sofi's. Nine days without a stop. Dasha's people up there said she wouldn't sleep." His voice is very gentle. "The cough went into her chest, they said. Like the grandmother. She just got tired."

I don't say anything.

"She'd given your coat to the girl," says Malachy. "The Ocampo girl. The night before. The girl was cold. She put it round her and did the buttons up. They said the girl wouldn't take it off after. Not even in the crawler. She's still got it on." He looks at me. "And your key. She was still holding your key."

* * *

I go up to the Ninth South at the end of the next day, after the medics have finished with me, after Dasha's gone home to the Ninth North to sleep for a week, after I've stood for an hour in the Archive with Malachy looking at the empty place on his big table where the Plate used to lie under the lamps.

The Ninth's warm. That's the first thing. I've never in my life felt the Ninth warm. The fans are running full, every one, all along the corridor, so loud you have to raise your voice; and the doors are open, half of them, people coming and going between them, carrying things, talking. The frost on the ceiling's dripping. Somebody's got a pan of something on a heater in the corridor outside the sixth door, and the smell of it is all down the tier.

The eleventh door's open.

Pilar Ocampo is sitting in it, on the threshold, with her knees drawn up. She's wearing a grey wool coat that comes down past her boots and has its sleeves rolled up six times, a warden's coat, the best coat I ever owned. In her lap, in her cracked grey gloves, she's holding a flow gauge and a valve key on a ring.

She looks up at me with her coin-flat face, and doesn't say anything.

I sit down on the floor of the corridor beside her, with my back against the ice-brick wall, and for a long time we just sit there, the two of us, listening to the fans.

"Mama's at the plant," she says at last. "Papa's at the plant. Tomás and Juli are asleep." She looks down at the gauge in her lap. "She gave me this. Your mama. She said give it back to the warden when she comes." She holds it out. "She said you'd need it."

I take it. I turn it over in my hands, the old brass gauge, the key worn bright where my fingers have held it for twelve years. Then I get up, and go to the eleventh door's frame, to the meter under its frosted window, and wipe the window with my glove.

The needle's on the red. Nobody's had the time to turn it back. Two hundred litres an hour for five. The floor.

"Ocampo, Ninth South eleven," I say aloud, to nobody, to the corridor, to the whole warm loud dripping tier. There's no ledger on my wrist. I left it in the _Wick_. "Household of five. Tithe owing—"

I stop.

I fit the key. The valve's stiff. They're all stiff, up here. I put my shoulder into it, my whole weight, the way I've done ten thousand times, and it turns, and the needle comes up off the red, and up, and past the old floor and the older one and every line on the dial I've ever read aloud into a ledger; and up, to the stop. And the fan behind the eleventh door changes its note, the way they always do, from a sigh to a roar.

Pilar's watching me.

"What's that?" she says.

"That's the stop," I say. "That's as far as it goes."

"Is that allowed?"

"I don't know," I tell her, and go on to the twelfth.

I do them all. Every door on the Ninth South, all sixty-one, from the eleventh to the end and then back round to the first. Some of them are open and some of them aren't, and I don't knock, and I don't say _air-warden_, and I don't read anything aloud. I wipe each window with my glove, and fit the key, and put my weight on it, and turn every valve on the tier as far as it will go, until the whole long cold corridor under the surface ice is roaring with air like the inside of a giant's lung, and the frost on the ceiling is coming down in drops all along it like rain.

Pilar Ocampo follows me the whole way, in my coat, with her cracked grey gloves, watching. At every door, after I've turned it, she reaches up and wipes the window again, the way I do, and looks at the needle on the stop, and says, quite seriously, to the meter, to the door, to whoever's on the other side of it, the word I've been trying to say for twelve years and never had the right to.

"Breathe," she says.


# Chapter 21: The Long Round

*The Last Return · 2917*

There's a plate against his knees.

That's the first thing Til sees, after the coat. A rough grey rectangle of steel the size of a door panel, its edges wavy and black from an old torch, leaning against the drawn-up knees of the figure in the coat the way you'd lean a picture against a wall, face out, so that whoever came in through the arch would see it first. In the blue light of the ice it looks like a gravestone. It's covered in names.

Juno goes past her on her knees and kneels in front of it.

She doesn't touch it. She bends close, with her helmet lamp, and reads, the way she reads a column in the counting-house: top to bottom, left to right, her lips moving very slightly behind her visor. Fifteen columns. Names cut into steel by a lot of different hands: some deep and neat, some barely scratches, some only a mark, a cross or a star or a little drawing of a fish, with a name cut beside it by somebody else.

"Three hundred," says Juno, after a long while. "About. I lost count in the ninth column." She puts her lamp on the top of the first column. "Look at this one."

Til looks.
